# Chapter 1

[T]{.dropcap}he pheasant arrived at four o’clock on a Tuesday, and Dana Whitaker signed for it because that was what she did. She signed for things.

She was home by then because Tuesdays she worked seven to three, a schedule she’d negotiated eight years ago when Iris started kindergarten and had defended since like border territory. The delivery man had the box balanced on one knee and a scanner in his free hand, and he looked at the label and then at her with an expression she recognized, the look of a man who had carried something strange enough that he wanted, just once, to be asked about it.

“You want to know what it is,” Dana said.

“Ma’am, I’ve been wondering since Columbus.”

“It’s a taxidermied pheasant.”

He considered this. “On purpose?”

“For a school auction.” She signed the glass with the little plastic wand, her name coming out, a seismograph reading of a signature. “Donated. We take what we’re given.”

“My condolences,” he said, and left her with it, and she stood on the porch a moment in the raw February air, a box the size of a dorm fridge at her feet, the street doing its four o’clock things, the Hendricks’ dog stating its case to a squirrel, somebody’s recycling bin still out from Monday, tipped and rocking. It had been packed by someone with strong opinions about bubble wrap, and when she got the flaps open the pheasant stared up at her with the fixed, offended expression of a bird that had been dead for forty years and still couldn’t believe it. The donation form, taped to its base, listed the donor as the estate of somebody’s great-uncle and its value as “sentimental.”

She carried it inside and set it on the dining room table, next to the microwave, which lived there now because the kitchen had no counters. The kitchen had not had counters since November. It had plywood where counters would someday be, and a sink that worked if you were gentle with it, and a contractor named Gary who answered texts on a lunar schedule. Gary had been the family’s weather for more than a year before he touched a cabinet, a year of estimates and start dates that came and went, the saga’s first installment already in the Christmas card before last, and then in November he had finally arrived and taken the counters out. Fifteen weeks. In January it had been a project, in February a situation, and somewhere in the last two weeks it had crossed over into a condition, a thing the family had, like a surname. She had learned to make coffee on plywood. She had learned a person could make almost anything on plywood if she stopped waiting for the situation to improve, which was, if she let herself think about it, a lesson with more range than the kitchen, so she didn’t let herself think about it, mostly, and made the coffee.

“There’s a dead bird on the table,” Iris said from the stairs. She was twelve and had recently perfected a way of coming downstairs that sounded like furniture falling.

“It’s for the auction.”

“Who’s going to bid on a dead bird?”

“Someone with a den,” Dana said. “Or a grudge. Don’t touch it, it’s older than Grandma.”

“It looks like the pig,” Iris said. “From your story. The dissecting one.”

“It looks nothing like the pig. The pig was a pig.”

Iris came the rest of the way down and circled the pheasant at a respectful distance, phone out, already photographing it for an audience of seventh graders. She shot it from below, from the side, came in close on the glass eye. Her braid had come apart on one side, and Dana’s hands wanted, on their own, to fix it. She kept them on the box flaps. More than a year ago Iris had informed her, with the cold precision of a lawyer, that she could do her own hair, and the treaty that followed had terms Dana was still learning. The terms kept moving. The treaty was about the terms moving.

“Marcy’s mom is letting her go to the thing a week from Friday,” Iris said, not looking up from her phone.

“The thing at Delaney’s.”

“It’s not a thing, it’s just people.”

“People at Delaney’s house, whose parents I’ve never met, until eleven.”

“Ten thirty.” Iris said it fast, a negotiator’s reflex, and then caught herself being caught. The tips of her ears went pink. On Sunday she had said the gathering ended at nine thirty, and she had said it looking straight at Dana. What had sat wrong for two days was not the lie. It was the eye contact. Dana had lied to her own mother at twelve, everyone lied to their mother at twelve, it was practically developmental, but she had done it looking at her shoes, at the wallpaper, at anything, because at twelve the face still knew what the mouth was doing. Iris had held her eyes and delivered it clean, and Dana had spent two days deciding whether she was raising a liar or a person with excellent nerve, and whether, at twelve, there was a difference.

She folded the box flat, taking her time with it. “Ten thirty is interesting,” she said. “Because on Sunday it was nine thirty.”

“I said around nine thirty.”

“You said nine thirty like you’d seen it notarized.” She slid the flattened box against the wall, where it joined the others, a cardboard record of everything this house had signed for. “Here’s what we’re going to do. You’re going to text Marcy and get Delaney’s mom’s number, and I’m going to make one extremely boring phone call, and if the actual end time of this non-thing survives contact with an adult, you can go.”

“That’s humiliating.”

“It’s thirty seconds.”

“Nobody else’s mom calls.”

“Then I’ll be exotic.” Dana picked up the donation form and clipped it into the auction binder, which lived on the dining room table too, between the microwave and the fruit bowl, the whole surface a refugee camp for the life the kitchen used to hold. “One call, Iris. Or Friday is a documentary about the postal service and me.”

Iris made a sound that was not agreement and was not defiance either, a sound with a whole treaty negotiation folded inside it, and went to the kitchen doorway and stopped, at the edge of the plywood. “When is Gary coming back.”

“When his heart heals,” Dana said. “Nobody knows. He’s very fragile.”

That got the laugh, the real one, the one that still sounded seven years old, the one Dana would have paid retail for, and Iris took a string cheese from the fridge and thundered back up to her room, and Dana stood in her counterless kitchen and allowed herself, briefly, to feel that she had handled it. The lie addressed, the door left open, no yelling, terms offered. Somewhere there was a version of this afternoon where she’d gotten it wrong, and she’d hear about that version at some future Thanksgiving, delivered across a table by a grown Iris with a grown Iris’s aim. But not tonight.

She spread the binder open and worked at the plywood standing up, because the auction was in eight weeks and the auction did not care about counters.

She had a binder with eleven tabs. She had a spreadsheet whose rows knew things about the parents of Bishop Middle that those parents would not have said out loud in confession: who could be counted on for wine pull donations, who bid drunk, who bid divorced, which two families could not be seated near each other since the incident at the fall carnival that no one discussed and everyone knew. She had a gym reserved, a caterer at the half-confirmed stage where they answered emails but wouldn’t sign anything, a DJ who kept trying to upsell her on lighting packages with names like Starlight Experience, and now a pheasant. Today’s list: confirm the table count with the custodian, chase the caterer, draft the donation-request follow-ups, enter the great-uncle into the spreadsheet. It was, all of it, hers to hold, because in October she had raised her hand in a cafeteria that smelled like industrial ranch dressing, and this was the price of a raised hand in Westerville. She had known that going in. Some part of her, the part she fed least and relied on most, had raised the hand because of it. A held thing could not wobble. A woman holding a gym, a caterer, a DJ, a seating chart, and a dead bird was a woman whose hands were visibly, certifiably full, and there was a safety in that she had never once said out loud.

Tom got home at six, kissed her temple, looked at the pheasant for a long moment.

“Okay,” he said.

“Auction.”

“I figured it was auction or something’s wrong with the food chain.” He set his keys in the bowl and his lunch cooler on the plywood, and rolled his shoulder, a slow backward circle, and she registered it. Site day, then. Which meant the Henderson bid had gone to a walk-through, which meant he’d be at the laptop later, which meant the evening had a shape now, and she filed the shape away. Sixteen years and she could read his day off his body before he said a word about it, and she did not often stop to ask whether he could do the same to her, and if he could, what he’d been reading.

“Gary?” he said.

“Silent.”

“I could call him.”

“You could,” she said, “but then he’d know we’re desperate, and Gary feeds on desperation. We have to be cool. We’re playing dead. Like our friend here.”

Tom looked at the pheasant again. The pheasant looked back with its glass eye, keeping its own counsel. “This is why the kitchen has no counters,” he said. “Because we’re playing dead.”

“It’s a strategy.”

“It’s something,” he said, mild, and got a beer from the fridge, and asked about Iris’s Friday thing, and she gave him the summary version, treaty terms and all, the lie and the phone call and the pending verdict, and he listened with the beer resting against his chest and nodded in the places a person nods.

“You’re calling the mom,” he said.

“I’m calling the mom.”

“She’s going to be mortified.”

“She’s going to survive it. That’s the whole curriculum this year. Being mortified and surviving it.”

Sixteen years of marriage had worn certain conversations smooth, and this was one of them: she reported, he ratified. It worked. She would have said, if asked, that it worked, and she would have been telling the truth, and the truth would have had a plywood layer under it that she didn’t look at directly, the same way she made coffee every morning without looking at where the counters weren’t.

They ate at the dining room table, the three of them and the pheasant, chicken reheated in the exiled microwave, and Iris, who had decided at some point between string cheese and dinner to be delightful, did an impression of her social studies teacher discovering the smart board was unplugged. It was merciless. It had structure. Act one, Mr. Kalb’s confidence, the marker uncapped with a flourish. Act two, the first click that did nothing, the second click, the man’s whole worldview beginning to buckle. Act three, and here Iris stood up from the table to get the posture right, Mr. Kalb following the cord hand over hand along the wall like a sailor in a storm, all the way to the outlet, and finding it, and holding up the plug to the class in silence, like an exhibit, like evidence of a crime society itself had committed against him. Tom laughed until he had to put his fork down. Dana laughed and watched her daughter take the bow that was owed, flushed with the specific joy of a twelve-year-old who has the room, and thought, with no ceremony at all, that this was a good life. It was a thought she had often, and on purpose.

After dinner the house sorted itself into its evening stations. Iris showered and went horizontal with her phone. Tom took the laptop to the armchair, the Henderson numbers open, the game on low beside him more for company than attention. Dana took the couch and the seating chart, and for forty minutes the only sounds were the dishwasher running its little heart out against the silence where a kitchen used to be, the announcer’s murmur, keys. She moved the Brennans away from the Wallaces for the third time. There was no table in that gym far enough for the Brennans and the Wallaces, there was possibly no gym, and she was considering the nuclear option, splitting them across the buffet line’s natural border, when her phone buzzed at 8:40, face down on the auction binder.

She almost let it go. One buzz, short, a text.

Rachel Beal. Dana hadn’t heard from Rachel in over a year, and her thumb was already swiping before her brain assembled a guess, reunion or fundraiser or somebody’s separation announced with a wine glass emoji.

so so sad about nic. are you going saturday??

The gym, the chart, the announcer’s voice, all of it kept going. Dana read the text twice. There was a kind of stillness that started in the hands, she would notice later, before anywhere else. Her thumbs hovered over the glass and did not move, and the screen dimmed, and she tapped it awake, and the words were still in the same order.

Across the room, Tom said something about a foul. The dishwasher changed cycles with a click and a sigh.

Sad about Nic. Are you going Saturday.

There were only so many things it could mean and her mind ran the list with a speed that shamed her, hunting for the survivable ones. Sad about Nic could be divorce. Could be a diagnosis. Could be a fire, a flood, a gallery closing, Rachel grieved widely and at volume, Rachel had once texted the whole group in mourning over a canceled television show. Are you going Saturday. Going where Saturday. You did not go to a diagnosis. She sat very still on her own couch, in the blue light, holding the phone, and let the list run out, and at the end of the list there was only the one thing left standing, the thing that had been standing there from the first read, patient, certain, waiting for her to finish.

She typed, what happened? and deleted it, because you could not ask that. Asking that told Rachel what Rachel’s text assumed you already knew, and something in Dana, faster than thought, some old muscle that managed rooms and phone calls and other people’s assumptions, was already protecting her position, already not wanting to be the one who had to be told.

She typed, just seeing this, and deleted that too.

She typed nothing, for a long time, while the screen dimmed and dimmed.

“You okay?” Tom said, not looking over. Sixteen years. He could hear a silence change.

“PTA stuff,” Dana said. The words were out before she chose them, smooth as a signature. “Julie’s melting down about the caterer.”

“Tell Julie it’s a gym,” Tom said, and the crowd on the television rose about something, and he leaned toward the screen, and that was that, the moment sealed over, the lie already load-bearing.

She looked back down at her phone, at the little gray words that had rearranged the temperature of the room without moving a single object in it. Upstairs the shower fan ticked off on its timer. The seating chart sat open on her lap, all its solvable wars. Nic at forty-six. Nic in a lab coat two sizes too big, freshman year, holding a scalpel over a fetal pig and saying, I’m going to name him Gerald, you can’t stop me. Nic’s last text, whenever that had been. A farmers market. Something about a braid. She had not answered it, she knew that much without looking.

And began, very carefully, in the manner of a woman signing for a package she did not remember ordering, to type.

# Chapter 2

[T]{.dropcap}he obituary was four paragraphs and Dana read it standing at the plywood in the dark, at 5:50 in the morning, before anyone else was up, with the coffee maker gurgling beside her elbow like a co-conspirator.

She had found it by typing a name into her phone at two in the morning with her husband asleep beside her, the screen brightness thumbed all the way down as if the light were the crime. Nicola Adair Chicago. The funeral home’s page had loaded with a stock photograph of lilies, and she had read the first line, and put the phone face down on her chest, and lain there listening to Tom breathe, and picked it up again, and that was how the night had gone, in installments, until the ceiling turned gray. Now it was morning and she read it properly, start to finish, standing up, dressed for work, a woman with a lunch to pack.

Nicola June Adair, 46, of Chicago, died suddenly at home on February 17th. Beloved daughter of the late Robert and Carol Adair. Cherished sister of Justine Adair-Ross (Daniel) of Evanston. Adored aunt of Ella and Sam. A photographer of rare warmth and wit, Nic saw people the way they hoped to be seen.

That was the sentence that got through. Not beloved, not cherished, those were the wooden pews of obituary language, everyone sat in them. Saw people the way they hoped to be seen. Somebody had fought for that sentence. Somebody had sat at a kitchen table with a funeral home’s word limit and fought for it, and it was true, it was the truest thing you could say about Nic in under ten words, and Dana had had no idea, none, that she was in a world where it needed saying in the past tense.

Visitation Thursday, March 5th, 4 to 7 p.m., at a funeral home on Ridge. A celebration of Nic’s life to be held in early April, details to follow. In lieu of flowers, the family asks you to shoot one roll of film of an ordinary day, and to actually get it developed.

Dana read the last line three times. That was Nic’s own voice, embedded in the obituary like a hair in amber, the actually especially, the little elbow in the ribs of it. Somebody had known her well enough to write that. The thought had edges on it and Dana set it down, the way you set down a knife when you notice how you’re holding it.

Saturday, Rachel had said. The visitation was Thursday. Rachel had the wrong day, which meant Rachel was forwarding grief, warm and fast and about eighty percent accurate, and for one bright ugly second Dana let herself hope the whole thing traveled at that accuracy. That there’d been an error. Some other Nic, some other Adair, the world was full of people, surely it had two of her.

The obituary had her photograph in it. It was recent. Her hair was mostly gray now, cut short, and she was laughing at whoever held the camera, mid-word, caught in the act of being Nic, and there was no other Nic and there never had been.

The coffee maker finished with its three-beep flourish, absurdly festive. Upstairs the pipes knocked, Tom’s shower. Dana poured her travel cup, wiped the plywood though nothing was on it, and went to work.

Work held her. The practice ran on her, and Wednesdays were hygiene-heavy, the waiting room turning over every forty minutes, and she sat at the front desk in the eye of it and was excellent. She rebooked the Kowalski kids around a band trip. She talked an insurance company through its own policy, patiently, twice. She intercepted Dr. Fanning before he double-booked himself for the third time this month, and when Mrs. Ostrander came out of the back teary because it was her first appointment since her husband passed, and the hygienist hovered helpless, Dana came around the desk and took the woman’s coat off the rack and held it open for her and talked to her about the parking situation on Schrock Road, the roadwork, the detour, sixty seconds of gorgeous nothing, and Mrs. Ostrander got her arms into her sleeves and her feet under her and left upright. It was a thing Dana knew how to do, had always known: you did not hand a drowning person more water. You handed them a coat and a detour. She watched the woman cross the lot to her car and stood at the window a moment with her hand still on the coat rack, and did not examine why.

She called Rachel at lunch, from her car, in the parking lot behind the practice, with a turkey sandwich unwrapped and untouched on the passenger seat. Rachel picked up on the second ring, already talking.

“I know, I can’t, I literally can’t, I keep picking up my phone to text her about it, isn’t that insane? Like, who do I text about Nic dying? Nic. She’s the one I’d text.”

“Rachel, what happened?”

“You didn’t hear?” A rustle, Rachel moving somewhere quieter, a door closing on office noise. “Okay, so, it was her heart, but not like, not a heart attack heart attack. It has a name, hang on, Justine told Amy and Amy told me. Something with dissection in it. Where the artery, like, the wall of the artery just, it just tears. Out of nowhere. It’s mostly women, apparently, women our age, women who are fine. She was fine, Dana. She’d just done a wedding two days before. There was nothing, no warning, nothing anyone could have caught. That’s what the doctors kept telling Justine, apparently, like it was supposed to help. Nothing anyone could have caught.”

The parking lot was the color of late February. Salt-white asphalt, a crust of old snow along the fence, a plastic bag doing slow circles by the dumpster, resting between attempts.

“A neighbor found her,” Rachel was saying, quieter now. “She missed a shoot Wednesday morning and didn’t answer, and she never missed shoots, so the studio called around, and the neighbor had a key for the plants. She was just, she was at home. They think it was fast. They keep saying that too. It was fast.”

Dana looked at the sandwich. She had made it at six forty this morning with the obituary still open on the plywood beside the bread, turkey, mustard, the pickle sliced thin, hands doing their eleven thousandth sandwich while the rest of her stood in a funeral home’s webpage, and it occurred to her now, distantly, that this was going to be the texture of it. That the news was not going to arrive once, cleanly, like weather. It was going to arrive like this, in a parking lot, next to a sandwich, in installments, forever.

“Thursday,” she said. “The visitation. You said Saturday.”

“Did I? God. Thursday. See, this is why nobody should hear anything from me.” Rachel exhaled, a long shaky pour of a breath. “Are you going? You have to go. God, Dana. You two. I still think of you as a unit, you know that? Dana-and-Nic. All of sophomore year I don’t think I saw one of you without the other one attached. You had that whole language. The eyebrow thing. You’d do the eyebrow thing across a whole party and then both just leave, like, simultaneously, and nobody ever knew how you decided.”

“I’m going,” Dana said, and heard herself say it, and it was true the way a signature is true. Decided in the hand before the mind convenes.

Rachel talked for eleven more minutes. Dana held the phone and watched the plastic bag and put sounds in the pauses. Rachel had a version of the last few years, secondhand, patched together from Christmas cards and Instagram: Nic thriving, Nic with a print in some juried show at a gallery whose name Rachel mangled twice, differently, Nic and somebody named Paul for a couple of years and then no more Paul, nobody knew the story there, Nic teaching a workshop for teenagers at a community center that Rachel was pretty sure was on the North Side, or possibly Evanston, or possibly that was a different thing. Rachel told it all in the tone people use when they’re building a case that everything had been good, that the story had been mid-sentence, because the alternative tone was unbearable on a Wednesday lunch hour. Dana agreed in the right places. She had her own patchwork and it was thinner than Rachel’s, and she noticed that, noticed it the way you notice a step missing in the dark, the drop arriving before the understanding. Rachel, who had been a satellite of the friendship, a warm outer moon, was briefing her, Dana, the famous other half of the unit, on the last six years of Nic’s life. And Dana was letting her. Was grateful, even, was taking notes with some appalling secretarial lobe of her brain, Paul, gallery, workshop, teenagers, filing it all under a heading she refused to read the name of.

When had she last actually spoken to Nic. Spoken, voice, phone against her ear.

Her birthday. Which birthday. The one in the snowstorm, no. The one where Iris had the ear infection, where Dana had taken the call pacing the upstairs hall with a bottle of children’s Tylenol in her free hand, half listening, saying uh-huh into the phone while reading the dosage chart, and Nic had said something that had made her laugh anyway, even then, even at half attention, and had said at the end, in the doorway voice, the leaving voice, we should actually talk sometime, like real talk, and Dana had said absolutely, soon, with the Tylenol in her hand. Iris’s ear infection year. Iris in first grade. Six years. The math sat in the car with her, on the passenger seat next to the sandwich, and would not leave.

“Anyway, the family said no flowers, did you see? You’re supposed to shoot a roll of film. That is so her I could scream,” Rachel said, and her voice went thick, and for a moment neither of them spoke, two women in two parking lots twenty miles apart, holding phones.

“I have to get back,” Dana said gently. “Rachel. Thank you for texting me. I mean it. I’d rather have heard it from you than from the internet.”

“Of course. God. Of course.” A pause, and then, with Rachel’s whole heart in it, tactless and true: “She loved you so much, you know. Like, of everybody. You know that, right?”

The bag lifted off the asphalt, cleared the fence in one try, and was gone.

“I know,” Dana said.

She went back in and worked the afternoon and was excellent, and drove home the ordinary way, and stood for a moment at the bottom of her own stairs before she went up to change.

The stairwell wall was photographs, it had always been photographs, she’d hung them herself in the first year in this house and added to them since, school pictures, the trip to Michigan, Tom’s parents’ fiftieth. And at the turn of the stairs, where the light from the landing window fell in the afternoon, the wedding pictures. The ones by the water. Sixteen years she had climbed past them, twice, ten times a day, carrying laundry, carrying a sleeping Iris, carrying her phone and her keys and the mail, and they had gone the way of all permanent things, into the wallpaper of her seeing. Now she stood on the third stair and looked. Tom laughing with his head thrown all the way back, a thing he did maybe twice a year, caught forever. Her own mother in the parking lot, hooting, actually hooting, at something outside the frame. Dana herself looking off camera at someone, mid-word, lit up. She knew who she’d been looking at. The photographer had been the someone. In half of them, if you knew how to read them, the subject was really Nic, Nic reflected, Nic implied, the laugh traveling toward the lens like light from a source just out of view.

Sixteen years on the wall of her own stairs. She climbed past them and went to change.

She told Tom that night.

She told him at the plywood, while he sliced peppers for fajitas on the cutting board balanced across the sink, because the plywood was for setting things on, not for knives, they had rules now, a whole provisional government. She waited until his hands were busy. She had not planned to wait until his hands were busy and she noticed that she had.

“A friend of mine died,” she said. “From college. Nic. Nicola. I found out last night, that was the text.”

Tom put the knife down and turned all the way around, and the fact that he turned all the way around made it briefly hard to talk. “Dana. Last night?”

“I didn’t want to say it and then just go to bed.”

“What happened? How old?”

“My age. Her heart. It’s a rare thing, a tear, it mostly happens to women our age who are otherwise fine. There was no warning.” She heard herself distributing the facts calmly, in order, a woman handing out programs at the door of the thing instead of being inside it. “The visitation’s next Thursday, in Chicago. I’m going to drive up.”

“Of course. I’ll move that Thursday, we can leave after Iris gets on the bus.”

“You don’t have to come.”

It landed a half-degree wrong, she heard it land, and Tom’s face did the thing where it didn’t change. “Okay,” he said. Careful. “Do you not want me to come?”

“It’s not that. It’s a long drive, and you’ve got the Henderson bid, and honestly I’ll be, I’ll drive up, I’ll pay respects, I’ll drive back. You never even met her.” That last sentence came out shaped like a fact and functioned like a door closing, and she watched it close, and did not stop it.

“Six hours in a car each way is a lot alone,” Tom said.

“I’ll take the good podcasts.”

He looked at her a moment longer. Sixteen years. He knew the difference between a wife who wanted space and a wife who wanted to be argued with, and she watched him try to read which one she was, and she kept her face pleasant and organized, and understood, in a small cold way, that she was making herself illegible on purpose and that he would let her. That was the deal underneath the deal. He turned back to the peppers.

“College friend,” he said. “Nic. Was she at the wedding?”

“She was,” Dana said. “She took the pictures everyone likes. The ones by the water.”

“Wait.” The knife stopped. “The photographer? The one who got your mom laughing in the parking lot? That’s, she’s the one who died?”

“That’s her.”

“Dana, those pictures are on our stairs. I look at her work every day.” He put the knife down. “I knew the face. I’d have known her in a room. I never had a name on it, that’s the thing, sixteen years and I never once had a name on it.” He turned around again, and there was something new in his face now, an adjustment, a man discovering the map of the last two decades had a landmark he’d mislabeled. “You never talk about her.”

“We lost touch,” Dana said. “You know how it is.”

And Tom, who was a kind man, and whose great marital gift and great marital flaw were the same thing, nodded, and let it be how it is, and the peppers went into the pan with a sound like applause.

At dinner Iris wanted the details, structurally, like an engineer. Where in Chicago. How far was that. Was Mom flying or driving. Why driving. Could you see the lake from the highway. Was the lake really that big, big like the ocean, or big like Buckeye Lake but more so. Tom fielded the geography. Dana ate her fajita and listened to her family map the route to a funeral like a road trip, and did not stop them, because their version of Thursday, weather and mileage and the lake like an ocean, was so much easier to sit inside than her own, and because Iris asked, at the end, spinning her fork, “Were you guys friends like me and Marcy?” and Dana said, “Yes,” and it was the truest thing she’d said all day, and nobody at the table but her knew to flinch at it.

She did the dishes at the gentle sink. Tom took the laptop to the armchair. And at nine, alone a moment in the counterless kitchen, Dana stood with the dish towel in her hands and looked at her phone where it lay dark on the plywood, and did not open the old thread, the one with Nic’s name on it, sitting down there in the list under the K’s and the M’s, under months of more recent business, the school, the caterer, Priya, Gary. She knew what was in it. She knew the last two messages were not hers. Thursday first, she told herself, in the voice she used for triage, one thing at a time, and hung the towel on the oven door, straightened it, straightened it again, and went up to bed past the water pictures in the dark.

# Chapter 3

[J]{.dropcap}ustine Adair-Ross called on Saturday morning while Dana was at the gym reserving folding tables, standing in the equipment cage with a clipboard, counting.

The school on a Saturday had the acoustics of a shipwreck. Her footsteps followed her down the main hall past the trophy case and the anti-vaping posters, and the custodian, Mr. Reyes, had let her in with the ring of keys he wore like a mayor, and walked her to the gym talking about the floor. The floor had been refinished over Christmas and Mr. Reyes had opinions about what an auction did to a refinished floor, specifically what four hundred parents in street shoes did, more specifically what heels did, and Dana walked beside him making the sounds of a woman who took the floor seriously, which she did, the floor was line eleven of tab four, felt pads and a shoe policy she had already drafted and would deploy at the right moment, because you did not spend your shoe policy early with a man like Mr. Reyes, you let him arrive at the shoe policy himself and then agreed with him.

“Twenty-two tables you’ll want,” he said, unlocking the cage. “People say twenty-four. Twenty-four and nobody can walk.”

“Twenty-two,” Dana agreed, and he left her to count them, satisfied, a man whose gym was in competent hands.

The cage smelled of rubber and varnish and the cold metal of the folded tables on their rolling racks. She counted with the clipboard against her hip, touching each table’s edge, and had reached the last rack when the phone rang. Unknown number, Chicago area code. Dana stepped out of the cage into the empty gym to take it, and the room did what empty gyms do to a voice, gave it a soul it hadn’t asked for.

“Is this Dana? Dana Whitaker? This is Justine. Nic’s sister.”

“Justine. Yes. Hi. I’m so sorry. I’m so terribly sorry.”

“Thank you.” Justine said it the way you say it on day eleven, worn smooth, a coin that had been through many hands. “I’m sorry to call out of nowhere, I got your number from Nic’s phone. That’s, actually that’s sort of the reason I’m calling. Do you have a minute?”

“Of course.”

“So. There’s the visitation Thursday, which, I hope you got the details, and then we’re doing the real thing, the memorial, in April. A celebration of life, they keep calling it, which makes it sound like a graduation.” A short sound, almost a laugh, sanded down. “We’ve got a space on hold, the gallery on Clark where she showed her work, they offered, everyone keeps offering things, it turns out, which is its own full-time job, accepting things graciously. Anyway. There’s going to be a slideshow, some people speaking. And I’m calling because I’d like you to be one of them. To say a few words about Nic. If you’re willing.”

Sound traveled strangely in the gym. The furnace came on somewhere above the ceiling, a big body turning over in its sleep. Banners hung in the rafters, conference champions, years and years of them, the oldest gone the no-color of old gold.

“Me,” Dana said.

“I know it’s a lot to ask. It’s just, you were her best friend. And I don’t want it to only be photography people up there, work people, everyone with a lens anecdote. I want somebody who knew her before. Who knew, you know. Nic-Nic. College Nic. The whole, the whole person.” Justine’s voice did something at the edges and got put back, a tablecloth twitched straight. “You’re in her favorites, did you know that? In her phone. I’ve been living in her phone all week, it’s horrible, it’s like reading someone’s diary except it’s also the utilities and the dentist. Five people in the favorites. Me, Ella, Sam, the studio, and you. Dana W. She never took you out.”

The clipboard was still in Dana’s hand. Twenty-two tables, she had counted, before the phone rang. The number stood in her mind with perfect clarity, twenty-two, the last fact from the old world.

Say it now, said some voice in her, low and urgent, from the sub-basement. Say it right now, this exact second, it’s the only second it will ever be sayable in. Justine, we lost touch. Justine, I hadn’t spoken to her in six years. Justine, I don’t know who Paul is, I don’t know when her hair went gray, I found out she died from a text with the wrong day in it. You’re looking for the keeper of college Nic and I let the position lapse, there’s someone better, there has to be someone better, ask Bev, ask anyone whose knowledge doesn’t have a fence line running through it. The words were all there, assembled, fueled, sitting on the pad. It would cost one terrible minute. It would be the single honest minute of this entire month, and Justine, day eleven, would even be kind about it, which would be the unbearable part, and after the kindness there would be the recalibration, the quiet demotion, the famous Dana filed under people who used to know her, and the favorites list would stand corrected at four.

“I’d be honored,” Dana said.

Her voice came out in the register she used at the front desk of the practice, warm and capable, the voice that said your insurance had gone through, the voice that made things be okay by declaring them handled. She heard it deploy all on its own, a reflex arc that never consulted her, hand off hot stove, yes off raised question, and Justine exhaled on the other end, relief moving through the line like weather.

“Oh, good. Good. Nic would,” and there the sentence went down and did not come back up for a second, “she talked about you all the time, you know. All the time. Dana would love this. Dana would lose her mind at this guy’s tie. Present tense, always, like you two had just gotten off the phone.”

The furnace filled the silence Dana couldn’t. Up in the rafters the oldest banner moved a little in the heat coming on, breathed, went still.

“It doesn’t have to be long,” Justine said, recovering, brisk again, a woman crossing an item off a list because the list was load-bearing. “Five minutes, seven. Whatever feels right. There’ll be me, and Bev from the darkroom co-op, and one of the brides is reading something, apparently, which, we’ll see. And you. You knew her better than anybody, you’ll know what to say.”

“I’ll figure out something worthy of her,” Dana said.

“I know you will.” Papers shuffled, Chicago-side. “Okay. I have to call the caterer, there’s a whole thing with the caterer, apparently celebrating a life requires a deposit. And a slideshow. Have you ever built a slideshow of your dead sister? Don’t. It’s four thousand photos and a software called, I’m not joking, Memory Weaver.” The laugh that came down the line this time was real, awful, alive. “I’ll text you the April date when the venue confirms. And Dana. Thank you. You have no idea, honestly. Every other part of this I can do with a checkbook. This was the call I was nervous about.”

“You never have to be nervous about calling me,” Dana said, which was, of everything she had said in four minutes, the only sentence with nothing wrong in it, and it was addressed to the wrong sister, twenty years too late.

She stood in the gym a while after the call ended, under the banners, in the furnace hum. Then she went back into the cage and counted the tables again, because the number had left her, and this time it came out twenty-three, and she counted a third time, touching each table as she went, out loud, alone in all that varnished silence, a forty-five-year-old woman saying numbers to folding tables. Twenty-two. It had been right the first time. She wrote it on the clipboard, 22, and underlined it, and stood looking at the underline, and thought about a woman in Evanston at a kitchen table with four thousand photographs, and put the clipboard in her bag.

She drove home the long way. She had agreed, somewhere in the equipment cage, to stand up in front of a room of grieving strangers in five weeks and present a friendship that had a six-year hole in the floor of it, and the wrongness of that sat in her chest all the way down Cleveland Avenue, at every light, not guilt exactly, or not yet, something more structural. A load miscalculated. She was good at loads. It was what she did, she looked at a gym and knew the tables, looked at a seating chart and knew the fault lines, looked at a family’s March and knew where to put the dentist appointments so nothing touched anything it shouldn’t. And she looked at herself at the third red light, hands at ten and two, a woman with a clipboard in her bag and a eulogy on order, and knew, with professional certainty, that the weight she’d just accepted was rated for a different woman. The one in the phone. Dana W. Never taken out.

And underneath that, lower, in the sub-basement, something else, and at the fourth light she made herself look at it, because she had a rule about the sub-basement, which was that you could keep things down there but you had to know what they were. Because Justine’s voice had done a thing to her when it said you were her best friend. It had felt, for one appalling half-second, before grief or guilt or anything else with a respectable name arrived, like being handed a title back. Like hearing her name read off a list she’d assumed she’d been cut from. Six years of silence, six years of Christmas-card broadcast, two texts sitting unanswered in a dead woman’s phone, and the verdict had come back anyway, from the phone itself, from the primary source: favorites. Five names. Hers. She had been claimed. Whatever the coming weeks were going to cost, and driving up Cooper Road in the gray late-February light she had no number for it yet, the down payment had been that half-second, and she had paid it, and it had felt, God help her, sweet.

The light changed. She drove home.

At home, Iris was at the dining room table doing homework next to the pheasant, having reached a separate peace with it. There was a sticky note on its wooden base now, in Iris’s round purple handwriting, and Dana stopped in the doorway when she read it.

“Gerald,” she said.

“He looks like a Gerald.”

He did. He looked exactly like a Gerald, it was the pheasant’s one true name, and Nic had named the fetal pig Gerald in September of freshman year, holding the scalpel like a conductor, this animal deserves dignity, Dana, and dignity begins with a name, and Iris had heard that story, had heard it twice, but never the name. Never once the name. The name was the part Dana could not say out loud in her own kitchen without something happening to her face, and so she had spent twelve years not saying it, and there was no chain of causation on this earth, no mechanism, no telling, nothing anyone could have arranged. Iris had simply pulled it out of the same air. For a moment the dining room had a fourth person in it, leaning against the doorframe, delighted.

“What?” Iris said, looking up. “Why is your face like that?”

“Nothing. It’s a good name.” Dana set her keys in the bowl. “It’s the correct name, actually. Did you get Delaney’s mom’s number?”

Iris slid her phone across the table, contact already open, martyrdom in every pixel, and then stood at Dana’s shoulder radiating protest while Dana made the call, close enough to intercept any treason. And the call was thirty seconds, as promised, and then it was ten minutes, because the thing on Friday ended at ten, not ten thirty and not nine thirty, splitting the difference, and Delaney’s mother turned out to be a hygienist named Carla who knew the practice, and they established this with the delight of two people finding a mutual border, and then established that they had a dentist in common, not Fanning, the other one, the one at the group out on Kestrel, and what Carla had to say about that man’s scheduling could not be said in front of the children, so it was said in code, at length, with enormous satisfaction on both sides. Iris lay down across two dining room chairs in theatrical death throes. Gerald presided, glass-eyed, patient. By the end of it Friday was settled, drop-off and pickup and the number exchanged, and something else was pointlessly, pleasantly begun, the way it still could be, Dana thought, hanging up. That easily. A phone call, a shared complaint, a laugh. Adult friendship was not a locked building. The doors worked fine. That had never once been the problem, and she put the phone down on the auction binder and did not follow the thought any further into the house of it.

“Ten,” Iris said from the chairs, eyes closed, funerary. “Everyone will still be there at ten. I’ll be the only one leaving.”

“You’ll be a legend of mystery.”

“I’ll be a baby.”

“Mysterious babies,” Dana said, “are the rarest kind,” and Iris fought the laugh and lost, and dinner needed starting, and the plywood received its cutting board, and Saturday closed over the house, warm and ordinary and requiring things of her every few minutes, a kind of wealth she had not understood a week ago. You could stand in the middle of an ordinary evening like a person standing in her own vault. Somewhere north, on a kitchen table in Evanston, four thousand photographs. Here, a child draped over two chairs describing the social apocalypse of a ten o’clock pickup, a husband’s key in the door, a dead bird wearing a name tag, everything, everything still answerable.

# Chapter 4

[T]{.dropcap}he driveway was salted though it hadn’t snowed since Thursday and wasn’t going to again, salted edge to edge with none of it wasted on the grass, a driveway that had been read the terms and had agreed to them. Dana pulled in behind her mother’s Buick, which was backed into the garage nose-out the way it had been backed into every garage of Dana’s whole life, a car kept permanently one decision from leaving. She sat a moment with the engine ticking as it cooled. Fifty minutes up from Westerville, and she had spent forty of them building the sentence and the last ten deciding not to decide, and now she was out of driveway and still didn’t have it.

Lorraine had the storm door open before Dana reached the step.

She always did. She sat, Dana knew, in the front room with a sightline to the road, not waiting, Lorraine would have gone to her grave before she’d have called it waiting, only positioned, so that no arrival ever caught her unready and no leaving ever caught her unfed. Seventy-two and straight as a shut door, hair set on a Sunday for a daughter, and the sight of the set hair did the old thing to Dana that it always did, the hair done for company that was only her, the love and the armor gone indistinguishable, the one wearing the other’s clothes because it owned none of its own.

“You didn’t say you were coming until you were already coming,” Lorraine said, which was hello. “I’d have thawed something.”

“I can’t stay for dinner, Mom.”

“I know you can’t. You never can.” No blade in it, or the blade so old it had worn smooth. She stood back to let Dana in, and the house closed around her, warm and ordered and faintly of the same cleaner it had smelled of since 1985, a smell that undid four decades in a single breath and set her down at nine years old on the bottom stair with her shoes already tied.

The kitchen was the kitchen. It had been the kitchen through every fashion the century had tried on it, avocado once, then oak, then the white it had settled into and would now outlast them all, and every surface held nothing, because nothing was allowed to hold on a surface, surfaces were for the brief transit of things on their way to their assigned places. There was a list on the refrigerator under a magnet shaped like Ohio, in Lorraine’s upright hand, and Dana didn’t have to read it to know its genus, defrost, call about the gutters, Kim’s birthday is the 8th get card, a life kept current in ballpoint. On the inside of the freezer door, when Lorraine opened it to survey what she might override the no-dinner ruling with, there was another list, taped and laminated, an inventory of the freezer’s entire population by date and portion, chili (2), the good soup (3), a thing labeled simply MEAT that Dana chose not to investigate.

“I really can’t, Mom.”

“I’m not doing dinner, I’m doing a plate for the road, those are different things and you know they’re different.” She was already at it, a container produced from the drawer where two hundred containers lived nested by size like a Russian doll’s whole extended family, and Dana let her, because you did not get between Lorraine and a plate for the road, that was a war you could not win and should not want to, and because the letting was one of the few dialects of affection they still had fluent between them.

Dana sat at the table. The chairs had been reupholstered twice in her memory and were on their third life in a small blue check, and she ran her thumb along the welting the way she’d run it as a child, the same thumb, the same welting, some seam in her that never aged doing it. On the wall by the phone, the real phone, the one on the wall with the cord Lorraine kept because you couldn’t lose a phone that was screwed to the house, there were the photographs.

They started with Dana, which you would never notice unless you’d spent a life not-noticing it and then, on one gray Sunday with a dead friend in your chest, noticed. The wall began with Dana at maybe six, gap-toothed on a swing, and went forward from there, Dana and then Kim, and then Iris and Kim’s girls, school years and graduations and the wedding by the water, forty years of a family climbing the wall toward the ceiling, and it began at six, and behind the frames, higher up and to the left, where the afternoon light raked across the wall at a low angle this time of year, there was a rectangle. A clean one. Wallpaper a half-shade brighter than the wallpaper around it, the exact size of a frame that had hung there long enough to shield its patch of wall from thirty-five years of sun, and had then come down, and had not been replaced, and had not been remarked upon, not once, in all the Sundays since. Dana had grown up under that rectangle the way you grow up under a ceiling, without ever once looking at it. She looked at it now. Her mother had her back to it, spooning the good soup into the container, and the light came in low and lit the brighter patch like a held breath, and neither of them said anything about it, because there was nothing to say that wasn’t the whole thing, and the whole thing had been agreed, decades ago, without a meeting, to stay down in the part of the house you didn’t go into.

“So,” Lorraine said, capping the container, pressing the lid until it burped. “What is it. You’ve got the face you had when you told me about the mammogram scare, which was nothing, which I said at the time.”

Dana almost laughed. Her mother could read her off her body the way Dana read Tom, the way, she understood in a small cold slide, she had learned to read anyone, at this table, before she was tall enough to see over it.

“A friend of mine died,” she said. “From college. Nic. Nicola Adair. You met her, actually. She took the wedding pictures.”

Lorraine set the container down and stood still a second, both hands flat on the counter, and Dana watched her mother receive it the only way her mother had, which was to steady the counter first, and then herself against the counter, and then to turn.

“The photographer,” Lorraine said. “The one who got me laughing in the parking lot. That awful wind.” Her hand went up, briefly, to her set hair, an old reflex against an old wind. “She was young. She was your age.”

“She was forty-six.”

“That’s young, is what I said.” Lorraine pulled out the chair across from Dana and sat, which she did not often do mid-task, and folded her hands on the blue check, and for a moment the two of them just sat in the ordered kitchen with the freezer humming its inventory behind them. “What happened to her. Was it the breast, was it,” and Dana watched her mother reach for the named, known, fileable griefs, the ones you could send a card into, “was she sick, did you know she was sick.”

“Her heart. A tear in an artery. It’s a thing that happens to women our age with no warning, women who are fine. She was fine. There was nothing anyone could have caught.” Dana heard herself hand it over the way she’d handed it to Tom, to Justine, to Rachel, in order, the facts laid out clean as a place setting, the woman passing out programs at the door of the thing instead of being inside it, and heard, under the front-desk voice, where the front-desk voice had come from, heard it in stereo, the daughter’s calm arriving out of the mother’s, one instrument tuned to the other across a table thirty-six years long.

“Well,” Lorraine said, and it was the whole eulogy, that well, it held everything Lorraine would never take out and hold up to the light. “That’s a terrible thing. A young woman like that.” She unfolded her hands and refolded them the other way. “Did you send something. The family.”

“I’m going up Wednesday. I’m speaking at the memorial in April, actually. They asked me to.”

“You’re speaking.” Lorraine’s chin came up a fraction, and there was the flash of it, the thing Dana had driven fifty minutes to see and had told herself she’d driven up to deliver news, pride and alarm in the same small motion, her daughter chosen, her daughter exposed. “In front of people. A eulogy.”

“A few words. She and I were close, in college. Her sister wanted somebody who knew her from before.”

“From before,” Lorraine repeated, and something crossed her face and was governed, the way the rectangle on the wall was governed, and she stood up again, because sitting had gone on long enough, sitting was a thing you did in the gap between tasks and the gap had closed. She went back to the counter and the plate for the road, and added a second container to the first without asking, the good soup and then the chili, provisioning her daughter for a grief the way she’d provision her for a blizzard, in portions, by date, and Dana sat and let her mother’s back do the feeling neither of them was going to do at the table.

“You’ll want to eat before you drive up Wednesday,” Lorraine said to the counter. “Not gas station. You get up before light and you don’t eat and then you’re on 75 running on nothing, I know how you do it, you’ve done it since you were a girl, you skip the meal and call it saving time.” The containers went into a bag, the bag got a folded paper towel tucked into the top for reasons known only to Lorraine and the long tradition of women who tucked folded paper towels into the tops of bags. “Is Tom going with you.”

“No. It’s a long way for a funeral he has no part in.”

“He’s your husband, that’s his part.” Lorraine said it flatly, to the bag, cinching it. “But you’ll go alone, because you always go alone, you’d drive to the moon alone and text me from the crater that you’d arrived and the parking was fine.” She turned with the bag and set it on the table in front of Dana, squared to the table edge, and stood behind the opposite chair with her hands on its back, not sitting, a woman at the rail of her own kitchen. “I’ll tell you what I told you when your father’s mother went, and you were, what, you were a junior, you drove home for it in that terrible car. You cried in the church and then you drove four of us to the luncheon and ran the whole thing and never sat down. And I thought, that one, that one will be all right, she doesn’t fall apart, she does the driving.” She looked at Dana across the blue-check chair. “That was a compliment, is how I meant it. I want you to know I meant it as a compliment.”

“I know you did, Mom.”

“You do the driving,” Lorraine said again, softer, and for one second the whole set-hair, backed-in-Buick, laminated-inventory architecture of the woman went transparent, and Dana could see straight through it to the thing behind, which was not coldness, had never once been coldness, was the opposite of coldness held so long behind glass that it had learned to look like it, and she thought, with the floor sliding, later, at her own table, I was never any trouble. She’d been told it her whole life like a blessing, you were never any trouble, not even that year, and she had worn it like one. She had learned it here, at this table, under that rectangle, at nine, and had gone out into the world and run it on everyone she loved, and had been running it on the woman standing at the rail of this kitchen for thirty-six years, and the woman had taught it to her by running it first, and neither of them had ever once, not at a single funeral or in a single hard year or across a single plate for the road, put it down where the other one could see.

Say it, said the sub-basement, low and certain, the same voice that had said it in the equipment cage with Justine on the line. Say it now. Mom, I did the thing you do. I had a friend who loved me and I let her go one skipped Sunday at a time, the way you let things go, we are a family that lets things go quietly and calls it not being any trouble. Say it, and watch what she does with it, because you have never once in your life watched what she does with a thing you actually hand her, you’ve only ever handed her the plate back empty and said it was delicious.

“I’ll eat before I drive,” Dana said. “I promise. No gas station.”

Lorraine nodded, satisfied, the ruling accepted, the gap between tasks now fully closed. “Take the soup first, it doesn’t keep like the chili.” She came around and did the thing she did at the end of every visit, which was to hold Dana’s face for one second in both dry hands and look at it, hard, an inspection with love inside it or love with an inspection inside it, the order never established, and then let go and step back into her own straightness. “You call me when you’re back from Chicago. Not a text. I want to hear your actual voice and know you slept.”

“I’ll call.”

“And you’ll be good up there. At the thing.” It wasn’t quite a question. “You’ll get up and you’ll be lovely and everybody will say so.”

And there it was, handed across the kitchen without her mother knowing she was handing it, the exact fear Dana had confessed to no one yet, the thing that would keep her up in a hotel in three days, you’ll get up and be lovely and everybody will say so, her mother’s blessing and her own private terror in one sentence, indistinguishable, the family gift, the family curse, the same object seen from two sides of a table.

“That’s what I’m afraid of,” Dana said, before the front-desk voice could reach it, and watched the sentence land, watched her mother not know what to do with a daughter who had, for once, said the underneath part out loud in this kitchen, watched Lorraine’s face open a bare half-inch on something and then, gently, close, the way the storm door closed, on its slow pneumatic arm, all the way, with a sound like a held breath let out.

“Well,” Lorraine said. “You’ve never once not risen to a thing. I wouldn’t start worrying now.” She picked up the bag and put it back in Dana’s hands, closing the visit the only way she knew how to close anything, by sending her daughter off provisioned, and Dana took it, because you took the bag, and stood, and let her mother walk her to the door she’d been watching the road through, and hugged her on the step in the salted cold, a real one, brief, both of them a beat stiff at the start of it and then not, thirty-six years of not-quite-saying-it warm in the middle of it after all, because the not-saying had never once been the same as not-feeling, that was the thing about this house, it was full to the rafters of the unsaid, and every square foot of it was love.

“Text me from the crater,” Lorraine said into her shoulder, dry, and Dana laughed, wet and surprised, one syllable, into her mother’s set hair.

She drove home in the failing light with the containers on the passenger seat, squared to the seat’s edge by her own hand out of a habit she had not chosen and could now, at forty-five, finally see the source of, and somewhere south of Mount Vernon the arithmetic she had been doing all month ran backward past Nic, past the ear-infection call, all the way to a bottom stair and a pair of already-tied shoes and a brighter rectangle on a kitchen wall that nobody had ever once looked at.

# Chapter 5

[P]{.dropcap}acking for a visitation was its own small dishonest art. Tuesday night Dana laid the black wrap dress on the bed, and the gray one next to it, and stood there long enough that Tom, passing the doorway with his laptop under his arm, stopped.

“The gray,” he said.

“It’s a visitation.”

“The black one’s a cocktail dress that behaves in church. The gray one’s sad on purpose.” He came in and sat on the corner of the bed, which shifted the dresses, which she smoothed back without thinking. “What time are you leaving?”

“Six thirty. It starts at four, I want margin for the construction on 75.” She began rolling the gray dress, tight, hedged against wrinkles, a woman who could pack for a week in a bag the size of a loaf of bread. It was a skill with a lineage. Her mother had packed like a woman fleeing a regime, everything ironed, nothing findable. Dana had corrected in the other direction her whole life. “I’ll be at the visitation four to seven, hotel after, home by Friday noon.”

“You’ll text when you get there.”

“I’ll text when I get there.”

He watched her build the bag. Shoes in their cotton sack. The toiletry kit that lived pre-packed, because she was the kind of ready that never fully unpacked, and she felt him watching and knew a question was being assembled back there, felt it the way you feel rain organizing itself in a certain sky. She gave him time by refolding a cardigan that did not need it. The house ticked around them, Iris’s music two doors down, a faint bassline with homework allegedly happening inside it, the furnace, the dishwasher far below.

“What was she like?” is what he finally asked. Not the question she’d braced for. Worse.

Dana zipped the outer pocket. In the bag now: dress, shoes, kit, phone charger, and a speech she hadn’t written for a memorial a month out, taking up no room at all and most of the room there was.

“She was the funniest person I’ve ever known,” she said. “Not jokes. She never told jokes. She’d just, she saw things. She’d say the thing you’d been almost seeing your whole life and never caught, and once she said it you couldn’t stop seeing it, she rearranged things permanently. There’s a kind of streetlight, the orange kind, she called it soup light, and I have never once in twenty-seven years stood under one without thinking soup light. She’s in my head at gas stations, Tom. Permanently. That’s what she was like. It’s why the photographs are like that. Everyone thinks a camera sees. Cameras don’t see anything. She saw.”

Tom was quiet. She had, she realized, said all of that to the duffel bag.

“And she was a disaster before ten a.m. Genuinely dangerous. You’d say good morning and she’d look at you like you were reading her rights. And she thought Ohio had a smell, which she could never describe but always announced, she’d get out of the car freshman year coming back from break and go, there it is, and inhale like a sommelier. And she sang wrong lyrics with total confidence, for years, decades, wrong on purpose eventually, I think, though she never once admitted it, she went to her,” and the sentence arrived at its cliff at full speed, and Dana stepped back from the edge of it with the practiced footwork of the whole last week, “she never admitted it. Ever. You could show her the liner notes and she’d tell you the liner notes were wrong.”

Tom smiled at the carpet. “She sounds like somebody you’d want at every dinner.”

“She was somebody you’d want at every dinner.” Past tense managed correctly that time, filed, borne. Dana set the rolled dress into the bag. “It’ll be a hard room, is all. Thursday. A room full of people who knew her recently.”

She heard the sentence get away from her, the last word of it, the little door it opened. Tom heard it too, she could tell by his stillness, a stillness with attention in it, and he was deciding, with all his careful kindness, whether to follow her through that gap, whether to say, as opposed to what, Dana, as opposed to people who knew her how. She turned and put the toiletry kit in the bag as if the bag were the urgent thing, aligned it with the shoes, and the moment stood there between them, wearing its coat, waiting to be invited to stay.

“Mom.” Iris, in the doorway, holding her math. “Why do they call it a visitation? Who are you visiting? She’s dead.”

“Iris.” Tom, mild.

“It’s a fair question,” Dana said, because it was, and because it broke the moment’s grip, and she could have kissed the child for it. “You’re visiting the family. You stand in a line, and when it’s your turn you tell them you’re sorry, and maybe one small good thing about the person, and then you get out of the way for the next people.”

Iris considered the machinery of this, leaning on the doorframe, one sock half off in the way that drove Dana quietly insane on ordinary nights. “That’s so awkward.”

“It’s completely awkward. That’s how you know it matters. All the important things are awkward.”

“What good thing are you going to say?”

“I don’t know yet,” Dana said, and that was true, and it was the small rehearsal of a much larger not-knowing waiting in April, and she felt Tom glance at her and kept her eyes on Iris.

“Was she your best friend?” Iris asked. “The lady.”

“Nic. In college she was.”

“Then why haven’t I ever met her?”

The math worksheet hung from the child’s hand. Twelve years old, in a doorway, no wind-up, no malice, asking with her whole clear face the question that four adults, two funerals’ worth of mutual friends, and one husband had been too polite to load. The house made its house sounds. The bassline two doors down turned over. Tom did not rescue her, and later Dana would understand that his not rescuing her was its own message, the first one he’d sent all week without a question mark on it.

“Because we lost touch,” Dana said. “She lived far away, and I got busy with you, actually, you were extremely time-consuming, you’re still time-consuming, and it just, it got away from us. It happens when you’re a grown-up. It shouldn’t. But it does.”

Iris absorbed this with the merciless neutrality of the young, filing it in whatever cabinet children keep their parents’ shortfalls in for later review. Dana watched the filing happen. There was no appeal process. There never was, with that court.

“Marcy and I are never doing that,” Iris announced, and went back down the hall, math dangling, verdict rendered, and Dana sat on the bed next to the packed bag and felt the sentence go through her cleanly, in the back and out the front, the way only a child can fire. Tom put his hand between her shoulder blades, once, warm, and didn’t say anything, the largest thing he’d said all week, and took his laptop downstairs.

Later, with the house dark and Tom asleep or being generous about it, she went down with her phone flashlight and pulled the photo box out of the basement.

It was a boot box, Doc Martens, the boots themselves long dead, size eight, oxblood, bought at a store in Athens that sold incense and attitude, worn until the soles went papery. The box lived on the shelf next to the Christmas mugs and above the roasting pan, and she hadn’t opened it in, she didn’t do the math this time, she’d stopped volunteering for that particular arithmetic around Tuesday. She sat on the basement steps with the box on her knees, in the furnace hum, in the cold clean smell of concrete, and took the lid off.

Chemical smell of old glossy prints, faint and specific as a decade. A drink umbrella, flattened, from a bar called the Union that had burned down, been rebuilt, and become a phone store. A wristband from a show at a club that no longer existed in a city she no longer visited, the band’s name half worn off her wrist’s worth of sweat before the band itself broke up. A ticket stub soft as cloth. And photographs, three inches of them, unsorted, because sorting was for the future, they’d agreed, twenty-year-olds on a dorm floor with the prints spread around them like a game, the future being a country they’d both assumed they were traveling to together, with layovers, with luggage, with time.

Dana went through them one at a time in the flashlight’s circle.

Nic at eighteen with the pig, Gerald himself, scalpel raised, safety goggles pushed up into hair the color of wet sand, mouth open mid-proclamation. Dana at twenty-one asleep on a textbook, drooling, photographed without consent and printed in triplicate as leverage, the leverage exercised for years, produced at parties, mailed once, unforgivably, to Dana’s grandmother. The two of them at the lake the summer they turned twenty-three, arms around necks, squinting into a sun half their lives gone, so young it read almost as a costume, rented youth, returnable. A whole sequence from the road trip junior spring, the car with the bad alternator, Nic shooting out the window at seventy miles an hour, half the frames just blur and guardrail and one, one, a barn and a water tower composed like she’d had an hour, because even then the eye was the eye. Dana held that one a long time. Anyone could take a picture of a barn. Nobody else took that one.

She was looking, she admitted somewhere around the second inch, for the point of infection, some frame where you could see it coming, the way you look back at a photo of a smiling stranger and hunt their face for the future. There was nothing to find. There was only a friendship in its ordinary blaze, two girls who did not appear to understand they were flammable, leaning on cars, leaning on each other, mugging, sleeping, eating enormous diner breakfasts with their whole lives visibly ahead of them like weather on a highway.

Except one frame stopped her, and what stopped it was not grief. Nic at twenty-four on somebody’s fire escape, camera in her lap, one bare foot up on the rail, a whole city behind her and no visible plan for the Tuesday. Dana had been twenty-four that year too, in a rented half of a house in Athens, with a job and a calendar and a boyfriend not worth naming now. She looked at it a while. Then she put it face down on the stair beside her instead of back in the box, and did not ask herself why that one, and it stayed face down the rest of the night.

And underneath the third inch, at the bottom, face down, one she didn’t remember: the two of them at her own wedding, sixteen years ago, caught unposed by some cousin’s disposable camera, the print’s colors going slightly to amber at the edges. Nic in her good dress, the green one, mid-laugh. Dana in white, mid-laugh, veil already crooked from the parking lot wind. Leaning into each other with the whole easy weight of people who have no idea they are already, right then, at the beginning of the end, that the little planet has begun leaving orbit, so slowly that neither of them will feel the tug for years. Whatever they were laughing at was gone, unrecoverable, and the laugh itself was right there, permanent, and that was the cruel kind trade of it, and Nic could have explained it to her, and Dana was going to have to learn to have that kind of sentence alone now, at the bottom of the basement stairs, at midnight, forever.

The furnace shut off. The basement went quiet the way only basements do, a quiet with a body to it.

Dana put the lid back on. Then she took it off again and took out the wedding photograph and the one at the lake, and after a moment the barn as well, and put them in the outer pocket of the duffel upstairs, between the charger and the shoes, without letting herself hold a hearing about why, and got into bed beside her sleeping husband, and lay on her back with her eyes open until the window grayed.

# Chapter 6

[S]{.dropcap}he left Wednesday at six thirty, a day early, and the lie that got her out the door was so small and frictionless she barely felt it go by: traffic, construction, margin. Tom carried her bag to the car in the dark, in his work jacket over pajama pants, and kissed her through the open window, and stood in the driveway with his arms crossed against the cold while she backed out, and was still there when she reached the corner, a shape in the mirror in the porch light, and she drove to Chicago with that shape riding along behind her eyes for the first hundred miles.

The truth was the visitation wasn’t until Thursday and she had built herself a spare day and hidden it inside the trip like cash in a coat lining. She could not have said, if stopped at the border and questioned, what the day was for. Margin, she’d have said. Margin for what. And then this morning, somewhere past Marysville with the sky going from black to the gray it would keep all day, her phone had buzzed in the cupholder, Justine: if you’re in early and want to be useful, I’ll be at her place from 10, fair warning it’s a lot. And Dana, at a rest stop outside Findlay with a coffee going cold in her hand, had answered in ninety seconds, and understood only as she hit send what the hidden day had been for all along. Some part of her had known there’d be a door. Some part of her had packed for it.

Indiana went by the way Indiana goes by. She tried the podcasts and turned them off. She drove the last stretch into the city with the radio down and the lake arriving in slices at the ends of cross streets, gray fitted to gray, the water and the sky sharing one seam, and the city gathered itself around the car, brick and salt-stained concrete and the bare trees like wire brushes, and she followed the phone’s directions north through neighborhoods she’d visited exactly twice in twenty years, both times before Iris, both times another woman entirely.

Nic’s building was the color of a rainy brick, which was what it was, three stories on a side street in Rogers Park with a ginkgo out front still holding March like a grudge. Dana parked and sat for a minute with her hands in her lap. Second floor, the windows on the left, she knew that much from a Christmas card envelope’s return address, apartment 2B written in Nic’s fast slanted hand, and one of those windows had a plant in it, she could see the green of it from the street, alive, watered, waiting for a person who was not going to, and Dana got out of the car before the sentence could finish.

Justine opened the door of 2B before Dana finished knocking. She was tall where Nic had been compact, gray where Nic had gone gray first, and she had a roll of packing tape around her wrist like a bracelet and the specific stunned crispness of a person on day fifteen.

“You look like her college pictures,” Justine said, instead of hello. “Sorry. That’s a weird thing to say to someone.”

“I’ll take it,” Dana said, and Justine’s face allowed something through, and they stood in a dead woman’s doorway, two strangers with one person in common, and then Justine stepped back and let her in.

The apartment was small and had been loved. That fact arrived before any detail, the way warmth arrives before you’ve found the fire. Light came in off the street through big old windows and landed on plank floors the color of toast, and every wall worked for a living. Photographs in thin black frames, hung close, salon style, floor to shoulder height. A green velvet chair worn to the shape of one particular person, a blanket over its arm mid-slide, arrested. Plants everywhere, on sills, on stools, on a filing cabinet, and each pot wore a tag of masking tape with a name in Sharpie. The nearest one, a monstera the size of a third grader, was labeled SUSAN.

“Susan,” Dana said.

“Don’t,” said Justine. “There are eleven of them. They all have names. The fern in the bathroom is Gary.”

“I have a contractor named Gary.”

“Is he also very needy and mostly brown?”

The laugh came out of Dana before she cleared it for release, one hard syllable in the quiet apartment, and Justine laughed too, at the laugh as much as anything, and put her hand over her mouth, and for a second the two of them stood there on the toast-colored floor in the terrible lightness that lives next door to the other thing, close enough to share a wall. Then Justine wiped her eyes with the back of the tape-braceleted wrist, all business, and showed her the system.

The system was sticky notes. KEEP, in Justine’s block capitals, DONATE, ELLA?, and a fourth category, a small anarchic minority of notes reading simply ???, which Justine did not explain and did not need to. Boxes stood half built by the door. The kitchen table held the staging area, bubble wrap, markers, the tape gun’s ugly little dispenser, and in the middle of it all the apartment went on being exactly itself around them, unpacked, unconsulted, the home of a woman who had gone out for a minute.

“I can’t do her desk yet,” Justine said, in the tone of a general conceding a front. “Or the negatives, there’s a whole closet of negatives and contact sheets, the co-op’s going to help with those, Bev’s going to help, you’ll meet Bev. So today is kitchen and the front cabinets, if you’re really offering.”

“I’m really offering.”

“You’ll be sick of me by four.”

“I run a dental office and a middle school auction,” Dana said. “I don’t get sick of people. It’s a diagnosed condition.”

So they worked, which was the mercy: there was work. Dana was given the bookcase wall’s lower cabinets, cables and old invoices and the sediment of a working life, and Justine took the kitchen, and for long stretches the only sounds were drawer runners and the squawk of the tape gun and the traffic below and, once an hour maybe, one of them saying a thing and the other one answering. Woman-hours of quiet. It was, Dana thought, kneeling at the cabinets with a shoebox of cords in her lap, the most honest visiting she had done all week, this, sorting a stranger’s cables with the stranger’s sister, no line, no script, nothing to perform. You could grieve sideways at a task in a way you could not grieve head-on in a receiving line. Somebody should tell the funeral homes. Skip the lilies. Hand everyone a drawer.

And the whole time, the apartment stated its case around her, exhibit by exhibit. Nic drank loose tea now, eight tins of it, a tiny scale, a notebook with brew times, an entire late-arriving discipline Dana had never heard about. Nic owned one expensive pan, French, cast iron, heavy as sin, and no matching plates, six plates from six lives. Nic had, in a drawer, an entire archaeology of single-purpose kitchen tools, an avocado thing, a cherry-pitting thing, a small guillotine whose victim class neither of them could identify.

“She could not walk past a gadget,” Justine said, holding the guillotine up to the window light, turning it. “Forty dollars, whatever this is. I guarantee it was forty dollars and I guarantee it’s been used once.”

“It’s for garlic?”

“It is not for garlic. Nothing needs this for garlic.” Justine set it in DONATE, taped the flap of a finished box, wrote KITCHEN MISC on it in block capitals, and then, with the marker still uncapped, looked back at the guillotine lying in the donate pile, and moved it to KEEP, and neither of them said anything about that, because there was nothing to say that wasn’t the whole thing.

And every object in these three rooms had been chosen. That was the thing that got in under Dana’s ribs, kneeling at the cabinets with a shoebox of cords in her lap, and it got in sideways, the way the worst things do, wearing the coat of an observation about kitchenware. Six plates from six lives. Eleven plants with eleven names. A forty-dollar guillotine for a vegetable that did not exist. Nothing in here had come with the house or arrived on a registry or been the one the store had in stock on a Saturday when a thing was needed by Sunday. Dana thought about her own kitchen, the one with no counters in it, and tried to name three objects in it she had picked because she wanted them, and got as far as one, and put the thought down where she put things, and would not pick it back up for three weeks.

The wall of framed photographs was work people, mostly, and weddings, other people’s joy in black and white, brides Dana would never meet laughing at things she’d never know. A fire escape with geraniums, the light coming through the petals like stained glass on a budget. An old man’s hands around a diner mug, every knuckle a biography.
