A wedding party mid-collapse into laughter, the formal pose abandoned, one bridesmaid fully doubled over, and Dana stood in front of that one a long moment, because it had the thing, the Nic thing, the shutter tripped at the exact instant the performance failed and the people showed through. She looked at it and heard, from clean across twenty-seven years, cameras don’t see anything, and had to go kneel back down at the cabinets and be busy for a while.

She did not look closely at the bookshelf. This she would return to later, in April, in her mind, over and over, the way the tongue returns to the socket of a tooth: she stood two feet from Nic’s bookshelf on a gray Wednesday in March, close enough that her shoulder brushed it when she turned, and registered it only as a category, books, spines, a clutter of small frames and dishes of lens caps and a stone from some beach, a shelf like anyone’s shelf, the visual noise a home is made of, and moved past it to the cabinets below, and knelt, and sorted cables, with the whole of it eighteen inches above her head the entire afternoon, patient as anything.

Around three o’clock Justine made tea, loose, in the proper pot, weighed on the tiny scale, because the tins were there and somebody should, and they drank it standing up amid the sticky notes, and it tasted like a wet garden, and they agreed that it was probably wonderful if you’d done the reading.

“Can I ask you something?” Justine said. “The film thing. In the obituary. Shoot a roll of an ordinary day. That was Ella’s idea, she found it in Nic’s stuff, apparently Nic said it to her at Thanksgiving, that nobody photographs ordinary days and ordinary days are the whole thing, the whole,” Justine’s hand described a circle that took in the plants, the wall, the worn chair, everything, “and Ella wanted it in there, she fought the funeral home guy about the wording, my nineteen-year-old fought a funeral director and won. Was that, does that sound right to you? For her? You knew her longest. I keep needing someone to tell me we’re getting her right.”

Out the window a bus went by, and its sound went by after it. Dana had known this woman three hours. She held the warm cup and looked at the fire escape photograph, geraniums, budget stained glass, an ordinary day if there ever was one, hanging framed on the wall of the woman who took it.

“It sounds exactly right,” she said. “It might be the most her thing in the whole obituary. She’d have hated the lilies anyway.”

“She’d have set fire to the lilies,” Justine said, and laughed, and stopped, and looked into her tea. And it was true, all of it, every word Dana had said, and she heard even so what she was doing as she did it, endorsing from the platform of an expertise six years expired, a woman signing a document with an old title under her name, and the signature holding, being honored, because the one person who could contest it was gone. Justine nodded, restored to task, and if some part of her, day fifteen, sister-radar, had begun to notice that Dana’s knowledge all had a vintage to it, that her stories deferred at a certain year like a fence line, she didn’t say so. She topped up the tea.

“Tomorrow’s going to be a zoo,” she said. “Fair warning. She knew half the North Side. The photography people alone. There’s a woman named Bev who will find you, everyone gets found by Bev.” She wound the tape roll on her wrist, around and around, a rosary of errands. “The people from her building are doing food after, at Marta’s, that’s the neighbor who,” and the sentence walked to its edge and stopped, and stood there, and stepped back. “Who had the key. For the plants. She’s got the plants now, actually, most of them, she took them the first week so they wouldn’t,” the sentence again, the edge again. “She’s watering them. Susan’s staying here till the end though. Ella made me promise. Susan holds the fort.”

They looked at Susan. Susan, enormous, glossy, magnificently uninformed, held the fort.

The apartment held still around them, the wall of strangers laughing in their frames, the green chair keeping its shape faithfully for someone who was not coming back to fill it, the tea going cool, and Dana set down her cup and picked up the next drawer, because the work was the mercy, and the work was almost done, and after it there was only tomorrow.

# Chapter 7

[T]{.dropcap}he line moved slowly because everyone in it was doing the same impossible thing at the head of it, and Dana stood in the line and hated herself for checking her watch, and checked it again.

The funeral home on Ridge was doing capacity business. She had arrived at four fifteen, having planned four sharp and then sat in the car in the lot for fifteen minutes watching people go in, gathering herself the way you gather something spilled. Inside, the vestibule smelled of lilies and wet wool and the radiators’ baked dust, and a man in a gray suit whose whole vocation was moving sad people gently through doorways moved her gently through a doorway, and there was the room, and there was the line, and there was, at the far end of it, past forty backs and bowed heads, the closed casket with the photograph standing on it.

She took her place at the end of the line and the room came to her in layers, the way rooms do when you’re standing still in them with nowhere to put your hands.

Coats over arms, the March damp steaming faintly off wool. A murmur that never resolved into words, the sound of two hundred people all speaking below the threshold on purpose. Photography people, identifiable at range, better glasses than everyone, worse posture, clustered near the back in dark clothes that were their regular clothes. Building people, cardigans, one man holding his hat against his chest in the old way, Marta somewhere among them, Dana thought, one of those gray heads belonged to the key, to the plants, to the finding, and she did not let herself guess which. Young people in the stiff clothes of people who did not own funeral clothes, students maybe, the workshop kids grown a little older. And a whole delegation of brides, women in their thirties and forties standing in twos and threes with their husbands, women whose weddings Nic had shot, come in their dozens for a vendor, except that word had never once applied and every face in that cluster said so. One of them held a small framed print against her chest like a passport.

The casket was closed, per the family, and on it stood the photograph from the obituary, big now, framed, on an easel. Laughing, mid-word, gray hair, forty-six. From the back of the line Dana kept meeting its eye by accident. The line shuffled forward a body-length at a time, and the photograph got incrementally larger, like something approaching.

Ahead of her, two of the photography people were talking in the low frequency the room enforced. She didn’t mean to listen. The room was made of listening.

“Tuesday I kept thinking she was late,” the woman said. “Isn’t that stupid. I had her enlarger station set up. I set it up like a place at a table.”

“Not stupid,” the man said, and then neither of them spoke again, and the line moved.

“You’re Dana.” A woman had materialized at her elbow, sixty-something, silver crop, glasses on a chain of red beads. “I knew it. I’d have known you anywhere. Bev Okafor. I ran the co-op darkroom, Nic printed with us Tuesday nights.” Bev took Dana’s forearm in both hands, a warm firm dry grip, and looked at her. “The famous Dana. Dana of the wedding photos. Dana of Ohio.”

“I don’t know about famous.”

“Oh, entirely famous. We heard about the counters,” Bev said. “Your kitchen. The contractor who vanishes. Gary.” Dana’s expression must have moved, because Bev’s grip tightened, kind, oblivious to the floor opening under the woman she was holding. “She’d read us your Christmas card every year like a serial. Installments. She did your mother’s voice. I hope that’s all right to say. She did everyone’s voice, it was a whole night when your card came, she’d make Raymond wait to start printing.” Bev shook her head slowly, her eyes bright and wet behind the glasses. “December she read us the kitchen one. The plywood. The microwave in the dining room. She laughed so hard about playing dead with the contractor, she said, that’s Dana, that is Dana top to bottom, she’ll outlast him, Dana outlasts everybody.”

The line moved. Dana moved with it, one body-length, on automatic.

“She was going to see you, you know,” Bev said. “This spring. She’d been saying it since the fall. Once wedding season gears up I lose my weekends, so before, she kept saying, before the season. Drive down, surprise you, see the famous kitchen.” Bev squeezed her arm once more, misreading everything in Dana’s face as simple grief, generously, so close to unbearable that Dana almost corrected her right there in the line, almost said it out loud into the murmur. “I’m glad you came, honey. You were her person. You know that.”

Then Bev was carried backward into other arms, other names, doing for the next mourner what she’d done for Dana, the finding, the gripping, the placing of each person in the constellation, and Dana stood holding the fact of it.

The Christmas cards. She had sent them, of course, sent them to a hundred addresses off a spreadsheet, the annual photo on the good cardstock, the annual paragraph, Iris’s year in review, the kitchen saga last December, three cheerful sentences about Gary. A broadcast. Zero postage of the heart. Sent to Nic’s address the way you leave a porch light on for no one, and Nic had carried each one to a darkroom on a Tuesday night and read it aloud, doing the voices, doing Dana’s mother, making Raymond wait, converting Dana’s form letter into an evening, into a serial, into a room full of strangers who now knew about Gary and the plywood, keeping Dana current, keeping Dana present tense, while Dana’s own phone held two unanswered texts like unclaimed baggage. Six years of one-way mail, and the mail had been answered after all, out loud, weekly, to an audience Dana had known nothing about. The room was very warm. Wool and lilies and radiators. Somebody’s toddler, far in the back, asked a question in the clear carrying voice of the very young and was gently shushed, and a little wave of helpless warmth moved through the nearest mourners, the toddler doing for that corner of the room what nobody else could.

The line moved again. Dana thought, with sudden total clarity, I could leave. People left. Emergencies existed, babysitters called, cars got towed. The room would close over the gap in a second, and Bev would say, at some future Tuesday, she had to go, poor thing, she was devastated, and it would enter the record that way, and no one would ever know. She stood absolutely still inside the thought for the length of one breath, feeling the exit behind her like heat.

Then the line moved once more and there was Justine, and beside her a young woman with Nic’s exact chin who must be Ella, and there was nowhere to go now but forward.

Justine looked worse than yesterday and was doing better, or the reverse, it was impossible to say, day sixteen, the crispness holding like paint on a door that had swollen in its frame. She took Dana’s hand in both of hers, tape bracelet gone, a bandage on one thumb where the tape gun had gotten her.

“You came early and packed cables,” Justine said quietly. “You’re in the will now, that’s how it works,” and Dana laughed, one small syllable, and saw over Justine’s shoulder two of the brides startle at the sound and then soften, because a laugh at the family end of the line meant something specific, meant intimacy, meant history, and they were not wrong, they were just not right in the way they thought.

“This is Dana,” Justine said to Ella, and Ella’s red-rimmed eyes came up and went wide.

“Aunt Nic’s Dana?”

Aunt Nic’s Dana. Possessive case, present tense, notarized by a nineteen-year-old’s grief. Dana took the girl’s hands because they were being offered, cold hands, bitten nails painted black, and Ella was already talking, the words coming fast and unguarded, grief with no crust on it yet.

“She told me the story of the pig like a hundred times. Gerald? From your lab? She’d do your face. Your face when she named it. She said you almost failed the practical because every time she said his name you had to leave the room, she said the TA thought you had a medical condition.” Ella laughed and cried at the same time, no border between them at all, none needed at nineteen. “And the thing with the fire alarm and the toaster strudel, and the road trip where the car, the alternator car, she had a whole voice for the car. Nobody else knows those stories now. You have to, at the memorial, Justine said you’re speaking, you have to tell the college ones. You’re the only one left who has them.”

“Ella,” Justine said softly, a hand on the girl’s back.

“Sorry. I know. I’m doing the thing where I make everyone hold my stuff.” Ella wiped her face with the heel of her hand, a gesture so exactly Nic’s that Dana’s breath went sideways, the same angle, the same economy, twenty-seven years and one generation transposed, alive in a funeral home, wearing black nail polish. “It’s really good you’re here. She’d be so glad. She’d honestly lose it.”

“I’m glad I’m here too,” Dana said, to the girl with Nic’s chin and Nic’s gesture, and it was true, and it was the kind of true that cost, and Ella nodded and gripped her hands once, hard, sealing something Dana had no way to unseal now, and the line took her onward, as lines do.

To the casket. The photograph on its easel, close now, laughing past her left shoulder at whoever had held the camera, mid-word forever, and beneath it the closed and polished wood with the plain fact of the matter inside. There was a kneeler and Dana knelt, because the body in front of her knew the choreography even with the rest of her gone to static. Behind her the murmur went on, the radiators ticked, somewhere the toddler again, briefly, like a bird getting into a church.

She looked at the photograph, and could not find one thing to say that was true enough for the room and small enough to survive saying. Every sentence she reached for came apart under its own weight. I’m sorry was the whole truth and it belonged to a different line, a line with only two people in it, a line that had been open for six years at any hour of any day, toll-free, and she had not called it, and it was closed now, and this here, this kneeler, this wood, this easel, was what remained of the number. So she said nothing. She put her hand flat on the cool wood for the length of one breath, the way you’d touch a door you knew you couldn’t open, feeling the polish, the cold coming up through it, and stood, knees announcing her age, and gave her place to the next person, some stranger with a better claim, and walked back down the whole length of the room past all that living murmur, past the brides and the cardigans and Bev holding somebody else’s forearm, out through the vestibule where the gray-suited man inclined his head, out into the March air that hit her face like water.

She sat in the car in the funeral home parking lot for a long time. The lot lights came on while she sat, all together, with a click she felt more than heard. People came out of the building in twos and threes, pausing under the portico to open umbrellas against a rain that was more idea than weather, and she watched them go to their cars, all the people who had known Nic recently, Bev of the darkroom, Marta of the key, somewhere in there, the brides with their framed print, the woman who still set up the enlarger station like a place at a table, each of them carrying away a piece of a woman Dana had been performing all evening, and each of them certain they had just met the person who’d known her best.

The famous Dana. Dana of Ohio. Her person. The only one left who has the college stories.

She had come to pay respects and had been, instead, inherited. There was an estate and she was in it, entered on every ledger in that building, and none of the executors knew the title was six years lapsed. In her purse her phone buzzed. Tom. how was it, love you. She held the phone in both hands like tea and looked at it, the little glowing rectangle of her actual life, her true and present-tense life, Westerville, the plywood, the treaty, the man in the driveway in his pajama pants, and in a month the whole room now emptying in front of her would reassemble in a gallery on Clark Street, and she would stand up at the front of it, and every minute between now and then, she saw with absolute March-cold clarity, was going to be spent deciding which woman walked to that podium. The one they kept describing. Or the one who had actually shown up.

She texted back, hard but glad I came, home by lunch tomorrow, love you too. Both clauses true. The sum of them a lie so smooth it didn’t even ripple going down.

Then she started the car and drove to the hotel, under the wire-brush trees, past the lake she couldn’t see but could feel out there in the dark, enormous and unlit and going about its business, the way the things we don’t look at do.

# Chapter 8

[S]{.dropcap}he was home by noon on Friday, as promised, and by twelve forty she was standing at the plywood eating crackers over the sink like a burglar, still in her driving clothes, listening to the house be empty. The drive back had been six hours of flat gray nothing, Indiana doing its one long sentence, and she had turned the podcasts on and off four times and finally driven the last two hours in silence, in the company of the visitation, which rode along whether invited or not, Bev’s red beads, the toddler’s clear voice, the cold coming up through polished wood into the flat of her hand.

She had a few hours until anyone needed anything from her. She had told herself, somewhere around Gary, Indiana, and had noted the town’s name going by like a small cosmic joke, that she would use them to start the eulogy, and she used them to unpack the duffel, run a wash, chase the caterer, restock the fridge, and stand for a while in the doorway of the dining room looking at Gerald, who had kept the place admirably in her absence and had nothing to report.

The three photographs from the duffel’s outer pocket she did not unpack. She left them where they were, zipped in, like documents in transit between two offices that had not yet agreed on jurisdiction.

Iris came home loud, fed her mother the week in no order, a quiz betrayal, a lunchroom realignment, and the evening’s agenda, Delaney’s at seven, which had apparently become neither a thing nor just people but a third category requiring extensive description.

Tom came home, kissed her longer than the usual six o’clock kiss, held her upper arms and looked at her. “You good?”

“I’m good.”

“You’d say that either way.”

“I would,” she agreed, “but this time it’s load-bearing,” and he let it go, because Friday dinner had its own momentum and Iris was already litigating, for his benefit, what a person wore to a third category, and the evening closed over all three of them, warm, ordinary, and Dana sat inside it like a woman sitting in a lit window, visible, present, and slightly elsewhere.

She drove Iris over at seven and was at the curb at ten, engine running, a woman who had driven six hours that day partly to keep a promise about a pickup. Marcy had been collected at nine forty-five, an atrocity, Marcy’s mom hadn’t even parked, she had honked, from the street, and the social fallout of the honk was tabulated in the car all the way home.

“So I was basically the last one there,” Iris concluded on Saturday morning, upside down on the couch with her feet on the backrest, hair fanned toward the floor. “Which is the opposite of what you said would happen.”

“You were a legend of mystery.”

“I helped Delaney’s mom load the dishwasher.”

“Legends of mystery,” Dana said, “are known for their helpfulness,” and Iris threw a pillow with real accuracy, and something in Dana’s chest that had been clenched since the funeral home parking lot let go a notch, one tooth on some inner gear, and she caught the pillow and kept it on her lap like a cat, and sat there being told about Delaney’s dog, Delaney’s basement, the documented crimes of a boy named Aiden, all of it, the whole broadcast, and thought: this. This was the wealth. A child who still filed the full report.

Justine texted later that morning while Dana was folding the wash. Venue confirmed. The gallery on Clark, Saturday April 4, 2pm. Speakers: me, Bev, Ella’s reading something short, then you. 5-7 min each, no pressure, whatever that means at a thing like this. Also the caterer wants a headcount, for a memorial, how would I know, do I count the people she photographed? That’s four thousand people. Anyway. Thank you again. J.

Dana thumbs-upped it, and then stood with a warm towel half folded against her chest, and wrote it in the family calendar in the kitchen, in the square for April 4th, in her ordinary handwriting, between a dentist reminder and the spring band concert. MEMORIAL, CHICAGO. She stood looking at how strange it looked there, how the word sat in the grid like a wrong species, something large that had wandered in among the dentist appointments, and then she drew a box around it, which helped nothing, and finished the towels.

Saturday was errands and soccer and Gerald acquiring, from some committee mom’s drop-off, a companion lot of donated auction items that now colonized the front hall: a bread machine, a framed print of a lighthouse, a gift basket themed, per its tag, “Cozy Night In,” which had been assembled by someone with a dark imagination, containing as it did two candles, a paperback thriller, and a single enormous novelty mug reading WORLD’S OKAYEST GOLFER. Iris auditioned the mug at dinner. The mug stayed.

Sunday she skipped the neighborhood loop. Priya texted at six, loop at 6:15? knees optional, and Dana looked at the message from the middle of the auction binder and typed can’t today, buried, thurs? and got back a runner emoji face down on its side, Priya’s hieroglyph for the death of discipline, and Dana huffed a laugh at it and returned to tab six, and did not clock, would not clock for weeks, that the exchange had taken nine seconds and gone straight to the archive of things handled.

The eulogy she started Sunday afternoon, at the dining room table, with the good legal pad and a cup of coffee and Gerald for a witness.

Five to seven minutes. She timed herself reading a page of the legal pad out loud once, an old PTA skill; a page was two minutes. Three pages, then. Three pages about Nicola June Adair, from the person who’d known her best.

She wrote, I met Nic on the second day of freshman year, when a lab TA with a clipboard made the worst pairing decision in the history of Ohio science education, and that was true, and fine, even good, and she wrote the pig, and the goggles, and the name Gerald delivered like a papal decree, this animal deserves dignity, and dignity begins with a name, and got most of a page out of September of freshman year alone, and it was warm and funny and she could hear the room laughing where the room would laugh. She knew how to build that. Rooms were her instrument.

Then she wrote, And for the next twenty-seven years, and stopped.

The pen sat on the pad. Out the window, March was doing its gray business with the neighbor’s maple. And for the next twenty-seven years, what. Every road out of that clause ran through territory she couldn’t map. For the next twenty-seven years she made everyone she met feel seen, that was Bev’s to say, Bev had witnesses. For the next twenty-seven years she was my best friend, that was perjury with a room full of people who’d watched her open Christmas cards. For the next twenty-seven years, comma. The sentence idled there like a car at a gate.

She flipped the page and tried coming at it from the other end. The last time Nic and I spoke, and stopped again, faster this time, the pen actually lifting off the paper as if the paper had gone hot.

She tried the middle. The wedding. Safe ground, sixteen years back, photographs on the stairs to prove it. She wrote the parking lot wind and her mother hooting and Nic in the green dress orchestrating two hundred people with nothing but a raised eyebrow and a camera, how she’d gotten the shot of Tom with his head all the way back by telling him, deadpan, that the marriage license had a typo and he was now legally wed to the caterer. Two more good paragraphs. She read the whole thing back from the top, out loud, quietly, standing at the table, one hand flat on the wood, and heard exactly what it was.

A page and a half of material, all of it aged in oak. College and the wedding. Anyone in that gallery could do the arithmetic she was inviting them to do: the college stories, the wedding stories, and then a warm general fog where the last decade and a half should be. Bev would be sitting in the second row having heard six years of Christmas cards read aloud, holding, without knowing it, more recent Nic than the keynote speaker owned. She was not writing a eulogy. She was writing around a hole, and the writing showed the hole the way snow shows a path, by lying evenly everywhere else.

She put the pen down and squared the legal pad with the table edge and sat.

The problem was not memory. Her memory was working horribly well, better every day, delivering whole scenes uncatalogued since the twentieth century, the smell of the biology building, the exact weight of Nic’s camera bag when it was Dana’s turn to carry it. The problem was jurisdiction. Everything she had was old, and everything recent belonged to other people, Bev’s Tuesdays, Ella’s Thanksgivings, the brides’ best days, Marta’s watering can, and she could stand up there and present her antiques as the whole collection. Nobody audits a eulogy. That was the horror of the assignment. She could not be caught. There was no one left to catch her.

Tom found her there at five, still at the table, pad squared, coffee cold, Gerald presiding over the legal pad like a notary.

“How’s it coming?”

“It’s not.”

He sat down across from her, moved the coffee cup out of the way like clearing a lane. “Read me what you’ve got.”

“Tom.”

“I’m a room,” he said. “Practice on a room.”

And it would have been so easy. That was what she kept snagging on afterward, doing the dishes, lying awake. He had made it easy on purpose, made himself a room, a safe one, the safest one there was, a man in a Sunday sweatshirt with his reading glasses pushed up in his hair, and she could have read him the page and a half and let him hear the hole and said, out loud, in her own kitchen, the sentence she had now not said for eleven days: I hadn’t talked to her in six years and I don’t know how to stand up there. Seventeen words. He’d have taken them like heavy things, quietly, adjusting his grip, asking where she wanted it set down.

“It’s just rough still,” she said. “It’s all first-draft embarrassing. Let me get it walking first.”

“Okay,” Tom said. A beat. “You’d tell me if it was more than the writing.”

“It’s a eulogy,” she said, light, the front-desk voice arriving unrequested, “the writing is the whole job,” and she stood and started dinner, and he watched her for a second longer, and then got up and set the table around Gerald and the golfer mug, three places, and let it be the writing.

Monday she went back to work and was excellent. Tuesday the caterer signed and Dr. Fanning tried to book two patients into one chair and was intercepted, and Mrs. Ostrander came for her follow-up and brought Dana a loaf of banana bread with a little note on it, for the parking advice, which Dana read twice at the desk and kept in her bag all day like a permission slip for something she couldn’t name. Wednesday, in the fifteen minutes between the last patient and the school run, she sat in her car in the lot behind the practice with the legal pad against the wheel and wrote, fast, not letting herself read it, a fourth paragraph: the road trip, the alternator, the barn, Nic shooting out the window at seventy with her seatbelt looped under her arm, that’s a citation, Dana had said, and Nic had said, art doesn’t get pulled over. Aged in oak, all of it, another antique for the display case, and she knew it even as her hand moved. But her hand kept moving. Because the alternative to writing around the hole was writing about the hole, and the hole was not five to seven minutes, the hole was not material, the hole was the thing itself, and there was no podium in the world you could say it from, and April 4th sat in the calendar square with a box drawn around it, coming at the steady rate that squares do.

That night she stood in the kitchen after the others had gone up, phone in her hand, and looked one more time at the name sitting in her text list, down under the recent business, the caterer, Priya, the committee chat, Carla now, patient as furniture.

Thursday, she told herself. Thursday night, Tom had his site walk-through early Friday and went to bed at ten, Iris had youth group carpool and would be asleep by nine thirty in the way of a child who has spent an evening being twelve at full wattage. Thursday she would sit down with the thread and read it, all of it, start to finish, like an adult, like a person who balances accounts, and find whatever was in there to find. There had to be a moment, the place where it broke, because things that broke had a break in them somewhere, and she was good at finding the break, it was what she did, you found the break and then you knew what you were repairing.

She put the phone in the charger like closing a drawer, and went up, and Thursday came.

# Chapter 9

[T]{.dropcap}he thread went back eleven years. That was the first discovery, before she’d read a word of it: she scrolled to the top and the top existed, a beginning, the day she’d gotten this number and the old phone’s history hadn’t carried over, and the first message in the record was Nic, of course it was Nic, new phone who dis followed ninety seconds later by it’s dana. i can tell because you read it and didn’t answer. CLASSIC dana, and Dana, alone at the kitchen table at ten fifteen on a Thursday night with the house asleep above her, laughed out loud, once, and then sat very still, because there it was, the whole book of the thing, in the first two messages, eleven years ago, already a joke. Already true enough to be a joke.

She had made tea she didn’t want, for the company of the cup. Gerald kept the watch from the far end of the table, glass eye catching the phone’s glow. She started at the top and read down, and it was, for the first long stretch, wonderful.

Iris-baby era. Nic narrating her own disastrous dating life in installments, each man introduced with a title like a boxing announcer, The Mumbler, Captain Improv, a man known only as Coupon, whose crime was never specified and never needed to be, a saga called simply Greg that ran four months and ended with the single message greg has concluded and Dana’s reply, a string of crying-laughing faces, and she could remember crying laughing, could place herself in the nursery glider with Iris asleep on her chest at three in the morning, phone glowing, biting her lip to keep from waking the baby, typing one-thumbed. Nic asking for photos of Iris constantly, more iris content, i’m a subscriber, i pay for premium. Nic’s reviews of the photos: she looks like a furious little judge. i love her. tell her i said the sentence stands. the cheeks have gotten worse. i need you to understand these are elite cheeks. she’s wearing the hat like she lost a bet. never stop dressing her. An entire spring devoted to the question of whether a raccoon Nic could see from her fire escape was the same raccoon every night or a rotation, a controversy that generated forty messages, blurry photos entered into evidence, the raccoon or raccoons named, collectively, The Committee. Dana scrolled and the years ran under her thumb like water, and the mess and glory of it was all still there, recorded, timestamped, admissible. They had been funny together. Whatever else was true, the record showed two people who had been funny together for years after the phone calls stopped, and she had forgotten that, had filed the friendship’s last era as silence, and it hadn’t been silence. It had been this. A thinning, glittering wire.

She watched the wire thin.

It was gradual and then it was countable. Somewhere in the middle years the rhythm changed, and once she saw it she couldn’t unsee it: Nic, Nic, Dana. Nic, Nic, Nic, Dana. The gaps on her own side stretching, a day, four days, sorry, insane week, and Nic absorbing each apology with a grace that read, at the time, like ease, no apologizing in this establishment, we’re not that kind of restaurant, and read now, at the kitchen table, like a woman holding a door and pretending her arm wasn’t tired. Her own messages, Dana noticed, had shrunk in a particular way, gone administrative. Nic would send three inches of raccoon tribunal and Dana would send back omg and a heart. Nic would ask a real question, how ARE you, like actually, and the record showed Dana answering with logistics, good, busy, Iris has swim now, we redid the bathroom, the answers of a woman summarizing a life instead of living it out loud to someone, and Nic would take the logistics graciously and spin them into material, the BATHROOM. renovations dana. domestic empress, making it easy, always making it easy, and there was the pattern in its full terrible clarity: one of them turning herself into an audience, the other into a newsletter.

And she could remember some of it. Not the four-day gaps. Those were gone, they had been nothing at the time and were nothing now. But there had been an evening, and she could still place the room, when a message had come in about a show going up somewhere on Damen, and Dana had read the first line off the lock screen and thought, with a clarity she had not once examined in six years, I will answer that tomorrow, and had put the phone face down on the arm of the couch. She had not been busy. She had been sitting in a house that worked, on a Tuesday, doing nothing at all.

The Iris content requests spaced out. The boxing announcements stopped, and Dana couldn’t tell if the dating had stopped or the reporting had, and that distinction, invisible at the time, sat on her now with its full weight: there had been a point, somewhere in these gray pixels, where Nic had stopped telling her things, and it had happened without a sound, the way a tide goes out behind you while you’re facing the land.

Paul appeared exactly twice. this is paul, he’s tall and reads, over a photo of a smiling bearded man in a bookstore, and Dana had replied, she saw, with genuine warmth, HE’S LOVELY, more paul content, and there had been no more Paul content, not one photo more, and she hadn’t noticed, hadn’t asked, and fourteen months later, unprompted, between a birthday message and a meme: paul has concluded. No boxing title. No saga. The one that got a title had lasted four months; the one that got none had lasted two years, and even at the time some sleeping part of Dana must have registered the difference, because her reply had come eleven hours later, oh no, are you ok??, and Nic had written back totally fine! long story, will call you, and there was no call in Dana’s memory, and she sat with the cold tea and interrogated herself like a detective with a suspect she already didn’t believe. Had Nic called and gotten voicemail? Had Dana said call me this weekend and let the weekend close over it? There was no record. The thread recorded everything except the part that mattered, the negative space, the calls that didn’t happen, and will call you sat there years deep with nothing after it but a meme, and the meme was funny, and Dana had liked the meme, thumbs-up, tap, done, a friendship maintained with the same gesture she used on the school district’s snow-day announcements.

She made herself keep an even pace through the last stretch, the way you walk the last flight of stairs in the dark. The final two years of the thread were thin as winter light. Her own contributions she could count on two hands, and most of them were reactive, the tapback, the ha, the heart on a photo. Nic’s were spaced now too, the rationing visible if you knew to look for it, but each one was still handmade, and that got through, every single message from Nic’s side still built, a little machine of a sentence designed to be answerable in one second flat, no needs, all bits, easy, easy, easy. card received, kitchen saga is my roman empire, GARY. A happy birthday each way, hers to Nic punctual, Nic’s to hers three days early on purpose because, one year, i like to beat the rush. And then the messages ran out into white space, and there at the bottom of eleven years sat the last two, both gray, both Nic’s, fourteen months old.

saw a girl at the green city market today with iris’s EXACT braid. the double one from your card. tell her the braid has gone national

And below it, eight days later, the final entry in the record:

hey. thinking i might be ohio-ish in the spring. coffee? i’ll come to you, i want to see this famous kitchen

That was all. No follow-up, no nudge, no did you see this, nothing. Nic, who had absorbed a decade of four-day silences with a doorman’s grace, had sent that one and then held the door and waited, and the record showed exactly how long she’d waited, which was the rest of her life.

Dana put the phone face down on the table.

The kitchen hummed its appliance hum. Upstairs a floorboard ticked, the house settling, nobody coming. She had sat down at ten fifteen to find the moment it broke, that was the actual assignment she’d given herself, locate the rupture, the fight they’d never had, the offense one of them must have committed, some load-bearing wall of an event that would explain six years, and she had read eleven years of evidence in two and a half hours and found nothing. There was no moment. There was no wall. There was only frequency, hers, decaying on a curve so smooth you could have graphed it, no cliff anywhere, every individual gap defensible, insane week, every insane week true. The defense was airtight and it was damning: all of it true. The weeks really had been insane. The baby really had been up, the bathroom really had needed redoing, the auction, the practice, the treaties, all real, a whole honest life, laid course by course like brick, and you could build a wall out of entirely true things and it was still a wall. She had not decided anything, ever, at any point. That was the thread’s verdict. You could read the whole record and never find the day the friendship ended because there wasn’t one; there were only a thousand days on which it slightly didn’t happen, and she had been present for every single one of them, thumb hovering, meaning to.

And the last message asked for coffee. In the spring. In her own kitchen. The famous kitchen, eighteen inches from where the phone now lay face down, and Dana looked at the plywood, at the space where the counters weren’t, and did the one piece of arithmetic she had been outrunning since Bev’s red-bead glasses in the receiving line. Fourteen months ago, thinking about spring: last spring, which had come and gone without a word from Ohio. This spring, Bev had said, before the season, she’d been saying it since fall. It was the same trip, carried over a year. It had been alive the whole time. It had been alive for over a year, patient, parked, waiting for one word back, and the word it had been waiting for was so small, the word was yes, the word was anytime, the word was a thumbs-up, the gesture she spent on snow-day announcements, and Nic had died at home on a Tuesday in February, with the spring still ahead and the trip still parked and the door still held.

Dana did not cry at the kitchen table, quite. What she did was pick up the phone and turn it over and read the last message again, and then press her thumb and hold it against the screen, against the gray bubble, the way you’d press a bruise to check it was real, and the phone, misunderstanding, helpfully offered her a menu of reactions, the thumbs-up, the heart, the ha-ha, hovering over a dead woman’s last question like a tray of hors d’oeuvres, and she put the phone down fast, face down again, push, like putting out a small fire.

She washed the mug. She dried it and put it away, which she never did at night. She wiped the plywood, swept two crumbs into her palm, carried them to the sink like evidence. She stood in her kitchen at one in the morning doing the closing-time rituals of an ordinary night, because her hands did not know what else there was, and her hands had a point: there was nothing else. There was no reply to compose. That was the administrative fact her whole body kept walking into like a glass door, that the thread was now a document, finished, in the archival sense, in the sense that nothing further would ever be added to it by either party, and every instinct she had, the fixer, the manager, the woman who returned every call in her jurisdiction by end of day, all of it stood in front of that white space at the bottom with its hands full of yes and anytime and come, please, I’ll make the coffee, Gary willing, come see the plywood, come do the voices in person, come meet the furious little judge, she’s twelve now, she’d worship you, fourteen months of speech assembling itself in her all at once, whole paragraphs, arriving fluent and useless, and no address left on earth to deliver it to.

She checked the lock on the back door, which was locked. She turned off the lights room by room, the house going dark behind her in the order it always did. On the stairs, in the dark, she passed the wedding photographs without looking at them, and knew exactly where each one hung anyway, the way you know where the furniture is in a house you have lived in a long time, her mother hooting at shoulder height on the left, Tom’s head thrown back at the turn, the lit young woman looking off camera at someone just out of frame, and went up to bed, and lay down carefully next to her sleeping husband, on her back, eyes open, a woman balancing accounts, and the number the account kept returning was not six years. Six years was survivable, six years was drift, everyone drifted. The number was eight days. The braid, then eight days of held breath, then the coffee. She had had eight days of warning and eleven years of record and a phone in her hand the entire time, and somewhere in the dark arithmetic before sleep took her she understood that the question she’d brought to the table tonight, when did it break, had been the wrong question all along, had been, in fact, one more piece of management, because broken was a comfort. Broken meant a moment, a cause, a story with a villain or at least an accident. Nothing had broken. The door had been open. It had been open the whole time, and she had simply, on a thousand ordinary days, not walked through it, and there was no way to grieve that like a tragedy, because it had the shape of a choice, made in no single moment, by her, only ever by her, one unanswered evening at a time.

# Chapter 10

[T]{.dropcap}he auction hit its iceberg on a Tuesday, a month out, in the form of an email from the caterer with the subject line “Small Update,” which was how caterers said catastrophe.

The small update was that the caterer had double-booked April 18th, discovered it “during a systems migration,” and was withdrawing with sincere apologies and a full refund of the deposit they had held for a week. Dana read it at 7:40 a.m. at the front desk of the practice, between a hygiene check-in and a call about a crown, and felt the day reorganize itself around the email the way traffic reorganizes around a stalled truck.

She forwarded it to the committee with the subject line “We’re fine, don’t panic, but read this,” which guaranteed panic, she knew that, but there was a protocol to these things: you gave the committee its panic early, in a controlled burn, or you got the panic later, at scale. By nine the group chat had produced fourteen messages, two of them useful. Julie proposed a potluck, for four hundred people, in a gym, and had to be talked down with love. Someone’s husband knew a guy with a food truck, singular, one truck, and the ratio math was left as an exercise for the group. Dana ran her front desk with one hand and the crisis with the other, and by noon she had a list of nine alternative caterers, by three she had called all nine, by five she had two maybes and a hard lead on a barbecue operation out of Galena that a mom on the committee swore by, brisket for crowds, done it for a church of six hundred, ask for Denny.

By six she was at her own dining room table with the binder open, doing triage, when Priya let herself in through the garage, carrying her running shoes in one hand and a bag of clementines in the other, because it was Tuesday, and Tuesdays they ran the four-mile loop at six fifteen, and had for two springs, and the 10K was a month and a half out.

The clementines were liturgy. Two springs ago, mile three of their first loop, Dana had bonked in a spectacular, legs-of-wood, seeing-sparkles fashion, and Priya had produced a clementine from her jacket like a magician, already peeled somehow, that part had never been explained, and had fed it to her segment by segment on a stranger’s lawn while pronouncing, in her flattest deadpan, that this was why you trained with a pharmacist’s daughter. Clementines had attended every run since. They were carried and mostly not needed, like insurance, like grace.

“Tell me you’re dressed under the table,” Priya said.

“I can’t tonight.”

“You said that Thursday.”

“Thursday I couldn’t either.”

Priya set the clementines on the table, in the ancestral spot where offerings to the auction binder were traditionally laid, and read the room, the binder, the legal pad of caterer names with lines through them, the golfer mug drafted into pen-cup service, the woman at the center of it in work clothes at six p.m. She sat down and started peeling a clementine. “What happened.”

“The caterer pulled out. Systems migration.”

“For the gym thing? The auction?” Priya said “the gym thing”, fond, a little dry, the voice of a woman who had watched her friend disappear into a binder since October. “Okay. So people eat grocery store meatballs and bid drunk anyway. This is fixable, this is the most fixable crisis in Westerville.”

“It’s four hundred people, Priya.”

“It’s four hundred people who would eat a folding table if you put a toothpick in it. Half of them are coming to watch the Brennans and the Wallaces anyway, food is set dressing.” She held out half the clementine, segments fanned, the ritual gesture, mile three, the stranger’s lawn, two springs of Tuesdays folded into an open palm. “Eat the orange and put on your shoes. The binder will still be dying when we get back, I promise, I’ve seen it, it’s got weeks left. It’s got a better prognosis than my marigolds.”

And it was funny, and it was kind, and it was Tuesday, and the loop was four miles of the only conversation in Dana’s week that had no agenda and never had, and every single fact in the room pointed one direction, and Dana heard herself go the other way.

“I can’t just,” she started, and the front-desk voice wasn’t the one that came out, something tighter came out, something with a whine of load in it like a beam. “Not everything is a bit, okay? I have four hundred people and no food and a month, and I have a thing in Chicago I’m supposed to be writing that I cannot write, and I don’t have forty-five minutes to jog in a circle so we can both pretend our knees are fine. I don’t. I’m sorry. I don’t.”

The clementine half sat in Priya’s open hand.

What Dana watched, in the two seconds that followed, was a nine-year friendship do a thing she had never once seen it do: flinch. Priya’s face didn’t crumple, nothing so large, it just went briefly, carefully neutral, a screen saver coming on, and she set the clementine half down on the table next to the binder, adjusting it slightly, as if its placement mattered, and said, “Okay. Sure,” in a voice with all the Tuesday taken out of it.

“Priya.”

“No, you’re slammed, I get it.” She was gathering the shoes, unhurried, pleasant, already at the door to the garage. “The thing in Chicago. That’s the memorial? For your friend?”

“April fourth.”

“Right.” A pause, hand on the doorframe, and Priya looked back at her, and there was something in the look that Dana would spend the next several days trying to un-see. Not hurt exactly, or not only. It was recognition. It was a woman reading a road sign and noting, without comment, which road she was apparently on. “Text me when you’re back in the rotation,” Priya said, light as anything, and the garage door shut with its ordinary sound, and the ordinary sound was the worst part.

Dana sat at the table. The clementine half sat where it had been placed, precisely, next to the binder, sweating faintly in its pith, and she looked at it for a long moment, and then she put her head in her hands, briefly, elbows on the caterer list, and heard it. Heard herself. Insane week. Not the words, the shape. The shape of a busy woman declining, warmly or not warmly, one small offered thing, one clementine, one four-mile loop, one coffee in the spring, no single instance of which would ever be the moment anything broke, because nothing broke. Nothing ever broke. Things thinned. She had read the thread, she had graphed the curve with her own eyes at her own kitchen table five days ago, she had lain awake balancing the account and diagnosing the exact machinery, the wall built of entirely true bricks, and here she was at the same table, five days later, laying the next brick with her own hands, running the same program on the next friend down, in real time, with the lab results still warm on the counter. Knowing changed nothing, and the knowing arrived with a chill that had nothing to do with the clementine. She had thought, some naive midnight part of her had thought, that seeing the pattern would break the pattern. The pattern did not care about being seen. The pattern was not a misunderstanding to be cleared up. It was a groove, worn by years, and the wheel found it in the dark without asking her.

She got up, went to the garage door, opened it. Priya’s car was already backing out of the drive. Dana stood in the doorway in her work clothes holding half a clementine, and Priya, reversing, either saw her or didn’t, and the car straightened and went off up the street, taillights, gone.

She ate the clementine standing in the garage, in the cold, next to the recycling. It was excellent. That felt like part of the sentence somehow.

The next morning she called the barbecue people from Galena at eight sharp and had them signed by ten, a miracle conducted entirely in her competent voice, Denny himself, brisket for four hundred, pickles and both sauces, done, crisis over, and the committee group chat erupted in relieved confetti, and Julie sent a GIF of a fainting Victorian, and Dana typed we ride at dawn and got eleven laughs, and sat back in her chair at the front desk having saved the gym thing, again, being excellent, again, and felt the whole apparatus of her own adequacy hum around her like the practice’s fluorescent lights, bright, dependable, and not, she thought, the point. Increasingly, somehow, not the point. A woman could apparently run a flawless rescue of four hundred people’s brisket while the actual cargo, the human cargo, the Tuesdays and the clementines, slid quietly off the back of the truck, and no spreadsheet anywhere would ever show the loss, because the loss was of the exact category the spreadsheet was designed not to see.

At lunch she took her phone to the car out of habit, the parking lot office, the turkey sandwich in its assigned seat, and sat looking at two threads.

The first was Priya’s. Last message from Priya, Sunday: a photo of the race-day weather forecast with three fire emojis, ironic, it showed rain. Unanswered. Below the white space where a reply should have been, the thread’s whole history scrolled away downward, two springs of it, split times and knee complaints and a running bit about a rival walking club, and Dana looked at the shape of it, the frequency of it, and saw the graph again, the curve she now saw everywhere, in everything, the way you see a watermark once someone tilts the page. She typed, I was awful. The orange was delicious. I’m sorry. She looked at it, and this time, this one time, watched herself almost do the thing, almost file it under later, the meeting’s in ten minutes, tonight, tomorrow, almost let the cursor blink itself out into the general amnesty of a busy life, and pressed send so hard the phone clicked against her ring.

The reply took four minutes, which Priya would later claim was a coincidence of meetings and Dana would never fully believe. the orange forgives you. the orange is bigger than this. thursday, 6:15, no dead caterers allowed.

Thursday, Dana typed. 6:15.

The second thread she opened and did not type into. She just looked at it a while, the white space at the bottom, the door still held, fourteen months and forever, and then she locked the phone and ate her sandwich in the salt-white lot, and for the first time in three weeks the sandwich tasted like something, mustard, the sharp pickle, actual bread, and she noted that too, filed it, evidence in a case she was only beginning to understand she was building: that the two threads were the same thread, at different points on the same curve, and that one of them could still be answered, and that answering it had taken nine words and four minutes and had cost her, in the end, nothing at all. Nothing at all. That was going to be the unbearable part, she could tell already, whenever the full bill arrived, whenever some future midnight presented it itemized. Not that it had been hard. That it had been this easy, the whole time, for six years, and she had had the nine words in stock every single day, shelved, inventoried, paid for, and the store had been open the whole time, and, she saw, squinting through the windshield at the plain March light, was open still.

# Chapter 11

[B]{.dropcap}y the middle of March the eulogy existed in four incarnations, all of them dead.

Incarnation one, the antiques: college and the wedding, funny, a page and a half, a display case with a hole where the last fifteen years should be. Incarnation two, the pivot to the audience: less about Nic, more about what Nic gave people, a rhetorical trick that let other people’s recent decades stand in for her own missing one, and it was clever, and reading it aloud in the parked car she had heard exactly how clever it was, you could hear the engineering, like a bridge that showed its math. Incarnation three, written at eleven p.m. in a fury of honesty, began I need to tell you something about the last six years and got four sentences before she tore it off the pad, folded the page in quarters, and put it in her bag, where it still was, radiating, like something not fully extinguished. Incarnation four was incarnation one with the adjectives improved.

She was sitting with incarnation four on a Wednesday night, pen capped, when Rachel called wanting to carpool.

“To the memorial. I’m coming, Amy’s coming, possibly Deb, Deb’s checking with Steve. We could caravan. Or all pile in one car, road trip, God, is that ghoulish? Piling in a car to a memorial? It feels either exactly right or completely ghoulish and I can’t tell which.”

“It’s exactly right,” Dana said. “She’d want the carpool. She’d want a specific playlist and assigned snacks.”

“She would. She’d have opinions about who sat where. She’d put Deb in front because of the carsick thing and then make it seem like an honor.” Rachel’s voice did its wobble and recovered. Dana was at the kitchen table with the legal pad in front of her, and she had picked up the phone with the gratitude of a woman rescued from her own handwriting. Tom was at the armchair with the Henderson numbers. Iris was upstairs allegedly asleep. The house had its ten o’clock sound, dishwasher, furnace, the small tick of the cooling stove.

They did fifteen minutes of logistics and Rachel-weather, Amy’s new hip and whether it was highway-rated, the eternal question of Steve, a detour through the fall reunion that hadn’t happened and the spring one that wouldn’t either, and Dana half listened with the pen in her hand, drawing nothing in the margin, and it was easy, it was the easiest quarter hour of her week, Rachel asked nothing of anyone except surface area.

“So I have to ask you something,” Rachel said then, dropping half an octave, the confidential register. “Because you’d know. Amy talked to Deb, and Deb talked to that guy Marcus, do you remember Marcus, from the co-op, he does the architecture photos, very serious, wears the scarf indoors? And Marcus said,” a breath, “that the last couple years she’d gotten kind of. Isolated. That she’d dropped off. Stopped coming to the co-op nights half the time, stopped doing the group shows, backed out of one last minute that she’d already printed for. And Deb got sort of worked up about it, like, were we all just not paying attention, was she struggling and nobody, you know. Deb went back through her own texts with her and got upset about the gaps. And I said, before anybody spirals, I’ll ask Dana. Dana would know. You’d know better than anybody.”

The refrigerator cycled on. Dana looked at the legal pad, at incarnation four, college and the wedding and the barn, the display case, and heard the question arrive at her door with its luggage, the question she had no standing to answer, addressed to the exact title she had been letting everyone use since the end of February.

And here was the thing she would take apart later, lying awake: she could have said I don’t know. It was even sayable, there was a soft version, honestly, Rachel, we’d drifted, I’m the wrong one to ask, and Rachel of all people would have absorbed it mid-sentence and rolled on, Rachel’s whole genius was absorbing things mid-sentence. The exit was right there, lit, ground-level, no stairs.

“That doesn’t sound like isolated to me,” Dana said. “That sounds like Nic auditing her commitments.”

“That’s what I said!”

“She did it in college too. She’d go dark for a stretch and you’d think something was wrong and then it would turn out she’d been in the darkroom for three weeks building something, and she’d surface with a whole finished wall of prints and no memory of anyone’s birthday. Sophomore spring she missed so many Sunday dinners the dining hall lady asked me if she’d transferred.” All true. Every word of it true, and twenty-seven years old, and Dana heard herself deploy it into the present tense of the last two years like a woman spending old currency in a modern store, confidently, at speed, the bills crisp from the vault. “And the last few years she was shooting film again for herself, that’s a solitary thing, that’s darkroom time, that’s not, people always read an artist going inward as an artist going under. Bev didn’t think anything was wrong. Bev saw her every week.”

“Did she seem happy though? When you guys talked?”

The furnace filled a half-second.

“She seemed like Nic,” Dana said.

“Okay. Okay, good. That’s what I told Deb,” Rachel said, audibly relieved, audibly standing down, a woman who had wanted exactly this, the verdict from the highest available court, and had gotten it, and could now sleep, and could now sleep Deb, who would sleep Amy, the ruling propagating outward through the phone tree all night like a stone’s rings. “Because you’d know. Deb was spiraling a little, you know Deb, and I said, Deb, if anything had been really wrong, Dana would have known. You two never really lost that, right? Even long distance. Some friendships just hold.”

“Some friendships just hold,” Dana said.

They wrapped up the carpool, the departure time, seven thirty sharp which meant eight, whether Deb’s carsickness was a front-seat matter or a medication matter, a warm five minutes of Rachel at her Rachel-est, and then goodnights, and Dana set the phone down on the legal pad and sat looking at nothing in particular, at the middle distance of her own kitchen, and conducted, silently, in the space where a better woman would have already confessed, the review.

Item one. She had just testified. Rachel had come to her as a character witness in the matter of Nic’s last years, the years Dana knew exactly nothing about, and Dana had taken the stand and testified, fluently, from evidence a quarter century old, and the testimony had been accepted, entered into the record, and was being relayed to Deb at this moment, and through Deb to Marcus of the scarf, and the case would close. She seemed like Nic. Bev didn’t think anything was wrong. Dana had met Bev for four minutes in a receiving line and could not have said what Bev thought about the weather.

Item two, and this was the one with the teeth. She had believed her own testimony while giving it. It hadn’t felt like lying. It had felt like advocacy. Somebody had said a thing about Nic that filed her under struggling, under case, under poor Nic, and every loyal cell in Dana’s body had stood up to fight it, instantly, at full voltage, because she knew Nic, and the Nic she knew was nobody’s case, the Nic she knew went dark to build things. And the Nic she knew was also, and here was the tooth going in, eighteen to forty years old. The woman who had died at forty-six might have been exactly what Dana said. Or what Marcus said. Or a third thing nobody in the phone tree had the standing to name. Dana did not know, could not know, had structured six years expressly so as not to know, and had defended her ignorance under the flag of loyalty to a jury that trusted her completely, and the defense had worked, and the working was the worst part. The hand was getting practice.

She looked over at Tom in the armchair light. He had the laptop open but he wasn’t typing, and she knew, in the way of long marriage, that he had heard every word of her side of it, the whole performance, she seemed like Nic, some friendships just hold, and was sitting with it, and would not raise it, and that his not raising it was becoming a shape in the house now, a piece of furniture they both walked around, getting larger by the week, in the manner of furniture in dreams.

“Rachel,” she said. “Carpool logistics.”

“Sounded like more than logistics,” Tom said, mild, eyes on the screen.

There it was. Not a question. He was done asking questions; he’d moved to observations, laid down flat where she could pick them up or step over them, little level things, no hooks in them, and she stood at the crossing for a moment, and the moment was there, and it was even still warm, and she could feel the whole conversation that lived on the other side of it, the one that began Tom, I have to tell you what I’ve been doing, and how long the conversation would take, and what it would cost, and that it would cost him too, the newest arithmetic, he would have to learn what the stairs pictures meant now, he would have to re-file his own wedding.

“You know Rachel,” Dana said. “Everything’s more than logistics.”

“Mm,” said Tom, and typed something, and the furnace ticked off, and upstairs a floorboard creaked under a child who was definitely asleep, and Dana went back to the legal pad, to incarnation four, and read her own opening line, I met Nic on the second day of freshman year, and wrote nothing, and drew, instead, in the margin, without deciding to, a small box, and boxed it again, going over the lines, the way she’d boxed April 4th in the calendar, retraced until the pen nearly went through the page.

# Chapter 12

[K]{.dropcap}im called at 6:50 on a Friday, which was how Dana knew before she answered that it was going to be a whole thing, because Kim only ever called at the hinge of the day, the twenty minutes when a person with any sense knew you were getting dinner on a table, and Kim did not call then despite that, she called then because of it, because the version of Dana that answered with a pan going was the version that couldn’t fully leave, could only half-listen, and half of Dana was, Kim had learned across four decades, still more Dana than most people managed at full strength.

“Okay, don’t say anything, just let me get it all out first,” Kim said.

“I’ve got the burner on, Kim, give me one second.” Dana slid the chicken off the heat, tucked the phone between ear and shoulder, and reached for the wooden spoon, and heard the small sounds of her sister on the other end, a car door, wind, the particular acoustic of Kim calling from a parking lot, which was where Kim did her best calling, in the transitional spaces, never settled, always between the thing that had happened and the place she was fleeing it toward.

“I quit. Before you say anything. I quit the dental supply thing, I know, I know, don’t do the voice.”

“I’m not doing a voice.”

“You’re doing the voice with your face, I can hear your face.” Kim exhaled, a long theatrical plume of it. “Donna made a comment about the invoices in front of the whole, and I just, Dana, I could not, I have been eating that woman’s little comments for fourteen months and today I just stood up and I said some things and I got my bag and here I am, in the Kroger lot, being a person who quit a job at forty-two with, don’t ask me how much in savings, do not ask me the number.”

That was a Kim call, in its whole familiar architecture, delivered from a parking lot, the crisis and the comedy of the crisis arriving in the same breath because Kim had never in her life been able to feel a thing without immediately narrating how ridiculous it was to be feeling it, and Dana stood at her stove with the chicken cooling and did the thing she did, the thing she was so good at that the family had built her a whole identity out of it, she got calm, she got level, she became the flat wide surface a panicking person could set herself down on.

“Okay. First, breathe. You’re okay. You’re in the Kroger lot, nobody’s dying, you have a bag and a car and you’re upright.” She heard her own voice do it, the front-desk voice, warm and containing, and she reached for the next rung, because a Kim call was a ladder you walked a person down, rung by rung, from the roof they’d climbed out onto. “Second, Donna sounds like a nightmare and you’ve said that for a year, so this isn’t nothing, this is a thing you’ve been walking toward. Third, we’ll figure out the money. There’s always a bridge. Remember after Craig, we figured it out, this is smaller than after Craig.”

“Everything’s smaller than after Craig, after Craig is my whole unit of measurement.”

“So this is a quarter-Craig. You’ve survived a full Craig. You’ll survive a quarter.”

Kim laughed, wet and grateful, and Dana felt the small clean satisfaction of it land, the thing she was good at, the talking-down, and she walked her sister the rest of the way down the ladder in about nine minutes flat, the resume they’d update, the person Kim knew at the other supply company, the specific reassurance in the specific amount that Kim could absorb, and by the end of it Kim was steady, and breathing, and saying “okay, okay, you’re right, you’re always right, this is why I call you and not Mom, Mom would already have me in a pauper’s grave,” and Dana said the warm closing thing and told her to text when she got home safe, and they hung up, nine minutes, crisis to calm, handled, and Dana put the phone face-down on the plywood and turned the burner back on under the chicken and stood there.

And could not, for a moment, move.

Because she had just done it. She had done it in real time, with the week she’d had, with Nic’s whole text thread still sitting behind her eyes, eleven years of answering that had turned, by the end, into management, the warm brief containing thing you do to a person to move them gently off the line. She had just done that. To Kim. To her own sister, calling from a parking lot because a thing had happened to her and she wanted her person, and Dana had walked her down the ladder in nine efficient minutes and felt good about it, and had not once asked her a question she didn’t already know the answer to. When did I last call you, just you, no reason. The answer arrived on its own, standing at the stove, and the answer was that she couldn’t remember. Kim called her. Kim always called her.

They had gotten Kim to school, that year. Dana had gotten Kim to school. Nine years old, a lunch made wrong, a six-year-old’s hand in hers on a sidewalk their mother was too scraped-out to walk, and Kim had gotten to be the little one, gotten to cry when she was scared, gotten to be carried, and Dana had gotten to be the one who carried, and here they were, thirty-six years down the sidewalk, and Kim was still the one who got to call from parking lots and fall apart out loud and be walked down the ladder, and Dana was still the one holding the ladder, and Dana had spent a low decade of it quietly, privately, never-out-loud resenting her sister for getting to be the mess, when the truth she could suddenly see from the stove, the truth with the chicken burning now at the edges, was that Kim was not the mess. Kim was the one who’d never learned to seal it. Kim, from the Kroger lot, need out loud, love out loud, everything sent and nothing held back, was the healthy one. Was the one who did it right. Was, God help the whole sorry lot of them, the one who took after nobody in that house, who had somehow come up under the same rectangle and the same scraped year and grown the opposite way, toward the light and the noise, while Dana grew the way their mother grew, straight and closed and unfailingly useful, a desk, a rock, a wide flat surface a drowning person could climb out onto and never once suspect was drowning too.

The chicken had gone past done. She moved it off, mechanically. Upstairs the shower ran, Tom, and somewhere Iris was doing the thing she did, being twelve out loud, and Dana stood in her kitchen holding the ladder she’d been holding her whole life and understood that she had a sister she had not actually called, just to call, in longer than she could name, and that the reason was not that she was busy, the reason was never that anyone was busy, and that Kim, right now, was driving home from a Kroger lot alone, a quarter-Craig lighter and nine minutes managed, and would text when she got in, because Kim always texted, because Kim always reached, because Kim had never once, in forty-two years, decided that the safest way to love a person was to make yourself the easiest possible one for them not to worry about.

She picked the phone back up. She stood with it in her hand a long moment, her thumb over her sister’s name, the number she knew by heart, and the pan ticking as it cooled, and she did not know yet how to do the thing she was looking at, had no practice at it, had built a whole life specifically to avoid ever needing the practice. She did not call. Not that night. She would think about that later, in the hotel in Chicago and after, that she’d stood there holding it and seeing it and had still set the phone back down face-first on the plywood and gone to plate the ruined chicken, because seeing the shape of a thing and being able to do the opposite of it were two different rooms in a person, and Dana had spent forty-five years furnishing only the one.

Kim’s text came at 7:40. made it home. thank you. you’re the only one who doesn’t make me feel like a disaster. love you the most.

Dana looked at it for a while. Then she typed love you too, quarter-Craig, and sent it, and it was true, and it was warm, and it was also, she knew, exactly the size of thing you could send with one thumb from across a kitchen, and she put the phone down and did not call, and told herself, the way you tell yourself the thing that lets you set it down, that she’d call her this weekend, when there was time, when it was calmer, when she could do it right.

She knew, even then, standing there, what that sentence was. She’d read a whole thread of it, eleven years of it, in her own handwriting. When it’s calmer. When there’s time. It was insane week wearing a better coat, and she had just laid another brick with it, in her sister’s name, about the one person left alive who still, from every parking lot in Ohio, kept reaching.

# Chapter 13

[J]{.dropcap}ustine called on Saturday morning, two weeks out from the memorial, and Dana knew before she picked up that something had shifted, because Justine texted logistics and called only for weather, the interior kind.

Dana was on the good hill above the soccer fields, in the folding chair, with a travel mug and a fleece blanket over her knees like a spectator at a much colder, much smaller Olympics. Saturday practice ran nine to ten thirty, and the hill was her office hours, sacred, phone permitted but binder banned, a rule Priya had imposed two springs ago and Dana had kept out of superstition. Below, twenty girls in pinnies ran a passing drill under a sky the exact color of the season deciding, two lines interleaving, the coach’s whistle arriving on the hill a half-beat after his arm moved, sound lagging light, one of those small standing proofs that the world had an order to it.

“Is now okay?” Justine said. “You sound like you’re outside.”

“I’m watching my daughter’s soccer practice from the good hill. I have coffee and a folding chair. This is the best I’ll be all week.”

“Okay. Good.” A breath, Chicago-side. In the background of the call there was no background, no kitchen noise, no traffic; a closed room. Nic’s apartment, Dana thought, with the certainty of someone who had stood in its particular quiet. “So I’ve been building the slideshow. Which, I want it on the record, the software is called Memory Weaver and every time I open it, it says Welcome Back, Justine, like a casino greeter. Welcome back. To this. Anyway, I’ve been in her photo library for two weeks. Forty-one thousand photos, Dana. She photographed everything. There are nine hundred pictures of Susan. The plant. Nine hundred. There’s a time-lapse of a new leaf that runs eleven days.”

“Of course there is.”

“There’s a folder called ‘plates I have known.’ It’s photographs of meals, except it’s not the meals, it’s other people’s tables after, the wreckage, napkins and glasses and, they’re beautiful, that’s the insane part, I’m crying at pictures of dirty dishes at one in the morning.” Justine’s laugh came down the line and it was real, and it kept going a half-second past real into the other thing, and came back, a swimmer touching the wall and turning. “Anyway. That’s not why I’m calling. I’m calling because, okay. I need to just say it in order or I’ll say it wrong.

“Thursday night I was in her laptop pulling photos, and I needed her email open because some of the good ones she’d emailed to people and never kept, people have been sending them back all month, it’s a whole gift economy of dead sister photos, it’s the only nice thing about any of this. And her email was open, and I was in there, and I saw the drafts folder. The little number next to it. And I thought, invoices, right, she never sent invoices on time, it was a running thing at the studio, they used to threaten to hire a bounty hunter.” A pause with a decision inside it, the sound of a person stepping off a curb she’d been standing on for two days. “It’s not invoices. Dana, there are twenty-six drafts in there, going back years, and they’re all to the same address. They’re all to you.”

On the field below, the whistle blew and the lines re-formed. Dana’s coffee had stopped being a thing she was holding. It was just an object her hand was around.

“To me,” she said.

“Emails. Started and never sent. Some are two lines, some are, some are long. The first ones are from years ago and the last one is from,” and Justine’s voice did the smallest thing, a stitch, “February. There’s one from February.”

The whistle blew. The lines ran. Iris took a pass off her instep, first touch clean, and sent it on, and the ordinary miracle of that, her daughter’s body knowing things nobody had fully taught it, happened at its usual distance, in its usual silence, one half-beat ahead of its own sound.

“I didn’t read them,” Justine said, too quickly, and then, because she was on day thirty-something and some membrane in her had worn through to honest, “that’s a lie, I read the first paragraph of one and made myself stop. It felt like standing in her bedroom with a flashlight. But that paragraph, Dana. It wasn’t invoices. It was, she was talking to you. It was her voice, her actual talking voice, the one I’ve been trying to get the slideshow to have and can’t, because a slideshow doesn’t have a voice, it just has forty-one thousand pictures and Ken Burns effects. It was like she was mid-conversation with you and had been for a while.”

“Okay,” Dana said, from somewhere.

“So here’s the question, and there’s no wrong answer, and I’ve thought about this for two days before calling, which is why I sound like I’m reading a script, I basically am, Daniel timed me practicing. They’re hers, but they’re addressed to you, and I’ve decided that makes them yours, the way letters are the receiver’s, there’s actually law about this, I looked it up at two a.m. like a crazy person, the paper belongs to one person and the words to another, which is the most accurate description of anything I’ve ever found in law. So. I can send them to you. I can print them and mail them, actually, I’d rather print them, I don’t want to forward her email around like a, I want them to be a thing you hold, that feels right, don’t ask me why. Or,” and here the script held its breath, “I can leave the folder exactly where it is and never mention it again, and that’s a real option, I mean it, I’ll take it to my grave with a clear conscience. Some doors you’re allowed to leave shut. I’ve read exactly one paragraph and I already know they’re not,” she stopped, chose, went on, “they’re not nothing. So. You get to decide. Take your time.”

On the field the drill broke up into a water break, girls scattering toward the bench in their loud loose constellation, Iris among them, socks pushed down, arguing about something with her whole body, alive, twelve, entirely unaware of the hill, and Dana watched her daughter shove a teammate affectionately sideways and thought, with a strange floating precision, that she would remember this exact composition for the rest of her life, the green field, the gray sky, the water break, the folding chair, the blanket, the phone against her ear where a woman in a closed room in Rogers Park was holding out twenty-six unsent letters like a box with weather inside it.

Take your time. There was no time to take. She had known the answer since the word drafts, known it the way she’d known she was driving to the visitation before the mind convened, the hand signing ahead of the committee, and she spent the pause not deciding but bracing, because she could feel already, in outline, what she was saying yes to.
