Not comfort. Whatever was in twenty-six drafts spanning years of a silence Dana had told herself was mutual weather, it was not going to be comfort. Her voice, Justine had said. Mid-conversation with you. And had been for a while. The whole time, some part of Dana translated, sitting very still on the good hill under the deciding sky. She was talking to you the whole time. All those years of gray bubbles built to be answerable in one second, all bits, no needs, and behind them, out of sight, a second channel, the real one, running continuously, addressed and unsent. The thread had been the lobby. There had been a whole house behind it.

“Print them,” Dana said. “Please. Mail them.”

“Okay.” Justine exhaled, and Dana understood that Justine had needed her to say yes, that leaving the folder sealed had been a real option for Dana and never one for Justine, who could not have lived above that closed door, and who had needed, day thirty-something, to hand at least one of the unliftable things to a person with standing to lift it. “Okay. I’ll do it today. Good paper, I’ve decided, don’t argue with me, I’ve been making insane decisions about paper all month, the memorial programs took two days. You should have them by Wednesday probably.”

“Justine. Thank you. For stopping at one paragraph. I don’t think I could have.”

“You could have,” Justine said. “You’re the same kind of animal I am. That’s probably why she kept us both.” And then, before the line could get heavy enough to break: “I have to go feed Susan. That’s a lie, you don’t feed plants on a schedule, apparently, I looked it up, but I’ve been talking to her, the plant, I stand in the apartment and give Susan the updates. Attendance projections. Catering. If you tell anyone that I’ll deny it.”

“Susan holds the fort,” Dana said.

“Susan holds the fort,” Justine agreed, and was gone.

The whistle blew the girls back into lines. Dana sat on the hill through the rest of practice, coffee cold, chair damp at the seams, watching the drills without seeing them, and drove Iris home through Saturday errand traffic while Iris conducted the radio and narrated the practice’s internal politics, a goalkeeper controversy with factions, and made lunch, and confirmed the brisket count with Denny, four hundred, both sauces, and folded the second load, and lived, hour by hour, the whole visible Saturday of a woman with nothing in transit.

She told no one. It did not even occur to her as a decision. It had the frictionless quality of all her worst moves, the signature arriving before the committee, the drawer sliding shut on its own soft runners. Sunday she ran the loop with Priya at eight, actually ran it, clementines carried and not needed, no dead caterers discussed, and it was good, it was almost wholly good, four miles of ordinary talk with one sealed compartment riding along inside her like a stone in a coat pocket she kept her hand around. Monday and Tuesday she was excellent. Tuesday night Tom asked, observation-flat, laptop light on his face, “You okay? You’ve been somewhere else since the weekend,” and she said April was just a lot, and everything she said now was true, she had gotten so good at true, true had become the highest grade of material she built her walls out of.

And Wednesday she worked her seven to three thirty and drove home through the ordinary streets, past the school with its reader board counting down to spring break, past the marigold flats appearing outside the grocery like a rumor of a different season, and the mail truck was just pulling away from the corner when she turned onto her street, white and boxy and utterly routine, the same truck that brought the seed catalogs and the insurance statements and, once a year, in the other direction, carried away a hundred Christmas cards off a spreadsheet. She pulled into her own driveway and shut the engine off and sat, hands at ten and two, looking down the walk at the black mailbox on its post, its little door shut, its little flag down, everything about it ordinary, and could not, for a long minute, make herself get out of the car, because she knew, the way you know a light is on in a house before you round the corner, that they were in there. Then she opened the door and went to get the mail, because it was hers, because it had been hers for years, because the least you could do for a letter that had waited that long was not make it wait while you sat in a car.

# Chapter 14

[T]{.dropcap}he envelope was the stiff cardboard kind, DO NOT BEND printed on both sides, and it was heavier than paper had any right to be. Justine had written the address by hand, block capitals gone slightly soft at the corners, a hand that had addressed a great many things lately. Inside, the pages were printed on good stock, a full ream’s weight of care in the choice, and clipped, not stapled, with a single black binder clip, and on top there was a sticky note in the same block capitals as KEEP and DONATE, reading: IN ORDER, OLDEST FIRST. There was no other message. The sticky note was the message.

Dana had the house to herself until five forty. She noted the fact. She poured a glass of water she wouldn’t drink, and sat at the dining room table, because the dining room table was where she processed incoming paper, some clerk in her having filed even this under correspondence, and Gerald presided from the far end, and the furnace ran, and the golfer mug held its pens, and she squared the stack in front of her, twenty-six documents deep, the whole archive of a held door, and sat with her palms flat on the table on either side of it for a moment, the way you’d sit before lifting something you’d been warned about, and lifted the clip.

The first draft was dated in the header, indifferent, the machine’s timestamp doing its bookkeeping regardless of what was being kept, and the date was five years and some months old, and it was four lines long.

hey. so this is stupid but i keep composing texts to you and deleting them because they come out weird, so i thought maybe email, email has room. anyway. i keep thinking about the ear infection call. that was the last real one, did you know that? i’ve been counting. this is exactly the weird i was trying to avoid. delete this.

Dana read it twice. The ear infection call. Nic had been counting too. Six hours of interstate apart, the two of them, each privately marking the same call as the last real one, each keeping the same file, and neither ever saying so, and the file had a name now, the file was in her hands. She had not sent it. Nic had written delete this and had not sent it and had not deleted it either, and that, Dana would understand over the next hour, was the whole grammar of the folder: twenty-six letters ending in some version of delete this, preserved, every one, in a folder their author opened often enough to keep adding to for five years. Not a diary. A diary doesn’t have an address on it. These had an address on them, the same address at the top of every page, and they were mail that had never been able to find its nerve, and the address was eighteen inches from Dana’s untouched water glass, and the address was her.

The second was from a month later and it was longer and it was the one where the floor went.

ok, attempt two. i’ve been thinking about why i can’t just call you, because on paper it’s easy, people call people. and i think i figured it out and i don’t like it, so naturally i’m going to say it to a draft folder instead of a person, which is very much the whole problem in one sentence.

here it is. i think i became too much for you. not in a dramatic way. in a math way. i did the math. every time we talked the last few years, i was the mess and you were the fixer, i was the boxing announcements and greg concluding and paul concluding, and you had this whole, you had a WHOLE THING, dana, a marriage and a baby and a house that worked, and i was the funny disaster that called after ten. and somewhere in there i watched you start managing me. you got gentle the way you’re gentle with a thing on your list. i know that gentle. i’ve watched you do it to caterers.

and i couldn’t stand it, so i started rationing myself. shorter texts. funnier. easy. i made myself the easiest possible person to be friends with, easiest in the world, no needs, all bits, and you know what, it worked perfectly, there was nothing left to hold onto and you stopped holding on. i built that. i keep telling justine you got busy. you did get busy. but i got small on purpose first, and i’ve never said that to anyone, and i’m not saying it now either, technically. delete this.

Dana put the page down on the table, flat, both hands on it, the way you’d hold something down in wind.

The room stayed a room. That surprised her; some part of her had expected weather. The furnace ran, the maple out the window did its gray business, Gerald kept his counsel, the water glass held its water, and in the middle of all that ordinary standing furniture the entire load-bearing story of the last six years finished coming down, not loudly, the way a building doesn’t come down loudly if the charges are set right, just a long exhale of masonry, floors arriving at floors, and where the story had stood there was now this single page of good stock under her two flat hands, saying the thing that could not be true because it was the exact mirror of the true thing, reversed, word for word, dated, timestamped, in evidence.

I became too much for you. Nic had watched Dana pull away, and had run the numbers like the exile she was, and had convicted herself. Too much, the mess, the disaster, and so she had rationed herself down to bits and gray bubbles, made herself weightless so that the leaving would cost the leaver nothing, held the door with a tired arm and called it ease. And three hundred and fifty miles east, in a house that worked, Dana had been running the same silence to the opposite verdict, telling herself the story in which Nic’s unmoored life had simply floated free of school calendars and dental schedules, in which Nic had drifted off, unencumbered, into galleries and Pauls and darkrooms, in which the fade was nobody’s fault, which had only ever been the comfortable name for its being Nic’s. Each of them had watched the other recede and assigned herself the dock, and the silence had needed only one sentence to fail, one, in either direction, in any of two thousand days. Do you miss me too. Five words. Either mouth. The whole structure had run for six years on the perfect symmetry of two people declining to say five words, and from the outside, from the distance of, say, a darkroom, or a dining room, it had looked like agreement. It had looked like two busy women at peace.

She read the third one. She had promised herself, lifting the clip, that she’d read three and stop tonight, ration the intake, and the promise nearly didn’t survive its first test, because the third one was from later that same year and it was two lines and the two lines were:

update: saw a mom at the market yelling at a kid in a very dana voice and i missed you so hard i had to go sit in the car. this folder is becoming a problem. delete this.

Dana sat back.

The clock on the stove, visible through the doorway over the plywood, said 4:52. Iris at five forty, Tom at six. Twenty-three drafts remained under the clip, five years of them, February at the bottom like a floor she could already feel through the intervening pages, and everything in her, every trained and load-rated cable, wanted to read straight down through all of it right now, tonight, in one sitting, take the whole building’s weight at once, get it borne, get it grieved, get it filed, be composed by dinner, be excellent by Thursday, present at the memorial a woman who had already absorbed the worst and could therefore be trusted at a podium. She sat in the gray light and looked at that impulse from a distance of about eighteen inches, which was as far as she had ever gotten from it in her life. Take the hit fast, alone, off the books. Nic had watched that hand work from Chicago and drawn the only conclusion available to a person locked outside it: too much for her. Nothing had been too much. Dana had simply never let anything be seen costing her anything, and a person who cannot be seen paying can never be paid back.

She put the binder clip back on the stack. Her hands were not entirely steady and she watched them do it anyway, square the corners, seat the clip, the clerk finishing the day’s filing with the vault on fire.

Then she carried the stack upstairs and put it in the one place in the house that was hers alone, the cedar box on her closet shelf with her grandmother’s things, the box that smelled like a century of Sundays, and set the pages in under the christening gown and the war letters, one more archive of things people couldn’t say to each other in time, and closed the lid, and came down, and started dinner, because it was taco night, and the routine received her, without questions, chop, sizzle, the pan’s applause.

Iris came home loud, trailing backpack and grievance and a permission slip due yesterday. Tom came home with his shoulder rolling, site day, and kissed her temple at the plywood, and said, “Good day?” and Dana said, “Mail came,” which was true, and he took it for the small talk it was dressed as, and got the cutting board down, and asked Iris three questions about the goalkeeper situation, receiving eleven answers. And dinner happened, the three of them and Gerald, the fixture’s warm light, Iris relitigating the honk with fresh evidence, Tom running the bit where he pretended not to remember which one Marcy was, outrage, the salsa passed, laughter arriving on schedule like the whistle after the kick, and Dana sat inside her family’s noise and ate a taco with the building down inside her and the dust still moving through all her lower floors, and performed, flawlessly, one more time, the woman with nothing on fire.

And counted, under everything, on her own private clock, the hours until the house would sleep and she could climb back up to the cedar box, and knew that this count, at least, this one thing, was a count Nic would have recognized from the inside, would have photographed, even, plates I have known: how a person can sit at a full table, in the middle of her one good life, being loved, passing the salsa, laughing in the right places, and be, the entire time, in another room. Nic had lived years in that other room, it turned out, writing letters. Dana had just moved in. The rent, she was beginning to understand, was the same for everyone, and it was always, always paid in the same currency, which was the people at the table, right there, an arm’s length away, asking about your day.

# Chapter 15

[S]{.dropcap}he waited out the house.

Tom went up at ten with the Henderson bid finally signed off and sleep owed him from three counties over. Iris’s light went dark at nine forty and her phone glow at ten fifteen, Dana clocking both from the hall. She loaded the dishwasher, wiped the plywood, laid out tomorrow’s permission slip where it could not be lost again, all the small closing ceremonies, and then she stood at the bottom of the stairs a moment in the sleeping house, one hand on the newel post, and went up past the wedding photographs, and got the cedar box down from the closet shelf, and brought it to the dining room table, and turned on the one good lamp.

Gerald presided. The furnace ran. She lifted the clip and set the first three face down to her left, read, accounted for, and squared the remaining stack in front of her, twenty-three deep, five years thick, February at the bottom like a floor she could feel through the pages, and began.

The middle years were, and she was not ready for this, funny.

Not funny the way the texts had been funny, built, rationed, engineered to be answerable in a second. Funny the way Nic was funny at one in the morning with a glass of wine and nobody watching, digressive, all the caps-lock in the wrong places on purpose. A draft from four years back opened ok so your christmas card came today and i need to file a formal response that i will never send, which is the only kind of response this folder handles. And then two full pages, an annotated review of the card, the photo graded like a wine, iris has the expression of a woman who has seen the ledgers and knows where the money went, the annual paragraph parsed line by line, you said “we’re keeping busy!” with an exclamation point, dana, an exclamation point, you’re not keeping busy, you’re building a fortification, i know your punctuation, i’ve known your punctuation since we were eighteen. Dana sat in the lamplight with her hand over her mouth. In a darkroom on Ridge, Bev and Raymond had gotten the performance, the voices, the serial. Here was the script. Here was where the Tuesday nights had been written, alone, at this hour, at a kitchen table with one good lamp, probably, a long day’s drive from this one.

There was a draft about going gray, from the year it must have started: found the first ones today and i’m not fighting it, i’ve decided, i’m going to go silver like a fox and be magnificent, and the only person i wanted to tell is you, because you’d say something perfect and mean about my vanity and then next week a box of fancy shampoo would show up at my door with no note, that’s your whole love language, anonymous logistics, you think nobody’s cracked the code. i cracked it in college. everything that ever showed up with no note was you. Dana read that one three times. The shampoo would have gone out Tuesday. She could feel the order forming even now, six years late, her thumb already reaching for a cart that had no address left to ship to, and she made herself put the page down.

There was one addressed, technically, to Iris. dana’s daughter: you don’t know me, and this is a letter inside a letter inside a folder, so you really don’t know me. i’ve watched you grow up one christmas card at a time, which is like watching someone grow up through a fence, one board gap a year. you went from furious judge to person with opinions about her own hair. whoever you’re becoming, i was rooting for her from two states over. tell your mother nothing. this folder is a sovereign nation.

A draft about the raccoons, three years old: committee update. there are DEFINITELY two of them. i have photographic proof and no one to show it to who understands the stakes. A draft about turning forty-three: justine took me to a restaurant where the menu had no prices and i spent the whole night composing your review of it in my head. you were very funny. i laughed at the wrong moment and had to say it was the wine. A draft that was just an inventory, titled in the subject line things i bought instead of calling you, and it was the gadget drawer, itemized, the avocado thing, the cherry pitter, a tiny guillotine, forty dollars, i don’t know what it’s for, the man said garlic but the man lies. i’m going to die owning this and someone’s going to have to figure out what it was for. Dana put that page down and looked at Gerald and did not laugh, and then did, one broken syllable of it, alone, at midnight, at the table where she was supposed to be writing a eulogy that was due in three weeks, no, two, no, less, and she stopped and made herself unclench her jaw.

And Paul was in there. The real Paul, the two-year Paul, the no-title Paul. One draft, mid-era, longer than the others around it.

paul concluded, which you know, because i sent you the world’s most heavily engineered two-word version. here’s the director’s cut, for the folder. he said, and i quote, you never let me carry anything. i told him that was insane, i said name one thing, and he stood there in the kitchen holding his keys and said, that’s the game, nic. naming one thing is the game. you make everyone play it and nobody’s ever won.

then i helped him carry his boxes down. i made it funny on the stairs. he laughed and looked so sad doing it. anyway he was right, and you’re the only person i could ever have admitted that to, which is why i’m admitting it to your ghost in a drafts folder instead, because the actual you would be KIND about it, you’d be so kind and so useful, you’d have a plan by thursday, and i couldn’t stand it. delete this.

Dana read it twice, and the second time it opened all the way, like a door with a two-stage lock. Because she had a plan by Thursday. She always had a plan by Thursday. It was the thing she was, the thing everyone came to her for, coats and detours and briskets for four hundred, and here, in evidence, in lowercase, was what the plan felt like from the other side of it. Managed. Handled. A thing on the list. Paul in a kitchen holding his keys and Nic on a fire escape holding a phone and the whole world of people who loved a fixer, all discovering the same locked door in her, the door that only opened outward. You make everyone play it and nobody’s ever won. Two women, Dana thought, one wall between them, and it had been the same wall, hers and Nic’s, and they had built it together from both sides, mortared it with jokes, and each of them had believed to the end that the other one held the only key.

The late drafts went spare, and she felt the change under her hands the way you feel a road change under tires. Six months, then four, then gaps of most of a year. Two lines. One line. saw a kid in a bishop middle hoodie at midway and stood there like a dropped call. A whole draft that read, in its entirety, hi. nothing. hi.

And then, three years into the silence, between two of the small ones, a page that was not like the others.

It had no lowercase charm in it. The typing was bad, letters doubled and not fixed, a woman going at a keyboard with her whole hand.

you are a coward. there. i said the thing. you are a coward and you have been one since we were twenty-two and the difference is that back then i thought it was shyness and i covered for you and i have been covering for you for twenty years. i have been the easy one. i made it easy. i made it SO easy, dana, i took the whole weight of the thing and carried it in a joke so you would never once have to do a single hard minute to keep me, and you took that deal every time. every single time. you have never in your life had to want me out loud. i have organized it that way for you and i am so tired and i would like, once, ONE TIME, for you to pick up a phone without a reason.

There was a second page under it, dated the next day, and it was six lines.

that was unfair. you have a child and a job and a mother and you are not a coward, you are a person with a life, and i am a person who arranged herself to be optional and then got angry about being optional, which is a scheme, not a grievance.

i’m not deleting it though. i want to know i thought it. if i delete it i’ll just think it again in march.

Dana read the first page twice and the second page once, and found that the first page was the only thing in the folder so far that had not hurt. It was almost a relief to be called something. Twenty-two drafts of a woman apologizing for her own weight, and here, on one badly typed page, was a person with a temper, alive, furious, entirely correct, and Dana sat in the lamplight with her hand flat on it and thought, there you are.

Then she read the retraction again and it took the relief away, because Nic had gotten up the next morning and talked herself out of it, and had filed the anger where she filed everything, and had gone on being easy for three more years.

And Dana was angry then, and it did not go where anger is supposed to go at two in the morning with a dead woman’s letters flat under your hands. It went at the retraction. At a woman who could type ONE TIME, for you to pick up a phone without a reason, and mean it, and be right, and then get up the next day and take it back and file it and go on for three more years being the easiest person on earth to fail. Say it, Dana thought at the page, at a woman five weeks dead. Say it out loud to me once and I drive up that weekend, you know I do, you knew it then. You had my number and my address and one stamp’s worth of nerve to find, and you never went and found it, and you let me be the only one who did this to us.

Then it went out of her the way a match goes out, and left the smell of itself in the room, and she sat in the lamplight with no floor anywhere under her, ashamed at a depth she had not known was available, because she had just sat at her own dining room table and been furious at a dead woman for failing to make her do better.

One from a January, no salutation: bev says i should just call you. bev doesn’t know there’s a version of me that got left and a version of you that did the leaving and neither of them is real and i can’t figure out how to get either of us out of the story alive. The subject lines went blank. The jokes thinned the way her own texts had thinned, and Dana, who had graphed one decay curve already this month at this table, watched its mirror image assemble under the lamp, the two curves converging on the same white silence from opposite sides, symmetrical, patient, and she made herself keep an even pace, page after page, down the last flight of stairs in the dark, and somewhere on that flight she stopped being a reader. She would tell Tom that on Friday, the closest she’d get to naming what the night was. Somewhere in the late pages she stopped reading letters and started keeping a vigil, the way you sit with someone, hours past the point of conversation, because leaving before it’s over is the one thing you won’t do twice.

The February draft was on top of the last page, alone, and it was dated ten days before the neighbor with the key found her, and it was different, and Dana knew it was different before she read a word, because it was typed clean. No lowercase slouch. Sentences with their shoulders back.

Dana.

I’m doing it. April, before wedding season eats the calendar. I’ve told Bev, I’ve told the plants, I’m telling the folder, which makes it official in the only jurisdiction I’ve ever fully belonged to.

I keep looking at the text I sent you last winter. The coffee one. You never answered it, and I’ve spent a year deciding what that means, and I’ve landed here: it deserved better than a text. Twenty-seven years, six of them silent, and I tried to fix it with lowercase and a question mark, like a coward, like a person leaving a note on a windshield. So. No more notes. I’m going to drive down some ordinary Tuesday in April and knock on the door of the famous kitchen, and you’re going to open it, because you always open it, that was never once the thing I doubted.

And if it’s awkward, it’ll be awkward. All the important things are awkward.

Not deleting this one. Practicing.

The lamp hummed. The furnace had cycled off somewhere in the last hour and the house stood in its deepest quiet, two forty-one by the stove clock, and Dana sat with the last page flat under her two hands and did not move for a long time.

All the important things are awkward. She had said that. She had said it to Iris, in a doorway, three weeks ago, about receiving lines, and had believed she was coining it, and here it was in a dead woman’s February, typed clean, shoulders back, and there was no way to know who had said it first, in some dorm room, some diner, some car with a bad alternator, which of them had minted it and which had carried it, because that was what twenty-seven years did, it made a common currency, and you went on spending it long after the border closed, both of you, in your separate countries, out of the same worn purse.

April. Some ordinary Tuesday in April. She would have knocked. Dana looked down the table at the front hall, at the actual door, brass knob, sidelight, the door of the famous kitchen, and let herself see it for one full unguarded second, the knock, the opening, the two of them on the mat with twenty-seven years and six silent ones between them and the coffee going on behind, and then she folded the seeing away, carefully, because it had never happened and the tense it lived in did not exist, and because the memorial was Saturday, April fourth, and the arithmetic of that, the trip and the funeral landing in the same square of the calendar, one printed over the other like a double exposure, was more than the lamp and the hour could hold.

She squared the stack. All twenty-six now, read, in order, oldest first. She clipped them, and sat, and understood that she had been wrong at this table two weeks ago, wrong in the one way she hadn’t priced in. She had read the thread and found no moment, no break, and concluded the whole ruin was drift, weather, a thing that had happened to them. The folder said otherwise. The folder said Nic had been standing at the door the entire time, hand raised, practicing, and the door had been six years unanswered but never once locked, and the last thing her friend had done on this earth, among the last things, was decide to knock anyway. It hadn’t been drift at the end. At the end it had been courage, gathering, ten days short.

Dana carried the box upstairs through the dark house. On the way she stopped, without planning to, at Iris’s door, open the width of a hand, and stood there a while in the hall listening to her daughter breathe, the deep even oceanic breathing of a child with no drafts folder anywhere in her, everything sent, everything said, the whole loud account current, and Dana stood in the dark with the cedar box against her chest like a person holding weather, and made, in that hallway, in the jurisdiction of two forty-one in the morning, the only ruling of the whole long night: Tom. Friday. All of it. Out loud, to his face, before the folder taught her its last and favorite trick, which was how good a held thing could feel, how warm, how almost like company, a letter forever practiced and never, ever sent.

# Chapter 16

[T]{.dropcap}he cedar box sat on Dana’s dresser all day like a houseguest who knew something.

She’d put it there Wednesday at nearly three in the morning and had not opened it since, twenty-six printed drafts clipped inside it, Nic’s whole unsent life, and it kept her company across the bedroom in a way that was not comfortable and not exactly unwelcome either, and on Thursday, home early, the house empty until the school run, she stood in front of it and did not open it and instead went down to the basement to answer a question the box had put to her at two in the morning and she had been refusing to hear ever since, which was: and what did you keep.

Because that was what the folder had done, past the grief of it, past the courage of it. It had made her a person who had been kept. Nic had kept her, for five years, in a cedar box’s worth of letters never sent, had gone on writing to her into a silence, and the fact of being kept like that, so faithfully, by someone she’d let go, had a shadow question folded inside it that Dana had carried up the stairs at two forty-one and set down and walked away from, and the question was whether the keeping had ever run both ways, or whether Nic had been alone in it, writing to a woman who had not so much as saved her postcards.

The basement was Tom’s downstairs and everyone’s junk. She went past the table saw and the good tools on their pegboard, past the Christmas boxes and the crib they kept saying they’d give away, to the far corner under the stairs where her own past lived in the archaeological layers of a person who moved efficiently and threw away almost nothing, and she found it faster than she wanted to, because a thing you have to hunt for is a thing you half-abandoned and a thing your hands go straight to in the dark is a thing you knew exactly where you’d put. A banker’s box. Her own block capitals on the end, younger, rounder, DANA, COLLEGE & MISC, the ampersand of a girl with time.

She carried it up to the dining room table, where the family did its reckonings, and sat down where she’d sat to read the thread and to read the drafts, the same chair, the confessional of the house, and Gerald presided from the far end with his glass eye, and she took the lid off.

And it was all there.

Not organized. Mercy and indictment at once, because unlike everything else in her life this one box had never been sorted, never itemized, a box she’d carried through four moves and never once opened to curate, which meant it was the one honest archive she owned, the only record she’d never gotten her managing hands on. Ticket stubs gone the brown of old teeth. A cassette, hand-labeled in the lowercase slouch she’d have known anywhere now, SONGS DANA GETS WRONG (A CORRECTIVE), a lie, and Nic’s whole sense of humor in a title, because Nic was the one who sang them wrong and knew it and had made Dana a tape of the correct versions purely so she could argue about it, and Dana had kept it twenty-six years past the last machine that could play it. A photograph of herself at twenty-one asleep on a physiology textbook, mouth open, a thread of drool caught in the flash like a filament, printed, she happened to know, in triplicate, one copy of which had been mailed without mercy to her grandmother and one of which she had found in the boot box three weeks ago, and here was the third, kept, the evidence of her own humiliation saved twice over for a quarter century because Nic had thought it was funny and Nic thinking a thing was funny had been, for a long time, reason enough to build a whole life’s worth of keeping around.

And the postcards. That was where the room changed.

They were rubber-banded, the band gone to powder and snapping at her thumb, a stack of them thick as a deck, and they were Nic’s, sent, actual mail with stamps and postmarks on it, and Dana understood what she was holding about four cards in, and had to put her hands flat on the table on either side of them. Because they had dates. Postmarks. A whole decade and a half of Nic reaching across real distance in the years before the reaching went silent, and Dana, who could not stop being the woman she was, began without deciding to put them in order, oldest to newest, the clerk in her sorting even this, and the order was the whole story, the order was the same graph the thread had drawn, the exact same decaying curve, only this was the top half of it, the half before the silence, the half Dana had kept.

The early ones came thick. Postcards from shoots, from cities, the fronts always chosen to be funny, the worst tourist card in every gift shop, a fiberglass muskie, a mothballed battleship, the largest ball of something in three states, and the backs full in the lowercase slouch, running out of room, arrows up the margins, small and constant and reaching, thinking of you at this genuinely upsetting roadside attraction, wish you were here to see how bad it is. Every few weeks. Then every few months. Then the gaps started, and Dana watched them start, in her own kept archive, watched the postmarks stretch, a season, then two, and she saw, with the cold forensic clarity the whole spring had been teaching her, that the gaps in the postcards did not match gaps in Nic. Nic kept sending. It was the frequency that thinned, on Nic’s side too, slowing to meet the slowing on Dana’s, both of them decaying toward the same silence from opposite ends, except that Dana now knew, because she had read the cedar box, what Nic had done when the postcards finally stopped. She hadn’t stopped writing. She’d stopped sending. The postcards ended and the drafts began. Dana’s box closed at one end almost exactly where Nic’s opened at the other, the two archives clasping like hands, and Dana could date the seam, could hold the last postcard Nic ever mailed her, a stupid glorious card of a giant roadside chicken, postmarked a spring six years gone, four lines on the back that were not sad, that were just Nic, just reaching, ending with the thing she always ended with, the sign-off Dana had forgotten she knew until it undid her at her own dining room table, more soon. n.

More soon.

There was no more soon. That had been the last one. Dana had it in her hand, the final sent thing, and somewhere in the cedar box across the ceiling upstairs was the first unsent thing, and between them was a gap of a few months, near enough to a clean handoff, the last postcard mailed and the first letter drafted-not-sent within the same year, as though Nic had reached the far edge of what an unanswered person can keep doing out loud and had simply moved the whole operation indoors, kept writing, kept reaching, into a folder, into the dark, for five more years, more soon, more soon, twenty-six times more soon, and had died with her hand still up.

And Dana had kept the giant chicken.

She sat with it a long time. Outside the dining room window March did its gray business with the neighbor’s maple, and Gerald kept his glass counsel, and the furnace ran, and Dana held a postcard of a fiberglass chicken and understood the thing that was worse than having let a friend go, which was that she had not let her go, not really, not in the way that would have let either of them off. She had kept everything. She had carried this box through four moves and two decades, had kept the drool photo and the corrective cassette and every reaching card, had loved Nic the entire time in the one way that cost her nothing, silently, in a banker’s box in a basement, the way you keep a thing you have no intention of using, and had let the living woman who mailed giant chickens into her silence go on standing at the door, hand up, for years, because keeping was so much easier than answering, because a box in a basement never once asked you how you actually were, never needed a ride from the airport, never called at six fifty on a Friday from a parking lot, a box just sat there being kept and let you feel, God help her, close.

Two women. Two boxes. One of them had filled hers with everything she couldn’t bring herself to send, and one of them had filled hers with everything she’d received and stopped answering, and neither of them had ever once, in six years, put a stamp on the truth and mailed it. The only difference between them that mattered now was that Nic had run out of time and Dana had not, that Dana was sitting at a dining room table on a Thursday afternoon with a heartbeat and a box and a husband coming home at six, still inside the tense that existed, the one where you could still do the thing, still put the stamp on it, still answer.

She did not cry, particularly. She’d notice that later, that this hadn’t been the crying kind, that the crying kind kept not arriving in the shapes she expected and arriving instead as this, as a woman going very still at a table holding a chicken. She squared the postcards, oldest to newest, the whole reaching decade of them, and she did not put them back in the basement. She put them in the cedar box. Upstairs, that evening, before Iris got home, she opened Nic’s box and laid her own kept things in with the drafts, the postcards and the drool photo and the corrective cassette, the sent and the unsent together at last in one cedar box on her dresser, the whole correspondence reunited twenty years too late and six years too late and exactly, she decided, on time, because there was nowhere else on earth the two halves belonged except together, and because a person should not have to be dead for her letters and your letters to finally end up in the same box.

Then she went down and started dinner, and held the confession to Friday, hard, a date with a box drawn around it, because she was not going to do it to Tom, not for one more insane true week, not with the man himself coming through the door at six with his shoulder rolling, alive, in the tense that still existed, close enough to answer.

# Chapter 17

[I]{.dropcap}ris left for Marcy’s at six on Friday with a duffel and a sleeping bag and instructions to be human, and by six fifteen the house had the particular acoustics of a kept appointment.

Dana had built the evening. Not staged, she’d told herself, folding laundry that afternoon, just cleared, and she’d made the good pasta, and opened wine but poured it in the ordinary glasses, no candles, nothing that announced itself, and they’d eaten at the dining room table with Gerald and talked about the Henderson job finally closing and the goalkeeper situation, and Tom had told the one about the drywall guy and the wasp, which she had heard eleven times and laughed at eleven times because it earned it every time, and then the plates were in the dishwasher and the kitchen was wiped and the evening arrived at the place where they would normally drift to the living room and their separate screens in adjacent chairs, the marriage’s default idle, sixteen years smooth.

“Don’t turn the game on yet,” Dana said. “I need to tell you some things. It’s a lot of things. I’ve been,” and the front-desk voice came up under her like a moving walkway, an easier gait right there for the taking, and she stepped off it. “I’ve practiced this all day and I’m not going to do the practiced version. I’m just going to go. Okay?”

Tom turned his chair to face her. He didn’t say okay. He said, “Finally,” not unkindly, barely audibly, mostly to the table, and that one word told her the size of what he’d been carrying around the shape of, all these weeks, walking past it in the halls.

She started where it started.

“I hadn’t spoken to Nic in six years. Before she died. Not a fight. Nothing happened. I stopped answering, a little at a time, and I let it become the weather, and then she was dead and her sister called me her best friend and asked me to speak at the memorial, and I said yes, and I’ve spent a month letting a hundred people believe a thing that isn’t true, because it was true once and nobody alive can check.” Her hands were flat on the table. She watched them. “That’s the frame. Everything else hangs on it. You should know the whole thing, so I’m going to tell you the whole thing, and some of it’s going to be about you, and I need you to let me get all the way through.”

And she went. The text with the wrong day in it. The lie about Julie and the caterer, told eight seconds into knowing, and how frictionless it had felt, how prepaid, as if she had kept that lie fueled on the tarmac for years. The favorites list, and the half-second in the school gym when being claimed had felt sweet, she gave him that one whole, watched it cost her, paid it. Bev and the Christmas cards, the darkroom serial, the voices, and what it had done to stand in a receiving line discovering that her broadcast had been received all along, out loud, weekly, to a room. The thread read end to end at this table while he slept, the curve, the no-moment, the coffee text that had waited fourteen months and forever. Rachel’s phone call, and she made herself do that one in full, quoted her own testimony, she seemed like Nic, Bev didn’t think anything was wrong, and watched Tom’s face receive the fact that she’d said those sentences in this kitchen, twelve feet from his chair, while he listened and filed it under Rachel being Rachel. And then the drafts, the envelope, the cedar box eleven feet above his head this whole week, twenty-six letters, and what was in them, the mirror, a woman rationing herself smaller and smaller so that being left would cost the leaver nothing, and her own box under it now, the one she’d hauled up from the basement the day before and could not not tell him about, the postcards Nic had actually mailed across the good years, the giant chicken, the last more soon, every one kept through four moves and fewer and fewer of them answered, the mirror’s other face, that she had done the identical thing from her own end, filed a whole friendship in a banker’s box and let the living woman go, and Paul’s game, name one thing, and February, typed clean, the knock that was still seven weeks out when the artery tore.

At some point in the telling she found the strangest thing: it was tellable. A month of scaffolding and secrecy, a structure she’d maintained like a second job, and laid out plainly in her own kitchen it took forty minutes. Forty minutes, start to end. The vault, opened, was the size of a shoebox.

Tom listened, without decoration. Somewhere in the middle his hand made it across the table and sat on the wood near hers, not touching, available, like something plugged in.

“And here’s the part I owe you specifically,” Dana said. The wine sat untouched in both glasses, staging after all, both of them had known it. “Everyone thinks I pulled away from her because I got busy with Iris. That’s the kind version. The true version is that being around her made me feel judged, and nobody was doing the judging but me. She had this life, Tom. Galleries and fire escapes and terrible men with funny names, and it was hers, all the way down, every inch of it chosen, and I had,” she gestured at the house around them, the fixture, the plywood through the doorway, the whole warm solvent kingdom, “I had this, which I wanted, which I’d choose again, I need you to hear that part with the rest of it. And I still envied her so hard I couldn’t breathe around it some days. And instead of saying that out loud one time, one time, to her or to you or to a mirror, I turned it into a story where she was the one who’d drifted, too free for school calendars, and I filed her, and I stopped answering, and I built the whole thing so gradually that I never had to watch myself do it.” She breathed. “She died thinking she’d gotten too heavy for me. She spent five years apologizing for it to a folder. And the actual weight I couldn’t carry was mine. It was never once her.”

The furnace ran. Gerald kept the far end.

“Okay,” Tom said at last, quietly. “My turn?”

“Your turn.”

He was silent a moment longer, arranging something, a man who measured twice. “I want to say the generous things first, because they’re true and because you’ll discount them if they come second. You didn’t kill her. A torn artery killed her, and it would have torn on the same Tuesday if you’d talked every week. And people drift. Everybody at that memorial has a Nic somewhere, everybody, that’s not an excuse, it’s just the truth about the size of the club.” He turned his glass a quarter turn, didn’t drink. “Here’s the other thing, and I’ve been carrying it a while, so it may come out with corners on it.

“Watching you this month has been like watching you every month, only louder. You came home from that visitation folded in thirds and told me you were good, load-bearing, joke attached, and I let you, because that’s the deal, right, that’s been the deal a long time. You run the front desk of this marriage.” He said it evenly, no blade in it. “I get the summary version. Everything handled, nothing itemized. And I signed up for it, I’m not clean here, it’s restful being married to the front desk, everything’s always fine, the insurance always goes through. But then some week like this one comes, and I watch you grieve a woman I never met, harder than you’ve ever let me see you feel anything, including,” and there his evenness paid out to its end, and he stopped, and chose to finish it anyway, “including some things we went through together, Dana. And I’m across the table for all of it. I’ve been across the table for a month. And what I keep thinking is, her sister found twenty-six letters. And I live here. And some nights I honestly couldn’t tell you my count’s any lower. That’s,” he opened his hand on the wood, closed it. “That’s my part. She’s not the only one who got the rationed version.”

And then he stopped, and did not look up, and let it sit out on the table at whatever size it actually was, which was more than she had ever watched him do.

The clock on the stove said nine ten. Outside a car went by, wet street, March’s last rain learning to be April’s first.

There were a hundred managing things to say and Dana, who knew every one of them by heart, who could feel them lined up at the front desk with their paperwork ready, said none of them. She sat in the silence her husband had earned, and let it be as long as it actually was, and then she reached the small distance across the table and put her hand on top of his hand, and his hand turned over under hers, palm up, and held on.

“Name one thing,” she said. “That’s the game a man told Nic once. You never let me carry anything, name one thing. Nobody ever wins it. So I’m not going to make you play. I’m going to just,” and her voice did what it did, and she let him see that too, let it happen at the table with the lamp on instead of in a car in a parking lot in another state, “I’m going to start handing you things. Badly. Late. I don’t know how to do it any other way than badly, I’ve been the desk since I was nine years old, you’ve met my mother. But the folder’s the other road, Tom. I’ve read all the way to the end of the other road now. It’s twenty-six letters in a box, and everyone at the table asking about your day, and being in another room.”

“Then hand me one now,” Tom said. “Tonight’s. Whatever’s heaviest.”

And she looked at him, this patient, level man in his Friday sweatshirt, palm up on the table like a dock, and reached for the heaviest thing in the building, and found that it was not the guilt and was not the memorial and was not even the February letter. It was smaller and it was worse and she said it before it could dress itself.

“I don’t know how to stand up there Saturday and I’m afraid that when I open my mouth what comes out will be excellent,” she said. “That’s the thing. Not that I’ll fall apart. That I won’t. That I’ll be excellent, because I’m always excellent, and I’ll cry the right amount and land the funny stories and four hundred, no, two hundred people will file out saying wasn’t she wonderful, and it will be one more Christmas card, the biggest one I ever sent, and she deserves,” her hand tightened on his, “she deserves one true thing said out loud by the one person who owes it, and I don’t know if I’m built to say true things standing up. I’ve never once had to. There’s no evidence I can.”

Tom held her hand and didn’t fix it. That was the entire intervention: he sat there visibly not fixing it, a man declining, with effort, the exact reflex she’d just spent an evening confessing, and letting the weight be a weight, in the open, on the table, between them.

“Then that’s what I’d want,” he said finally. “If I were her. Not excellent. Just true, whatever size it comes out.” He turned her hand over and looked at it like an estimate he was pricing honestly. “For what it’s worth, tonight you were true for two hours straight. In this kitchen. Standing up part of the time.”

“That’s one room.”

“Every venue,” Tom said, “is one room,” and it was such a flatly Tom thing to say, contractor’s metaphysics, delivered deadpan into the wreckage of the evening, that Dana laughed, wet and sudden and entirely against policy, and he grinned the way he did at a bid that had finally penciled, and outside the rain finished becoming April, and they left the wine and went up together past the photographs on the stairs, and she looked at them this time, every one, and named the photographer to him on the landing, out loud, like an introduction: Tom, Nic. Nic, Tom. Four words, sixteen years late, the account, at this one desk at least, beginning, badly and at last, to be itemized.

# Chapter 18

[T]{.dropcap}he second time she stood at the plywood with the phone and her thumb over her sister’s name, she pressed it.

There was no reason, no crisis to walk down from, no parking lot, no quarter-Craig, nothing on fire that a level voice could put out. It was a Saturday, seven days out from the memorial, and Iris was at Marcy’s and Tom was at the store and the house was empty and quiet in the way that had started, this spring, to feel less like relief and more like a room she’d been keeping people out of, and Dana stood in it and called her sister for no reason at all, and it rang, and she realized halfway through the second ring that she did not actually know what she was going to say, because she had never once in her adult life placed this exact call and had no script for it, no ladder, only the wanting.

“Dana?” Kim picked up on two, and her voice came up the line already climbing toward alarm. “What’s wrong. What happened. Is it Mom. Did something happen with Mom.”

“Nothing happened. Everybody’s fine.”

“Then why are you,” and Dana heard her sister actually stop, actually reorganize, heard the specific sound of a person recalibrating around a thing that did not fit the known shape of the world. “You’re calling me. On a Saturday. You never call me. I call you. Why are you calling me, you’re scaring me, just tell me who died.”

And there it was, laid out plainer than any confession, plainer than the drafts, plainer than her mother’s flat hands on the blue-check table: her own sister could not construct a reason for Dana to call her that wasn’t a death. Thirty years of a one-way ladder, and the proof of it came out of Kim’s mouth in four seconds flat, unrehearsed, the reflexive arithmetic of a woman for whom an unprompted call from her sister could only ever mean catastrophe, because closeness, ordinary reasonless closeness, the kind where you just call because you wanted to hear the person, had been so long absent from the account that Kim’s whole nervous system had filed Dana under emergencies-only.

“Nobody died, Kim. I promise. I just.” Dana stood in the empty kitchen and made herself say the true small thing, harder, it turned out, than the whole shoebox had been with Tom, because Tom had asked, and nobody was asking now, she was doing it into open air with no crisis holding the door. “I just wanted to call you. That’s it. I wanted to hear how you actually are. Not the job. You.”

The silence on the line went on long enough that Dana checked the screen to see if the call had dropped.

“Okay, now I’m really scared,” Kim said, but the alarm had gone out of it and something else had come in underneath, something younger, and Dana heard her sister sit down somewhere, heard a chair take her weight. “Who are you and what did you do with my sister. Did you get bad news at the doctor. Are you doing a thing where you go around apologizing to everybody because you got bad news, because I saw a movie about that and I did not care for the ending.”

“I’m not sick.” Dana laughed, and it cracked in the middle, and Kim caught the crack, Kim who had never once in her life missed the tremor under a person’s voice because Kim had never learned to look away from it politely.

“Dana. What is going on with you. Actually.” And now it was Kim doing it, Kim reaching down the ladder, the direction reversed so fast it made Dana dizzy, the mess reaching for the rock, and the thing was that Kim was good at it, was so much better at it than Dana had ever been, had a whole lifetime’s practice at the exact skill Dana had spent a lifetime avoiding, which was asking a person the real question and then just staying on the line through the ugly quiet while they found the nerve to answer it.

So Dana told her. Not the shoebox, not all of it, but the shape, the friend who’d died, the six years of not-answering, the drafts folder in a dead woman’s hand, the thing she’d seen in it that she’d been seeing everywhere since, in her marriage, in her mother’s kitchen, and, she made herself say it, in the nine efficient minutes it had taken her to walk Kim down off the Kroger roof and never once ask her a real question. “I did it to you too,” she said. “I do it to you. You call me and I fix you and I hang up and I feel close to you and I have not actually asked you how you are in I don’t know how long. I’ve been doing to you what she did to me. What I did to her. And you kept calling anyway.”

“Of course I kept calling,” Kim said, like it was the most obvious thing in the world, and maybe to Kim it was. “You’re my sister.”

“That’s not a small thing, Kim. That you kept calling. I don’t think I understood it was a thing you were doing. I thought you just needed stuff. I used to.” Dana closed her eyes. This was the part with a cost in it. “I used to think you were the needy one. I used to be, I don’t know. Superior about it. That I was the one who had it together and you were the one always calling from a parking lot. I had it backwards my whole life and I’m sorry.”

There was a wet sound on the line, and Dana braced for Kim to cry, Kim cried easily, Kim cried at commercials, but it wasn’t quite crying, it was something rougher, and when Kim spoke her voice had gone all the way down to the bottom of itself, past the comedy, to a register Dana had maybe heard twice in her life.

“You want to know the needy one,” Kim said. “You want to know what I actually remember, since we’re doing this, since you called me on a Saturday and broke my whole understanding of the universe. I remember I was six and Dad left and Mom went somewhere behind her own face and didn’t come back for about two years, and I remember I still had a sister for about a week, and then I didn’t. Then I had a little grown-up who made my lunch and got me to school and told me it was fine, everything’s fine, and did all of it perfectly and never cried once, and I was six, Dana, and I was so scared of you. Because you were nine and you’d turned into a thirty-year-old overnight and it meant it was really bad, it meant it was so bad that even Dana wasn’t a kid anymore. I didn’t lose Dad that year. I mean I did. But I lost you more. You went wherever you went to be able to do all that, and you never fully came back either, and I have spent, apparently, the last thirty-six years calling you from parking lots trying to get you to come out of there, and you thought I needed stuff.” Kim laughed, wrecked. “I don’t need stuff. I have never once needed stuff. I need my sister. I’ve needed my sister since I was six and I,” and there the sentence went out from under her, and she took the breath to put it back and the breath came apart too, and what arrived instead was smaller and worse. “I don’t know what I want you to do with that. There isn’t a thing to do with it. Forget it. I don’t know why I said any of that,” and she stopped, and did not start again.

Dana stood in her kitchen and did not say anything, because there was nothing to fix here, nothing to walk anyone down from, only a true enormous thing set on the table between them at last, and for once in her life she did the right thing with it, which was to let it sit there and be true and not reach for a level voice to make it manageable.

“I’m sorry,” she said finally. “I didn’t know I did that. I didn’t know that’s what it looked like from your side.”

“I know you didn’t. That’s almost the worst part, that you didn’t even know you were gone.” Kim blew her nose, honking, unglamorous, entirely herself. “Okay. This is the most we’ve said to each other since your wedding and I’ve decided I can’t do any more of it today or I’ll throw up. But you’re going to call me again. Not when someone dies. This weekend. And you’re going to do it every week, badly, until you’re less bad at it, and I am going to answer every single time even when I’m mad at you, because that is what I do, it is my one skill, I answer the phone.”

“Sunday,” Dana said. “I promise.”

“You said the memorial’s Saturday. Call me after. I want to know you got up there and did it.” Which was their mother’s exact line, their mother’s exact worry, handed down and warmed and made Kim’s, and Dana heard the whole crooked inheritance of it, the three of them, the sealed one and the scraped one and the one who never stopped reaching, and thought that maybe the reaching was the part worth keeping, that of everything that had come down to them out of that scraped-out year, Kim had somehow salvaged the only piece that mattered.

“I’ll call you after,” Dana said. “And this weekend. And I’ll be bad at it.”

“Historically,” Kim said, “yes. But I’m an optimist. It’s my whole problem.” And she hung up before Dana could answer, because Kim always got the last word, had gotten the last word since she was six, and it was, Dana thought, standing in the quiet kitchen with the phone warm in her hand and her face wet and something in her chest cracked open that had been sealed so long she’d forgotten it was a door, one of the ten thousand ordinary things she loved about her sister and had somehow managed, for thirty years, to keep herself too busy to say.

She wrote it on the plywood, in pencil, where the counters would someday cover it over. Call Kim Saturday. And then, under it, smaller: and then again. Not because she’d forget. Because she wanted to see it in her own hand, on a counter that wasn’t finished yet, in a house full of people she still had time to call.

# Chapter 19

[T]{.dropcap}he confession didn’t fix anything. That was the part no one warned her about, and the finding out ran on past Chicago and out the far side of April, so it belongs here in one piece even though most of it happened later: that she had done the hard impossible thing, said the whole of it out loud at the dining room table with her hand finally on his hand, and then Monday had come, and the marriage had to be conducted anyway, in daylight, by two people who had just learned they’d been running it on a summary for sixteen years and had no idea how to run it any other way.

They were both terrible at the other way. That was the spring’s other discovery, underneath the ones about Nic. He’d ask how the day was and she’d feel the front-desk voice come up ready and smooth, fine, handled, the auction’s a machine, and she’d have to catch it by the collar before it carried her off, and stop, and start again, and the starting again was the worst forty seconds of the day, every day, for weeks.

The first time she caught it clean she was at the sink with her back to him, which she had discovered was the only geometry that worked, because the window over the gentle sink did not have a face in it.

“Actually it was bad,” she said. “Julie sent forty texts about the gym floor. Forty. I counted them, because counting them was easier than reading them, and by number thirty-one I wanted to drive into the lake.” She turned the water off. The kitchen got very loud with the water off. “And I cried in the car at lunch about Nic. For no reason. There wasn’t even a reason, there wasn’t a song or a photograph or anything, a woman went past with a stroller and one of those clip-on fans, and that was it, that did it, twenty minutes in the lot behind the practice with a sandwich I didn’t eat.”

Behind her the chair moved. She kept her hands in the water that was no longer running.

“That’s the day,” she said. “I don’t know what you’re supposed to do with it. I don’t know what I’d do with it.”

“You want me to do something with it?”

“No.” It came out fast and it came out true and she was surprised by both. “No. I want it to have been said out loud in this room instead of driven around Westerville in a Honda.”

She turned around then, and he was sitting with his forearms on the table and his reading glasses pushed up into his hair, not moving toward her, a decision he was making, and the two of them stayed like that a second in the counterless kitchen, a woman with wet hands and a man not fixing anything, and it was awful. She could never get anyone to understand that later, on the rare occasions she tried. It cost so much more than the summary ever had. The summary was free.

“Can I say a thing,” Tom said, “and have it be allowed to be the wrong thing.”

“You can say it.”

He put his thumb on the base of the glass and moved it a half inch, the way he moved a level on a sill before he trusted the bubble, buying himself the length of it.

“I liked the summary,” he said.

The furnace came on under the floor.

“I’m not saying I want it back. I want that on the record before you go anywhere with it, because I can see you going somewhere with it already.” He wasn’t looking at her. He was looking at the glass, which was how he looked at estimates. “I’m saying I had sixteen years of coming through that door and hearing everything’s handled, and I believed it, and it was restful, and I built a whole life on top of the restfulness. And then you tell me it was a front desk, and you’re right, it was, and here’s the part I’ve been chewing on since Friday.” He stopped. He picked the glass up and put it down in the same ring. “You take a house down to the studs and you find out what was actually carrying the load. Sometimes it’s the thing you were about to throw in the skip. I don’t know yet which parts of the last sixteen years were holding this up and which parts were just in the way, and I’m not going to know by Thursday, and I need you to not need me to know by Thursday.”

“Okay,” Dana said.

“That’s it.” He looked up. “I’m forty-eight. Nobody taught me the other thing either, and I’m not sure I’d have signed up for the class.”

And Dana stood at the sink with her hands still wet and understood that she had been handed something, badly, in the only language the man had, and that the correct response was not to thank him for it or reassure him about it or, God help her, make a plan around it. She dried her hands on the towel and hung the towel on the oven door and did not straighten it.

“Noted,” she said. “For the record.”

“Noted,” he agreed, and got up and started the dishwasher, which was his version of a change of subject and also, she was learning, of a hand on her back.

He was bad at it in the other direction too, which she hadn’t expected. His great gift and his great flaw were the one thing, that he let things be how they were, and for sixteen years that had meant he let her be the front desk, took the summary, ratified the report, and now she was handing him the raw version and his reflex was still to nod and let it be fine, to reach for the walkway from his own side, well, sounds like a rough one, and go back to the hinge he was oiling or the drawer he was planing true, because a house this old always gave a man something to fix that wasn’t the kitchen, the kitchen being Gary’s jurisdiction and Gary being, that spring, a rumor. She’d watch him do it. She’d watch him take the flayed true thing she’d just paid so much to say and gently set it down and pick the small task back up, and the old Dana would have let him, would have been relieved, would have filed it handled and gone to bed.

The new one, three weeks into this, made herself say it, once, standing at the plywood while he worked a stubborn hinge on the back door. “You did the thing. I just handed you the real one and you said sounds like a rough one and went back to the door. I need you to not do that. I don’t know how to do this if you do that.”

And Tom went still, screwdriver in hand, and she watched sixteen years of the easy way go across his face, and watched him, God bless him, set the screwdriver down on the plywood.

“You’re right. I did do that. I’ve been doing that our whole marriage, it’s the deal, you hand me the summary and I take it, it’s restful, I told you that.” He leaned against the counterless counter. “So we’re both bad at it. Great. Two competent adults, completely incompetent at the one thing.” He almost smiled. “The alternator car.”

“What?”

“Your friend. The alternator car, in the eulogy draft, you read it to me. Shooting out the window at seventy with the seatbelt under her arm. Art doesn’t get pulled over.” He looked at her. “We’re the alternator car. We’ve been driving on it for years because it still goes. It still goes, Dana. But your friend died with twenty-six letters she never sent, and I keep thinking, I don’t want us to be the alternator car anymore. I’d rather we broke down on the shoulder and actually fixed the thing, even if it takes twice as long and it’s a pain and I have to say my feelings out loud like a person in a movie I’d never watch.”

Which was the most he’d said about himself in a year, and it came out in the register of a man describing a mechanical problem, a hand still resting on the door he’d been fixing, not looking at her.

“Okay,” she said. “The shoulder. Both of us. Badly.”

“Badly,” he agreed. “For years, probably.” And then he picked the screwdriver back up, because a man could only stay in the open so long, and this time she let him have it.

The kitchen stayed plywood. Gary did not come, the counters did not come, the plywood she’d made four hundred sandwiches on stayed exactly where it was, and Dana stopped, somewhere in April, waiting for it to be finished before she lived on it. She made the coffee on it. She handed Tom the hard true things across it, badly. She’d miss it a little when it went, which she was almost sure of already, and kept to herself, and went back to the peppers.

# Chapter 20

[T]{.dropcap}he first Sunday of it, two days before Chicago, she ran the loop with Priya at eight, the neighborhood still in its robe, and at mile two, between the culvert and the house with the flamingos, she handed her one.

Not the whole account. The whole account was Tom’s now, and Sunday dinner’s, in its time. What she handed Priya was sized for mile two: that the friend who died was a friend she’d lost years before the dying, the same slow way, the same skipped Tuesdays, and that the clementine night had scared her because she’d heard the same machine running, and that Priya was allowed, from here on out, to call it when she heard the gears. That was all. Twelve sentences, maybe, gasped in the rhythm running enforces, which turned out to be the ideal delivery system for hard things, no eye contact, blood already loud, the body too busy to perform.

Priya ran on beside her for a hundred yards of it, breathing, considering.

“Okay,” she said at last. “Two things. One, I’m sorry about your friend, the real version of it, not the casserole version.” Breath. “Two, if I’m calling it when I hear the gears, you get a word for when I do. A word that means stand down, she’s right, I’m coming out of the binder. Because you’ll argue. You argue when you’re wrong, it’s your tell, it’s why I never play cards with you.”

“What’s the word?”

“Clementine,” Priya said, deadpan, not breaking stride, and Dana laughed so hard she had to stop at the flamingo house and put her hands on her knees, and a man collecting his newspaper watched two middle-aged women in running jackets lose it entirely at seven minutes past eight on a Sunday, and the word entered the treaty, ratified, load-bearing, absurd, which was, Dana was coming to understand, the only kind of clause that ever really held.

Monday at eleven forty she had fifty minutes between the practice and Iris’s orthodontist consult, and she spent them in the car in the school pickup line, early, engine off, and that was where she finally opened it.

Not the text thread. The texts were a lobby and she was done mistaking lobbies for the house. She opened her email, and she typed the address from memory, its own small ambush, that her thumbs still held it after six years, the way feet hold the stairs of a childhood home, nic.adair, the old domain, autocomplete offering it back to her before the third letter like a coat kept at the door. The cursor sat in the empty body of the message, blinking its metronome, and the pickup line idled around her, minivans and exhaust and a crossing guard unfolding her sign, the whole ordinary machinery of eleven forty on a Monday, and Dana looked at the white field for a while and then began to type.

Nic. I read them all, and I owe you

She stopped mid-sentence. Her thumbs just stood down, the way a body stops at a curb it saw before the mind did, and she sat looking at what the sentence was about to be, and understood, with a clarity that arrived gently and would not be argued with, exactly why it could not finish.

Because she could feel how good it was going to be. There was the ambush inside the ambush. The letter was already assembling itself behind her eyes, shapely, true, every clause of it, the envy named, the wall described from her side, the door, the knock, the coffee she’d have made, and it would run three paragraphs and end somewhere honest and devastating, and typing it would feel like the truest hour of her year, and it would be addressed to a server that would swallow it whole or bounce it back, and either way it would be one more letter to a folder. Her folder. Everyone had one, apparently. Hers would just be newer, and it would grow, she knew herself now, it would grow one eloquent unsendable letter at a time, each one a little truer than anything she said to the living, and it would feel like keeping faith, and it would be the exact same room Nic had died in, furnished identically, and the rent was the people at the table.

The dead don’t need drafts, she thought. The dead are past the reach of eloquence. Every word she owed was owed above ground now, and above ground had a door that opened, several, in fact, one of them at this moment walking out of a middle school with a backpack worn on one shoulder in defiance of all orthopedic counsel.

She did not delete the half sentence. She looked at it once more, Nic. I read them all, and I owe you, nine words stopped at the curb, and left it exactly there, unfinished, unsent, one item in a folder of one, not a letter but a marker, the way you leave a stone on a grave, and locked the phone as the passenger door opened and the twenty-first century got in.

“You’re early,” Iris said, suspicious.

“I’m punctual.”

“You’re never this punctual. Is this about the orthodontist? Because Marcy said her cousin said the consult is where they get you. She said it’s all a pipeline.”

“A pipeline.”

“To headgear,” Iris said darkly, buckling in, and Dana pulled out of the line into the noon traffic, and let her daughter’s counsel wash the car, the cousin’s testimony, the known horrors, a boy in eighth grade who allegedly had a device with a key that his mother turned, a key, Mom, like a wind-up toy, like a music box, is that legal, and Dana drove and said mm and is that right in the right places and felt the morning’s white email field recede behind her at a speed that felt, for once, less like escape than like traffic simply moving, the lane going where lanes go, forward, at noon, with a child in it.

The orthodontist’s waiting room had a fish tank, and the fish tank had a diver.

It was the classic diver, plastic, mid-stride on the gravel, helmet, hose, a stream of bubbles issuing from his works, and Iris assessed him from the row of chairs with the flat merciless attention she brought to all institutional decor.

“He’s been walking since we sat down,” she reported. “He’s not getting anywhere. There’s a fake ship right there and he’s facing the wrong way.”

“Maybe he’s been to the ship.”

“He’s twelve inches from the ship, Mom, and he’s committed to the glass. He’s going to walk into the glass forever.” Iris slid down in the chair to tank level, eye to eye with the doomed explorer. “The fish don’t even see him anymore. He’s furniture. This is the saddest thing in Westerville.”

“He’s got air, he’s got a view, he’s got the only job in the tank.”

“He’s got a hose to a plastic lid,” Iris said. “It’s not a life, it’s a screensaver,” and a man across the waiting room laughed into his magazine and pretended he hadn’t, and Iris, warmed by an audience, developed the theme: the diver’s backstory, his estranged pod of fellow divers in better tanks, the ship he refused on principle to enter because entering the ship was what they expected. By the time the assistant called Whitaker, the diver had a name, which was Craig, and a philosophy, which was spite, and the waiting room man had given up all pretense of the magazine.

The consult was the consult, molds and small talk and a treatment plan with phases, and the pipeline claimed its next customer, eighteen months, upper and lower, no headgear, to Iris’s visible and complicated disappointment, a girl robbed of a grievance she’d already furnished.

In the car after, Iris flipped the visor down and examined her doomed teeth in the mirror for a while, and then, out of the blue middle of nowhere, in the voice she used for questions she’d been carrying: “Was your friend’s funeral sad? The first one. The visiting one.”

“Visitation. Yes. Sad and strange.”

“Is the other one going to be sadder? The one Saturday.”

Dana signaled, changed lanes, bought herself the length of it. Three weeks ago she’d have said a smooth true nothing, it’ll be a lot of people who loved her, and the lane would have closed over the subject. She could feel that answer on the shelf, wrapped, ready.

“It might be,” she said instead. “I’m one of the speakers, and I’m nervous about it, because she and I lost all those years, like I told you, and I haven’t figured out how to talk about somebody I loved that much and also let down. That’s the true answer. It’s a strange thing to be carrying around, so, now you know what’s in the bag.”

Iris was quiet a block. Visor still down, not looking at her mother, which was, Dana knew, how twelve receives the important things, at an angle, in a mirror.

“You should tell the pig story,” Iris said finally. “The dissecting one. Did the pig have a name?”

Dana kept her eyes on the road. “Gerald,” she said, and her face did what it did when she said it, and for once she let it.

Iris turned from the mirror. “Like our Gerald?”

“Like our Gerald. You got it right the first time.”

“Then tell that one,” Iris said. “That story’s really good, and it’s yours. Marcy’s grandpa’s funeral was all people saying he was a good man, like, eight people said good man, and nobody said one thing he ever did, and Marcy said it was like he’d never been alive. Tell the thing she did.” She flipped the visor up, case closed, counsel rested. “Also can we get slushies, my mouth tastes like mold of my own teeth, which is a sentence I never want to say again.”

They got slushies. It was forty degrees out and the middle of a Monday and they sat in the parking lot with the heater on drinking frozen sugar, blue for Iris, red for Dana, an indefensible tradition from the kindergarten years revived without either of them proposing it, and Iris narrated the goalkeeper situation’s stunning new developments, and Dana drank her slushie and watched her daughter talk with her whole face, everything sent, everything said. The car was pointed home. Both people in it were saying true things at an angle, in mirrors, over slushies.

# Chapter 21

[S]{.dropcap}he owed her mother a phone call and had owed it for twenty-four days.

She knew the number of days, not by counting but by weight, and she had told Tom everything three nights ago at the dining room table and had walked around since inside a strange thin quiet, like a house the morning after a party finally cleaned up, and something in her had decided, without consulting the rest, that the quiet was portable. That if she could hand Tom the whole shoebox and survive it, forty minutes and no lightning, and had since handed smaller pieces to Kim and Priya and her own daughter and survived those too, she could call her mother and do a smaller version of the same thing. Say one true sentence into the wall phone up in the kitchen with the blue-check chairs. Let it land. Watch what her mother did with it. She had never once in her life watched what her mother did with a thing she actually handed her.
