She waited until Iris was down and Tom was in the basement at his workbench and the house had gone to the particular hum of a Monday ending, and she stood at the plywood with a glass of the wine she wasn’t really drinking, and she dialed the number she’d known before she’d known her own address.

It rang in a kitchen fifty miles north. She could see the phone ringing, screwed to the wall by the calendar, the cord she’d stretched to its limit as a teenager, curled now back to its coil. Two rings. Lorraine did not answer before two, ever, because answering on the first ring told a caller you’d been standing by the phone.

“Ames residence,” her mother said, which was how she answered a phone she’d had thirty years, to callers who were, now, nearly all Dana.

“It’s me, Mom. I’m back. You said call, not text, so.”

“So you called on the twenty-fourth day.” Down the line came the small orchestra of Lorraine’s kitchen, a tap running and shut, the freezer’s hum, a timer somewhere counting down something Lorraine had under control. “I’d about decided you’d been eaten by Chicago. How was it. Did you sleep up there or did you do the thing where you drive back at midnight because a hotel is a waste of a bed you’re barely in.”

“I stayed over. One night.” Which was true, and which she heard herself deliver the way you hand a customs officer a document, the honest thing offered fast and whole so that nothing behind it gets opened, and she thought, three nights out from having told Tom the unedited whole of everything, that the reflex to hand her mother the tidied version was so old it lived below the level where she made decisions, in the sub-basement, running the house lights whether or not anyone was home.

“Good. Good, that’s good.” A cupboard, its particular squeak. “And I suppose,” a small pause where Lorraine chose the word, “there was the apartment. The sister’s. That can’t have been a nice thing, going through a person’s whole apartment. I did your grandmother’s and I don’t wish it on anybody, a life comes down to more bags than you’d think and none of them are the right size.”

And there it was, the door, held open an inch by her mother without her mother knowing, a life comes down to more bags than you’d think, and Dana felt the sub-basement stir, felt the true thing move up toward her mouth, because this was it, this was the opening, her mother had just said something real about grief and Dana could meet it, could say the thing she’d said to no one but Tom and a folder, Mom, I did to Nic what we do, I let her go a quiet Sunday at a time and called it getting busy, and I’ve been so afraid the whole drive up and back that I’m going to do it to everyone, that I already am, that I do it to you.

“It was a lot,” she said. “But it’s handled. Justine had a system. We got through it.”

Handled. We got through it. She heard the words leave her, heard the front-desk voice come out of her own mouth and go down fifty miles of copper into the very kitchen it had been built in, forty years ago, at a table under a rectangle, and she stood at her plywood counter with her wine she wasn’t drinking and listened to herself hand her mother the empty plate and say it was delicious, and she understood, live, in real time, with a clarity that had no comfort anywhere in it, that she was doing it right now, that the doing was not a thing in the past she could feel bad about from a safe distance, it was a muscle firing in her jaw this second, and knowing it did not stop it, knowing it had never once stopped it, the knowing and the doing lived in two different rooms of her and only one of them had a phone.

“Well, that’s you,” Lorraine said, warm, approving, the worst possible thing she could have said and the kindest she knew how. “You get through things. You always have. You could organize a shipwreck.” The timer went off down the line, a small insistent beeping, and Lorraine said “hold on, that’s the,” and set the phone down on the counter with a wooden knock, and Dana stood in her own kitchen listening to her mother’s kitchen with no one in it, the freezer, the far-off oven door, the ordinary sounds of a woman being useful to an empty room, and she thought: this is the recording. If I never call again, this is the last of it, my mother’s kitchen making its small competent noises without her, and it will go on making them, and one day it won’t, and I will have spent the whole of it saying it was handled.

“Sorry,” Lorraine said, back, breathing a little from the trip across the kitchen. “The good bread does not forgive you if you’re late to it. Now. Where were we. You got through the apartment. And how are you, actually, not the report, you sound thin. You sound like you did in February when you weren’t telling me about the mammogram thing.”

Her mother had asked her twice now. A person could go a whole life not seeing this about Lorraine: the reroute was never the whole of it, that underneath the auction tables and the freezer inventory and the you could organize a shipwreck, her mother kept coming back around, quietly, to the one door, how are you actually, not the report, and it was Dana, it had been Dana this whole time, who kept the door shut, who took her own mother’s offered inch and handed back the tidied version, who had been so busy being sure her mother couldn’t receive a real thing that she had never once tested it by giving her one.

Say it, said the sub-basement. She’s asking. She’s asking twice. This is the most she knows how to open the door and you are the one who keeps closing it and calling it her fault.

Dana opened her mouth. The wine glass had gone warm in her hand. In the next silence, the one where the true sentence had all the room it would ever need, she heard her own breath go in to make it, felt the shape of I’m not okay, Mom, I let her go and I’m scared I do it to everybody sitting right there behind her teeth, five years and forty of them, ready.

“I’m all right,” she said. “Tired. It’s a lot of driving. How’s the auction coming, are you still doing the silent tables or did they finally let you run the live one.”

She heard herself do it. She heard herself take her mother’s open door and, gently, with both hands, shut it. She chose the auction. She reached past the true thing and picked up the small manageable thing and set the true thing back down where it had sat her whole life, and she did it knowing, with her eyes open, three nights after proving to herself at a plywood counter that she could do the other, and that was the part that would stay with her, driving north the next morning, that she had known exactly where the door was and had shut it on purpose, because the phone made it easy, because you could hang up a phone, because fifty miles of copper was a safe distance to almost-say a thing across and then not.

“They let me run the live one,” Lorraine said, and off she went, glad, into the tables and the donated week in Gatlinburg nobody wanted and the Hostetlers’ feud over the quilt, and Dana leaned on the plywood and let her mother’s voice fill her kitchen, and it was, God help her, lovely, her mother was funny about the quilt, genuinely funny, dry as a stick about the Hostetlers, and Dana laughed twice for real and meant both, and hated how easy it was to let the real thing go when the small things were this warm, when love came at you in exactly the size you could carry and never once the size that would have cost you something to hold.

They talked eleven more minutes. Iris’s orthodontist consult, which Lorraine had opinions about, having raised two children with what she called perfectly good crooked teeth. The gutters. Whether Dana was eating. At the end Lorraine said, “you call me after the memorial, and I mean the actual day, not three days later, I want to know you got up there and did it and I want to hear it from your voice,” and Dana said she would, and this time she believed she meant it, and her mother said “all right then, go to bed, you sound like the floor,” which was Lorraine for I love you, had always been Lorraine for I love you, the whole dialect was made of weather reports and instructions and you sound like the floor, and Dana said “you too, Mom,” which was Dana for the same, and they hung up.

The kitchen was very quiet. The workbench had gone still in the basement. Somewhere in the house Tom was washing the day off his hands, and upstairs Iris slept with everything sent and nothing held back, twelve years old and still fluent in a language Dana was three generations into forgetting, and Dana stood at the plywood with the warm wine and the dead phone-warmth still in her hand and understood the shape of the thing she’d learned, which was not that her mother couldn’t take it. It was that the phone was the problem. That she had spent her life choosing the version of every hard conversation you could hang up, the text, the call, the drive-home-alone, the fifty safe miles, and that if she was ever going to hand her mother the shoebox and watch what she did with it, really watch, it would have to be in the kitchen with the blue-check chairs, in a room with no receiver to set gently back in its cradle, close enough to see her mother’s hands go flat on the counter and stay there.

She rinsed the glass she hadn’t drunk. She did not write it down, because some things you did not need to write down, you would not be forgetting them, and she turned off the kitchen lights and went down to tell Tom the drawer he’d planed had stopped sticking as badly, which it had, and which was true, and which was not, by a considerable distance, what she had gone down there to say.

# Chapter 22

[S]{.dropcap}he left for Chicago on Tuesday at six thirty in the morning, and this time the whole household did the leaving with her.

That was the difference, and she felt it, unfamiliar and better. Sunday night she had laid it out at the dinner table like a person with nothing to hide, because she was one now, provisionally, on probation with herself: she’d go up Tuesday, help Justine finish the apartment through Thursday, stay for the memorial Saturday, home Sunday. Five days. Tom had offered to come up Friday night with Iris, and Dana had sat with the offer honestly, in front of them, long enough that Iris got interested in the pause, and then said no, and said why: that she needed to stand in that room Saturday as exactly one thing, Nic’s friend, accountable, not flanked, and that she’d tell them everything after, the whole itemized account, and Tom had nodded slowly and said, “Sunday dinner. Full report,” and Iris had said, “Tell the pig story or don’t come home,” and that was the whole vote of the house, and she’d driven out of Westerville in the dark with it riding in the passenger seat where the dread used to sit.

Rain as far as Marysville, then a sky like wet paper. She stopped at the same rest stop outside Findlay out of some animal symmetry and stood with her coffee at the same window and noticed she was a different woman than the last one who’d stood there, four weeks and one demolished story ago, and got back on the road.

The apartment had entered its final stage, the stage where a home tips over into inventory. Half the wall of photographs was down, leaning in padded stacks, the walls behind them stenciled with the ghost rectangles of where they’d hung, brighter paint in the shapes of what was gone. The green chair wore a moving blanket like a horse. Boxes had proliferated and organized, block capitals on every one, and in the middle of it Justine stood with her hair tied up in a pencil, day forty-two, the crispness gone porous now, a woman running on lists and fumes.

They worked. Dana had brought work clothes and her own box cutter, which made Justine laugh for reasons she declined to explain, and they did the negatives closet’s outer strata, the equipment shelf, the terrible cabinet above the fridge that every home maintains for things too useless to keep and too specific to discard. The negatives closet alone was an education: binders by year, sleeves labeled in Nic’s fast slant, and inside them the shadow inventory of a whole career, weddings and portraits interleaved with the personal rolls, ordinary days by the hundred, a city of contact sheets, and Justine ran her thumb down one binder’s spine and said, “Bev says some of this should be in an archive, a real one, a university maybe. My sister. An archive.” She said it the way you test a word for weight. “She’d have hated it and told everyone.”

Bev was coming Thursday with two co-op people and a van for the darkroom equipment and the archive proper, everything cataloged, Bev had a system, Bev had sent a spreadsheet, and Justine spoke of the spreadsheet the way shipwrecked people speak of sails. Ella texted through the morning at intervals, dispatches from a sociology lecture she was visibly not attending, votes on disputed objects, KEEP THE GUILLOTINE ARE YOU INSANE, and around noon a photo of her dorm wall, where the fire-escape geraniums now hung in a cheap frame, with the message, supervising from here.

The gadget drawer’s survivors went into a box marked KITCHEN FINAL, and the guillotine came up again, and Dana said, “I know what it’s for now, for the record,” and told her, the drafts inventory, things i bought instead of calling you, forty dollars, the lying garlic man, someone’s going to have to figure out what it was for, and Justine laughed until she had to sit down on the arm of the blanketed chair, and then wasn’t laughing, and Dana stood in the kitchen doorway and let her not-laugh for as long as it ran, no coat, no detour, just present, which she was learning was also a thing a person could hand someone.

The eruption came at two, and it came by text message, and it was not, in the end, about Dana, and it was about Dana entirely.

Justine’s phone chimed on the counter. She glanced, and went still, and then read it aloud in a voice Dana hadn’t heard from her before, flat as a scraper: “Hi Justine, you don’t know me well, I’m Krista, I knew Nic ages ago through the Fullerton studio. I heard about the celebration Saturday and I was wondering, this is awkward, whether there’s any way to get one of her prints, even a small one, before things are dispersed. She meant a lot to me. No worries at all if not.” Justine set the phone face down with exaggerated care, the care of a person handling something explosive, and looked at the ghost rectangles on the wall.

“She meant a lot to me,” she repeated. “Ages ago. You know what I’ve got in her phone? I’ve got Krista. I looked people up, the first week, I looked everybody up, I was starving, I wanted anyone who’d known her. Last message between them is four years old. It’s Nic asking if she wanted to get lunch. Krista hearted it. Hearted it. That’s the whole reply, that’s the end of the thread, and now she’d like a print before things are dispersed.” Her voice was climbing, finding a register the apartment hadn’t heard from her, maybe from anyone, in weeks of careful lifting. “And she’s the fourth one, Dana. The fourth. Where were,” and her hands came up, taking in the boxes, the stacks, the whole diminished kingdom, “where were all of these people when she was alive and asking them to lunch? She asked. I’ve read the threads, she did the asking, over and over, for years, to all of them, and they hearted it, they thumbs-upped her, and now the room Saturday is going to be full, four hundred people can’t wait to celebrate her, and I keep thinking, if a tenth of you had just answered,” and there the sentence hit the wall it had been driving toward the whole time, and stopped dead, and the silence after it had a shape, and the shape was the woman standing in the kitchen doorway holding a box cutter.

Justine’s face went through it in real time, the arrival, the horror, the scramble. “Dana. I didn’t mean, that wasn’t,”

“You did,” Dana said. “And it’s all right. You’re allowed.”

“You came. You’re here, you’ve been here, you packed her cables, that’s not,”

“Justine.” Dana put the box cutter down on the counter, which felt oddly formal, a witness disarming, and came and sat on the chair’s other blanketed arm, the two of them perched on the covered green chair like people on a seawall. “The last message in my thread is from her too. It’s an invitation. It sat there fourteen months and I never answered it, and then she died, and you called me her best friend, and I let you, because it was the last title I was ever going to get from her and I wanted it.” The ghost rectangles held their brighter paint. “So when you’re doing the arithmetic on the people who hearted her and showed up now, I go in that column. I’ve been in it the whole time. I should have told you in the gym, the first call, and I’ve regretted the gap every day since, and I’m telling you now, four days before I stand up at her memorial, which is terrible timing, and the only timing I’ve got left.”

Justine was quiet for a long time. Below the window the street did its street things, a delivery truck, a dog with opinions.

“I know,” she said finally. “Not the whole shape of it. But I knew her stories about you stopped. Somewhere in there, the Dana stories got a vintage, they were always the same six stories, and she’d tell them like they were last week, and I let myself not notice, because they were good stories and she was happy in them.” She picked a thread off the moving blanket. “And then in her favorites, she never took you out. And I decided that was the truth about you two, the favorites list, and the vintage was just, distance. I decided it, Dana. On purpose. Day two, in her phone, at four in the morning, I needed one thing in that phone to be intact and I picked you.” She looked over. “So. You let me believe it, and I built it myself. That’s the actual paperwork.”

“Was she okay?” Justine asked then, and her voice on the question was Rachel’s voice, Deb’s voice, the whole phone tree’s voice, the question arriving at last at the person it had been looking for all along. “The letters. You’ve read them now. I’ve had one paragraph and twelve days of wondering. Was my sister okay?”

And here it was, the stand, the testimony, the second chance at the only question, and Dana took the breath you take before the true version.

“I don’t know,” she said. “That’s the honest floor of it, and I’m not building anything on top of it this time. What I can tell you is what’s in the letters. She was lonely, and a lot of the loneliness had my name on it, and mostly she blamed herself for it, which is the part I’ll be carrying for a while, because the math was mine. Once, that I found, she blamed me. She typed a whole page of it and then took it back the next morning, and kept both, and I’d like you to know I think the page she took back was the accurate one. And she was also funny, all the way through, the letters are so funny, Justine, there’s a two-page review of my Christmas card, there’s a raccoon census. And the last one, February, isn’t sad at all. It’s a plan. She was coming to see me in April. She’d told Bev, she’d told the plants, she was practicing. Whatever the whole truth of her last years was, and I don’t get to claim I know it, I know how it was pointed at the end. It was pointed at a door. She died seven weeks before knocking on it, and that’s the worst sentence I own now, and it’s also,” Dana looked at the ghost rectangles, the bright shapes of what had hung there, “somehow the one with the hope in it. She wasn’t done. She wasn’t anywhere near done. She was mid-sentence, and the sentence was headed somewhere good.”

Justine cried then, properly, for the first time in Dana’s presence, forty-two days of crispness letting go on the arm of her dead sister’s horse-blanketed chair, and Dana sat beside her on the other arm and did not manage it, did not coat-and-detour it, just stayed, one hand on Justine’s back, in the apartment with the brighter ghosts on the walls, until it had run all the way through.

“Okay,” Justine said at last, wet, wrung, upright. She blew her nose on a paper towel from the KITCHEN FINAL box, which meant unpacking the paper towels, which they both silently agreed to ignore. “Okay. Krista gets a print. A small one. Nic would give her one, she’d give everybody everything, that was half the problem.” She surveyed the kingdom, sniffed, restored herself to the list by force of will. “And you. Tomorrow, when we do the desk. There’s something I’ve been not dealing with, and Ella made me promise I’d stop not dealing with it, and I’ve decided it’s yours to deal with. Fair warning. It’s not letters this time.”

“What is it?”

“Tomorrow,” Justine said, in her block-capital voice, the sticky note made audible, and picked up the tape gun, and the two of them went back to work in the late light, side by side down the long afternoon, packing a life whose walls kept the bright shapes of everything that had been there.

# Chapter 23

[T]{.dropcap}he desk was the last unpacked country in the apartment, and they came to it Wednesday at noon with coffee and the wariness of people approaching a border.

It was a big oak teacher’s desk, bought at a school surplus sale decades ago per Justine, hauled up the stairs by persons unrecorded, and it held the concentrated sediment of a working artist’s whole administration: contact sheets, invoices in their famous arrears, grease pencils, a drawer of batteries in unknowable states of charge, filters in cases, cases without filters, a stapler of visible antiquity, and two decades of the small paper decisions a life runs on. They worked through it drawer by drawer, Justine calling the shots, KEEP, BEV, SHRED, and it was slow in a different way than the cables had been slow, because everything here was handwriting.

In the second drawer down on the left, under a folder of tax paper, there was a bundle held with a wide rubber band, and Dana knew it before her hand finished lifting it, knew the cardstock, the weight, the annual good paper chosen off the same website every October.

The Christmas cards. All of them. Hers, six years of the broadcast, rubber-banded in order, the most recent on top, the kitchen saga, three cheerful sentences about Gary, Iris’s year in review, and beneath it the year before, and the year before that, back and back, each one opened cleanly along the flap, each one kept, filed in a working desk between the taxes and the grease pencils like documents in active use. Which, holding the bundle in the gray window light, was exactly what they had been. Active use. Read aloud on Tuesdays. Performed. Mined for material for a folder of letters. Six years of one-way mail, and here was the other end of the line, the receiving station, maintained, in order, the file of a correspondent who had never once considered the correspondence closed.

She didn’t say anything. Justine, across the desk in the invoices, didn’t see, or saw and let it be. Dana held the bundle a moment longer, and then she did the only thing there was to do with it, which was put it in KEEP, on top, face up, and go on with the drawer, and carry the fact of it in her chest for the rest of the day like a stone warmed in a fist: that in the accounting she had been running for a month, the ledger of who had held on and who had let go, there had been, the entire time, in a desk in Rogers Park, this one more entry on the other side. Kept. All of it kept. Even the broadcast. Even the porch light left on for no one had been, at the far end of the dark, watched for, every December, like a season.

The camera was in the bottom right drawer, the deep one, sitting on a folded chamois like something bedded down.

Justine didn’t reach for it. She sat back in the desk chair instead and let Dana see it first, and Dana knew it at one glance, knew it across twenty-seven years, the black-and-chrome body gone soft-edged at the corners where the brassing showed through, the mechanical one, the one that predated everything, the one Nic had bought half-dead from a pawnshop junior year and resurrected with a repair manual and borrowed tools, her personal camera, always, through every digital generation that earned the rent, this one stayed, this one was for herself. There was tape residue on the base plate from some ancient repair. The strap was newer, the third or fourth strap of its life. And in the little counter window on the top plate, patient as a held breath, a number: 31.

“There’s film in it,” Justine said. Her voice had gone careful. “Thirty-one frames shot. It was on the desk when,” the sentence walked to its edge, and this time she made herself finish it, “when Marta found her, it was here, on the desk, next to the keys. She’d been shooting it that week. Ella figured out the counter thing, I don’t know cameras. Thirty-one out of thirty-six.”

Dana looked at the number in its little window and felt the whole apartment reorganize itself around five unshot frames.

“Ella wanted it developed the first week,” Justine went on. “I said no. Then I said soon. Then I stopped saying anything, and it’s been in that drawer, and every time I open the drawer I close it again, and Ella finally sat me down Sunday, my nineteen-year-old sat me down, and said, you have to either deal with it or give it to someone who can, that’s what the obituary asked everybody else to do, Mom, shoot a roll of an ordinary day and actually get it developed, and hers is sitting in a drawer.” Justine’s mouth did something between a smile and its opposite. “Actually get it developed. Her own line. The kid deployed her own aunt’s obituary against me. There’s no defense against that, there’s no counter to it.”

“Why can’t you?” Dana asked, gently, because it wasn’t a challenge, it was the question the room required.

Justine was quiet a moment, hands around her coffee.

“Because it’s the last roll,” she said. “Right now it can be anything. It’s Schrödinger’s week, it’s whatever I need it to be on a given night. The last things she looked at. And the day the prints come back, it’s thirty-one actual pictures, and some of them will be out of focus, and some will be the inside of her lens cap, and it will be a Tuesday, an ordinary Tuesday of an ordinary week, because that’s what she shot, that’s all she ever shot for herself, and I will be holding the actual last week, fixed, finite, done, and I,” she stopped, and set the coffee down, and looked at the camera in its drawer. “I can plan a memorial for four hundred people. I’ve discovered I can do almost anything if it has a checklist. This doesn’t have a checklist. This has an envelope, and a counter, and me opening it alone in a parking lot, and I know my limits now, day forty-three, I’ve met them all. This one’s past the fence.”

The radiator ticked. Down on the street, the delivery truck again, or its cousin.

“So it’s yours,” Justine said, and picked the camera up in both hands, carefully, and held it out across the desk. “That’s the thing from tomorrow that’s now the thing from today. Ella agrees, I asked her first, she said, and I quote, obviously Dana, she has the college eyes, whatever that means, she’s nineteen, half of what she says is in a language they invented last year. Take it to a lab, there’s one she used, Bev will know it. Develop the roll. Look at it before I do, that’s the actual favor. Look at it first, and then tell me what it is, so that when I open the envelope I already know what week I’m holding. You did it with the letters. You went first into the letters and came back and told me what country it was, and it turned out I could live there. So go first again. That’s the job. It’s the last job, and then I swear we’re even, and I’ll just be someone you know in Evanston who owes you forever.”

The camera crossed the desk.

It was heavier than it looked, they always were, that generation, brass and glass and no apologies, and it was, as her hands closed around it, the single most intimate object she had ever been handed, more than the letters, because the letters were addressed to her and this was addressed to no one, this was Nic’s own eye, the last weeks of it, pointed at whatever an ordinary week looked like to a woman practicing a knock, and the strap still held the faint shape of a shoulder, and the counter said 31, and five frames waited in the dark of it, unexposed, five frames of nothing, of never, the week’s sentence broken off like a reply in a pickup line, and Dana held it and could not, for a moment, say anything at all.

“Okay,” she managed.

“Okay,” Justine said, and cleared her throat, and reached for the tape gun, restored to jurisdiction, and then paused, struck by logistics, her native tongue. “The lab might need a couple days, so go tomorrow morning, Bev’s coming at nine, she’ll give you the name. If it’s ready Friday you’ll have,” she counted, and Dana watched her arrive at it, at the shape of the calendar, and elect, with visible effort, to say it plainly, “you’ll have them before Saturday. Which is either terrible planning or exactly right, and I’ve stopped being able to tell the difference, I think that might just be what this month is. Terrible and exactly right, every single day, no gaps.”

They finished the desk by four. SHRED filled two bags, BEV filled a crate, KEEP fit, in the end, in one shoebox, which Justine sat looking at for a moment, a person confronting the compression ratio of a life, and then carried to the car without comment. Dana stayed to break down the last boxes, and so she was alone in the apartment for a quarter hour at the end of the light, just her and Susan and the ghost rectangles and the blanketed chair, the rooms gone hollow, every footstep suddenly audible, the home already halfway to being a floor plan.

She took the camera to the big front window, where the street light was going gold, soup light, she thought, and stood holding it, not raising it, just holding it in both hands at the window of the emptied apartment, and looked down at the little counter a last time, thirty-one, the held breath, and then out at the street, the parked cars, the ginkgo trying its first green against its better judgment, the ordinary day going on down there in full ignorance of its own worth, exactly the way the last one had. Tomorrow, the lab. Friday, the envelope. She would go first. That was the job. Into the letters, into the last week, into whatever waited at frame thirty-one, and back out, carrying the report: what country it is. Whether a person could live there.

She put the camera in her bag, wrapped in the chamois, next to a folded page in quarters she had stopped pretending she didn’t know was still in there, and turned off the lamp, and told Susan to hold the fort, out loud, alone, in the dark apartment, in a voice that did not entirely hold, and locked a dead woman’s door behind her with the spare key that lived, now and for four more days, in the pocket of a friend.

# Chapter 24

[B]{.dropcap}ev arrived at nine with a van, two co-op people, and the energy of a woman liberating a museum ahead of a flood.

The co-op people were a silent young man named Theo who could carry an enlarger alone and knew it, and Raymond, who turned out to be seventy and built like a door, and who greeted Dana by looking at her for a moment and saying, “The cards,” in a voice like gravel settling: an introduction and a eulogy of its own. Bev had the spreadsheet printed and sleeved. Every binder of negatives had a row. The darkroom equipment had rows. Bev moved through the diminished apartment assigning numbers to grief, and Justine followed her with the checklist devotion of the rescued, and for two hours the five of them, six for the first hour, when Marta came across the hall with coffee and stayed to wrap glassware before she went home to her plants, turned the last of a home into a manifest.

Raymond worked beside Dana on the enlarger’s disassembly for a while, wordless, handing her the right screwdriver before she knew she needed it, and at some point, apropos of a column and not looking at her, he said, “December one. The kitchen one. She did your contractor as a ghost. Haunting the counters.” He fitted a lens into its case with fingers like dowels. “Best Tuesday of the winter.” The whole speech, the entire address, and Dana took it, into the general ledger, and handed him the next screw, and they went on working, and she understood that she had just attended a second, smaller memorial, congregation of two, order of service: one sentence, held wrench, amen.

With the van loaded, it was Bev who wrote the lab’s name for her, on the back of a co-op card, in a darkroom hand, all caps. “Ask for Dominic,” she said. “Tell him it’s Nic’s roll. He’ll know what that means. He processed her personal work for twenty years, he’ll want to do this one himself.” She held onto the card an extra half-second as Dana took it, the red beads swinging. “You’re the one taking it in.”

“Justine asked me to.”

“Good,” Bev said, and looked at her steadily through the glasses, a woman who had spent fifty years judging prints in low light and knew what she was looking at, and whatever she saw or knew or had assembled from six years of Christmas cards and one receiving line, all she added was: “Go before noon. He closes early Thursdays.”

The lab was a storefront on a commercial stretch, wedged between a taquería and a shipping store, its window full of faded film boxes arranged like a shrine to an argument the century had mostly lost. Inside it smelled of chemistry and warm machines. Dominic was younger than Dana expected, fifties, glasses pushed up on his head, and when she said it was Nic’s roll he stopped moving entirely for a moment, one hand flat on the counter.

“From the camera,” he said. “The FM.”

“It was in her desk. There’s thirty-one on the counter.”

He took the camera from her with both hands, and turned away from her, politely, to do the unloading, a small mechanic courtesy, the way you’d turn from someone to close a coffin lid, and worked the rewind with a practiced touch, and cranked it slow, listening to it, and brought the little cassette up out of the body and stood holding it in his palm.

“I did her first rolls off this camera,” he said. “She used to come in before she could afford it and I’d let her run a tab. Twenty-some years.” He set the cassette down on the counter between them, gently, a period at the end of something. “Tomorrow, end of day. I’ll print the contacts and a set of fives. I do it myself, nobody else touches it. No charge, and don’t argue with me, I’ve been waiting a month for someone to bring me this and I’d have gone to the desk and gotten it myself in another week.”

“Can I ask you something?” Dana said. “The last five frames. The blank ones. Do they,” she didn’t know what she was asking, and asked it anyway. “What happens to them?”

Dominic looked at her a moment. “Nothing. They come back clear. End of the strip’s just clear film.” He picked the cassette back up, weighed it. “You’ll see it on the contact sheet. Thirty-one squares and then five empty ones. First time it happens to you, it’s,” he chose the word with a craftsman’s care, “it’s a lot. So now you know it’s coming.” He wrote her a claim ticket by hand, tore it, slid it over. “Four o’clock tomorrow. Tell Justine the co-op’s covering the memorial prints too, Bev’s order, it’s all one job to us.”

Dana walked out into the noon light with a claim ticket in her wallet where the whole last week of a person’s seeing was now itemized, one line, one name, and stood on the sidewalk between the taquería and the shipping store while the street went by, and then she went and bought two tacos and ate them at the counter in the window, because it was noon and she was a body, and the tacos were outstanding, and she texted a photo of them to Tom and Iris with no caption, and Iris replied in nine seconds, TRAITOR. bring some home, and Tom replied, that’s her telling you she misses you, and then, a minute later, me too, and Dana sat in the taquería window holding her phone, itemized, received, and the afternoon stood in front of her with nothing in it but the notebook.

The hotel notebook was the problem she’d been walking around for a month, and she gave it the whole afternoon, at the little desk by the window on the fourth floor, with the lake out there past the rooftops in its flat spring silver.

She worked, in sessions, with rules. Phone in the drawer. Coffee from the lobby at the top of each hour, the walk down and back a mandated parole. She had brought the legal pad from home and the hotel provided its own thin pad besides, and by three o’clock both had grown their archaeology, layers, strikethroughs, margins full of arrows pointing at sentences that had seemed load-bearing an hour before and now stood condemned.

Attempt: the antiques, once more, with feeling. She read it aloud to the empty room and heard the Christmas card in it before the second paragraph.

Attempt: start with the obituary line, the roll of film of an ordinary day, build outward. Better. Truer to the room. And by the third paragraph it had become an essay, graceful, about photography and attention, the kind of thing that gets called moving by people checking their coats, and she drew a line through it.

Attempt: the letters themselves, quoted. She got one sentence of Nic’s onto the page, i’ve known your punctuation since we were eighteen, and stopped, and sat looking at it a long time, and then struck it too, not because it didn’t work but because it did, because it would bring the room to its knees, and the letters were not material. That was a line she found she had, discovered it the way you discover a wall in the dark, by walking into it: the folder was a sovereign nation, its own author had said so, and she was its only citizen now, and citizenship meant you didn’t strip-mine it for a strong second act. What was hers to spend was what had happened to her. What had happened to Nic stayed Nic’s.

Attempt: Iris’s compass, tell the thing she did, and this one ran, the pig, the goggles, the papal decree, two-thirds of a page in one pour, alive, hers, and then arrived at the cliff every road arrived at, the place where the story of what Nic did had to hand off to the story of what Dana didn’t, and stood there, and she capped the pen and called Justine.

“I need to ask you a terrible producer question,” she said. “Saturday. What do you want from me up there? Give me the brief. I’ll do whatever the brief is.”

There was a pause, and the sound of Justine setting something down, and when she answered her voice had the flat clean tiredness of the far side of Wednesday. “Tell them who she was,” she said. “That’s the whole brief. I’ve got a caterer for the food and a slideshow for the chronology and Bev for the art. Nobody in that room has the person, Dana. The room’s going to be full of people who each had a window on her, and every window’s real, and I need one person to stand up and be the door.” A breath. “No pressure. Two o’clock. Wear layers, the gallery runs cold till it fills up.”

Be the door. Dana thanked her and hung up and sat at the little desk while the light went long over the rooftops, and looked at the notebook’s strikethroughs, the antiques, the essay, the pour that stopped at the cliff, and understood, with the calm of a woman finally reading her own claim ticket, that the brief could not be met by any version she was able to write in advance. Everything writable in advance was a window. She could build windows all night, beautiful ones. The door was the thing she’d been carrying folded in quarters for two weeks, the thing that couldn’t be polished because its whole load was that it wasn’t, and the decision she kept deferring to the podium was not, she saw now, a decision about material. She had run out of drafts to hide in. Outside the window the lake held its silver until the sun let it go, and she ordered soup from the place downstairs, and slept, to her own astonishment, like a stone.

# Chapter 25

[T]{.dropcap}hat morning, before the lab, across the hall from where they were turning a home into a manifest, Marta’s door stood open, and Dana went through it to get away from the numbers.

That was the honest reason, though she had told Bev she was carrying the last box of kitchen glasses to Marta’s to be dealt with later, and she was, the box was real, glasses wrapped in newsprint that would print the day’s headlines backward onto Nic’s water glasses in the damp. But the true reason was the spreadsheet. Bev moved through 2B assigning row numbers to a life, binder 14, enlarger, the good tripod, and it was necessary, it was even a kind of love, Bev’s love came sleeved and printed, but Dana had been in that apartment ninety minutes watching grief get itemized by a woman who was extremely good at exactly the thing Dana was extremely good at, and she’d needed, suddenly and badly, to stand in a room where nobody was counting.

Marta’s apartment was the same shape as Nic’s and nothing like it. It was warm in the animal way of a place fully lived in, doilies and a recliner with a permanent dent and forty years of accumulation that no manifest would ever get its arms around, and it was, Dana saw, stopping in the doorway with her box, full of plants. Nic’s plants. A whole refugee population of them, on every sill and the top of the television and a card table dragged over to the good window, ferns and a jade gone leggy and a violet in a chipped mug and something enormous and tropical that took up its own corner like a lodger, all of them Nic’s, taken in the first week so they wouldn’t die alone in an apartment with nobody breathing near them, and Marta was at the card table with a watering can shaped like a swan, going down the row.

“Set it anywhere,” Marta said, not turning. “The glasses. Anywhere’s fine, I’ll do them tomorrow. You looked like you needed to not be over there.”

“Is it that obvious.”

“You’ve got the face.” Marta moved the swan to the jade. She was a broad soft woman of maybe seventy with reading glasses pushed up into gray hair and a cardigan that had been through everything, and there was a dog, Dana registered, a low brown dog of no particular committee’s design, asleep in a patch of sun with the total confidence of an animal that had never once been asked to leave a room. “Everybody who comes out of that apartment has the face. Bev’s very good at what she does and what she does is hard to watch. Sit. There’s coffee, it’s the bad kind, I’m not apologizing.”

Dana sat. The dog opened one eye, assessed her, found her adequate, closed it. She put her hands around the bad coffee and did not drink it, and Marta finished the row of plants and set the swan down and lowered herself into the recliner with the small sound a body makes at seventy, and for a while neither of them said anything, and it was the first silence in three weeks that Dana hadn’t felt she was supposed to be doing something inside of.

“That’s Susan, technically,” Marta said, nodding at the tropical lodger in the corner. “Or Susan’s the one still over there, I forget which she named which. She named them all. There’s a whole civil service.” She said it without the catch that every one of them, Justine, Bev, Ella, hit when they reached Nic in a sentence, said it flat and easy, and Dana understood she was in the presence of a different kind of griever, one who had not yet been told, or had declined to hear, that there was a correct way to do it. “I take Susan Sunday. Then they’re all here. Then in June the co-op wants them for the show, out on the fire escape, the geranium thing she always did, so they’ll go visit and come back. I told Bev they come back. I’ve grown attached, is the truth of it. I never was a plant person. I’m a plant person now. Life does that, it hands you somebody’s ferns.”

“It’s kind of you. Taking them.”

“It isn’t kindness.” Marta said it mildly, correcting a small factual error. “Kindness is a thing you decide to do. This I just did, the way you do the next thing. She missed a shoot on a Wednesday. She never missed. The studio started calling around, and I had the key, I’d had it for years, for the plants and the packages, and so I went over.” She looked at the swan watering can on the table. “And I’m the one who went over. That’s not a thing anyone gave me or a thing I earned, it’s just what happened, I had the key and it was Wednesday and I went over, and now I’m the woman who found her, that’s mine now, nobody can have it and nobody would want it, and I’ve stopped waiting for somebody to tell me where I’m supposed to stand with it.”

Dana held the bad coffee and did not move.

“Because there’s no place to stand, is the thing,” Marta went on, not to Dana especially, to the room, to the plants, to whoever was listening in a warm apartment on a Thursday. “At the visitation I didn’t know where to go. Not the family, obviously, I’m not family. Not the photography people, God, no, I don’t know an f-stop from a bus stop. Not the wedding girls. There’s no line for the neighbor. There’s no card that comes in the mail for the neighbor. The neighbor’s not in the obituary and the neighbor doesn’t get the casserole, the neighbor brings the casserole, and I stood in the back of that funeral home holding my own coat like a stranger, and I’m the one who watered her plants for nine years and I’m the one who found her, and there’s no seat for that, there’s no name for that, it doesn’t come with a seat.” She shrugged, a small settling of the cardigan. “So you stop waiting for the seat. You just do the grieving standing up, where you are, with a watering can. It counts. Nobody tells you it counts, so I’m telling you, in case you’re another one who’s waiting to be told. It counts.”

The dog sighed in its sun. Somewhere across the hall a man Dana didn’t know called out a binder number to a woman with a checklist. And Dana sat in a stranger’s recliner-adjacent chair holding coffee she wasn’t drinking and felt something come loose in her chest that had been clenched so long she’d stopped feeling it as clenched. Marta had a key and nine years of watering. Dana had a folder and six years of silence and a whole decade before that she’d let slide, and neither of them had a chair.

“I let her go,” Dana said.

She had not planned to say it. It came out of her in Marta’s warm apartment the way it had not come out of her in her own mother’s kitchen, and afterward she’d think it was the watering can that did it, the swan-shaped watering can and the low brown dog and the woman who wasn’t apologizing for the bad coffee, a room with nothing in it that was keeping score.

“We were close in college. Best friends. And then I just, over years, I stopped answering. Not on purpose. That’s what I keep saying, not on purpose, like it’s a defense. She’d reach out and I’d be busy and I let it thin out until it was gone, and I was the one who let it go, and I’ve spent a month letting a whole city believe I was her best friend, standing where you don’t get to stand, taking the seat, and I don’t have a key. I don’t have nine years of anything. I have a hole where fifteen years should be and everybody keeps handing me the front row.”

Marta looked at her for a moment over the tops of the reading glasses that had come back down onto her nose, and she did not gasp and did not reassure and did not do a single one of the things Dana had braced for, the it wasn’t your fault, the I’m sure she knew, the currency the last three weeks had been minted in.

“Well,” Marta said, and it was so exactly her mother’s well, the whole Midwest’s well, the word that held everything a person wasn’t going to unpack out loud, that Dana nearly laughed. “You came, though. Six-hour drive. You wrote the words. You’ve been carrying boxes for a woman you feel like you failed, in a city that isn’t yours, since Tuesday.” She reached over and, without any fuss about it, filled the swan again from the pitcher by the chair, an old woman keeping her hands busy through a hard thing. “I’m not going to tell you it wasn’t your fault, because I don’t know, maybe some of it was, people let people go, it’s the commonest thing in the world and it’s a real thing to have done and I’m not going to wave it off to make you feel better. But I’ll tell you what I decided about the seat, since you’re in the same fix I’m in and nobody’s told you either.” She set the swan down. “There’s no seat for you either. Best friend from before who stopped answering, that’s not in the obituary and that doesn’t get a card, that’s another one they didn’t build a chair for. So you’re going to have to do what I did. Stop waiting for the chair. Grieve her standing up, where you are, with what you’ve got, which is a lot of guilt and a six-hour drive and a good eulogy, and let it count, because it does, and because the only other option is to keep performing a seat you don’t believe you’ve earned, and honey, I’ve watched you all week, that’s going to kill you a lot slower and a lot worse than just standing up and grieving her like the rest of us who don’t have a place to put it.”

Dana put the coffee down and pressed the heels of her hands to her eyes, once, hard, and took them away, and did not fall apart, because that wasn’t the shape of it, the shape of it was smaller and steadier than that, the shape of it was a fist opening in a warm room.

“The bad coffee’s gone cold,” Marta observed. “I’ll make more of the bad coffee. You don’t have to go back over there for a while. Bev’s got it. Bev’s always got it, that’s Bev’s whole seat, the one she built herself out of a spreadsheet, we all do it, we all build the chair we can build.” She was up now, at the counter, running water, the dog rising to supervise, and she talked over the tap without turning around. “Take a plant, when you go. Not Susan, Susan’s spoken for and she’s a handful anyway. But there’s a spider plant put out about nine babies, you take one home to Ohio. That’s a real thing you can do. That’s a seat you can build. You keep one of her plants alive all the way down there and you talk to it like an idiot, and that’s grief, that counts more than any front row, and it doesn’t ask anybody’s permission.”

When the van was loaded, Justine stood in the doorway of 2B a moment with her hand on the light switch, and didn’t perform anything either. The apartment was down to Susan, the blanketed chair, and the ghost rectangles. “Marta takes Susan Sunday,” she said, to the room more than to anyone. “End of an administration.” She turned the light off, then back on, then off, an old habit from some childhood hallway maybe, checking that off was off, and closed the door, and on the landing Marta hugged her, and hugged Dana too, without ranking them, its own quiet ruling from the neighbor who had known everything first.

So Dana took a plant. A spider plant baby, one of nine, rootbound in a yogurt cup with a coffee filter over the drainage, engineered by Marta with a rubber band for the drive, and it rode home to Ohio three hundred and fifty miles on the passenger seat where the dread had ridden up and the photographs had ridden back and her mother’s soup had ridden south, and she did talk to it like an idiot, somewhere around Toledo, out loud, in broad daylight, feeling like a fool and doing it anyway, telling a spider plant in a yogurt cup about a woman it had lived beside and never met.

# Chapter 26

[F]{.dropcap}riday she cleaned the hotel room she wasn’t leaving, called the practice, confirmed Denny’s final count for an auction that suddenly existed again on the far side of tomorrow, and ran the lakefront path north for an hour in a wind that hadn’t heard about April.

The lake was doing its enormous gray indifference on her right the whole way, and she ran into the wind going out so it would carry her home, Priya’s law, and somewhere past the boulders she found the gait where the body takes over the accounting and the mind rides, and she let the mind ride. Tomorrow at two she would stand up in front of a full room with a pocket over each lung. Tonight there was an envelope with a week in it. Between the two stood only this, the path, the wind, the runners coming the other way with their nods, the whole broad daylight machinery of people maintaining themselves against the odds, and she ran it out to the turnaround and stood a minute at the rail with her heart loud, looking at the water Nic had lived beside for twenty years and photographed, Dana would have bet anything, never once, on the grounds that everyone did. Then the wind carried her home, as promised.

She was standing outside the lab at three fifty-five like a person meeting a train.

Dominic turned the sign at four and let her in and locked the door behind her, which she understood. On the counter, squared to its edges, lay a yellow-and-black envelope, and on top of it a contact sheet in a clear sleeve, and he stood behind the counter with his glasses down for once and said, “I looked. I had to look, it’s my looking that makes the prints. So I’ll tell you the one thing, and then I’m going in the back to clean tanks that don’t need it, and you take all the time in there you want.” He touched the corner of the sleeve, once. “It’s a good roll. That’s the thing. It’s a good week.”

Then he went in the back, and Dana stood alone in the shop with the machines humming, and slid the contact sheet out into the light.

Thirty-one small squares, six rows, and then the five clear ones at the end like windows with the shades up on an empty room, and she made herself not look at those yet, start at frame one, oldest first, the sticky note’s law, and she bent over the sheet the way she’d seen Nic bend over sheets a hundred years ago, and read the last week of her friend’s seeing, square by square.

The window with the morning in it, the radiator’s edge, a mug on the sill. Steam off the mug, backlit; she’d waited for the steam. The el platform under weather, one commuter’s umbrella turned inside out and the commuter mid-laugh about it, caught, seen. A produce stall, the grocer’s hands around oranges. Clementines, Dana thought, and had to straighten up for a second and hold the counter’s edge before she could bend back down. Marta’s dog on the landing, ears at attention, absurd, noble. Susan, three frames of Susan, the new leaf against the window light, unrolling its slow flag. A laundromat’s window at dusk, the machines lit up like aquariums. Two kids on one bike, the second standing on the pegs, arms out, king of a two-block world. A crosswalk. A kid’s mitten on a fence post, set there by some stranger the way the whole city agreed to do it, the small municipal kindness, photographed like a monument. The fire escape, twice: once bare in flat light, and once, frame twenty-two, in late sun with the light coming through the rail’s shadow in long soup-gold bars, the geranium pots still empty, waiting for the season, everything waiting for the season, and it was so plainly beautiful, so plainly the keeper, that Dana’s thumb stopped under it of its own accord. A diner counter, an old man’s coffee, his hands, and Dana recognized the hands, the knuckle biographies, the same man or his brother from the print that had hung on the apartment wall for years, a subject returned to, a correspondence of its own, decades long, conducted entirely in light. A shop window with the street reflected in it, and in the reflection, small, incidental, at the edge of frame thirty, a woman with a camera, gray-haired, half turned away, present the way the photographer is always present in a window, unavoidable, unemphasized, a signature in the corner of a canvas, the only picture of Nic on the roll and it was an accident and it wasn’t, nothing on any roll of hers had been an accident in decades.

And then frame thirty-one.

The bookshelf. Shot close, square-on, the composition locked and level, no accident in any millimeter of it: the spines soft in shallow focus at the edges, the little dish of lens caps, the beach stone, and centered, sharp, filling the middle of the frame, a framed photograph, and the photograph in the photograph was two girls at twenty-three, arms around necks, squinting into a sun two decades gone.

The lake picture. Her own copy of it was in a duffel pocket eleven blocks away. Nic’s copy had been on that shelf, framed, all this time, at eye level, in the middle of the wall Dana had stood two feet from on a gray Wednesday in March and registered as a category. Books, spines, clutter. She had knelt below it and sorted cables all afternoon. And in the last week of her life, a week and a half after typing a letter about knocking on a door, Nic had put the mechanical camera to her eye in her own living room and framed her friend’s photograph on her shelf, deliberately, in good light, and pressed the shutter, and advanced the film, and set the camera on the desk next to her keys.

Frame thirty-one of thirty-six. And then the five clear squares, the shades up, the room empty, the week’s sentence stopped mid-word.

There was one wooden chair by the lab’s window, for waiting, and Dana sat down on it with the contact sheet in both hands and did not perform anything for anyone, because there was no one, Dominic’s tanks clanking faithfully somewhere in the back, the machines humming their patient chemical hum, the street going by beyond the faded film boxes, and she sat in the wooden chair in the last light of the first Friday of April and looked at frame thirty-one until it blurred, and pressed the back of her wrist against her mouth, and rocked once, twice, small, an inch of motion, the body finding some old rhythm it kept for exactly this and had never once been allowed to use, and the sound she made was not large, and she did not stop it, and the machines hummed, and nobody came, a man cleaning clean tanks at four twenty on a Friday so that a stranger could come apart in his shop in peace.

After, she washed her face in the little bathroom with its safelight bulb, and bought, over Dominic’s objections, two eight-by-tens to be printed for Sunday pickup by the co-op’s runner, frame twenty-two and frame thirty-one, the fire escape and the shelf, and stood at the counter while he wrote the order with ceremony in a ledger that predated every machine in the room.

“Can I ask what it was,” he said, not looking up. “Thirty-one. I looked at it an hour, I couldn’t place the photo in the photo.”

“It’s us,” Dana said. “Her and me. We were twenty-three.” The words came out level and astonished her. “I’m the friend.”

Dominic finished the line in the ledger, and put the pen down, and looked at her, glasses down, a craftsman confirming a print against its negative.

“I know,” he said. “She talked about you. You match the stories.” He slid the envelope across. “Go call the sister. She’s been waiting on this a month, whether she knows it or not.”

Dana called Justine from the car, parked under the ginkgos in the last of the light, the envelope on the passenger seat, and Justine picked up on the first ring, and said nothing, because she knew what the call was, and waited, and Dana looked through the windshield at the street Nic had walked with a camera every week for twenty years and gave her report, the job, the whole job, done as promised, go first and come back and say what country it is.

“It’s an ordinary week,” she said. “That’s the country. It’s the mug and the plant and Marta’s dog and the market and the fire escape when the light comes through. It’s the exact thing she asked everyone for in the obituary, she was already doing it, she never stopped doing it. There’s one frame of her, by accident on purpose, in a shop window, looking like herself. Nobody in it is sad, Justine. It’s a week in the life of a woman paying attention.” She looked at the envelope. Her voice held because it had somewhere to stand now. “And the last frame is for me, and I’ll show you tomorrow, after. It’s the best photograph I’ve ever seen in my life, and you can live there. I promise you can live there. I’m going to.”

Justine was quiet on the line a moment, and when she spoke it was thick and steady at once. “Okay,” she said. “Okay. Tomorrow, after.” And then, because she was Justine: “Eat something that isn’t from a lobby. That’s the whole rest of my speech.”

Dana ate at the taquería, at the window counter, the same stool, because ritual was ritual and the tacos had earned tenure, and then she called home from the hotel with the envelope on the desk beside her, and Tom picked up with dish noise behind him and Iris shouting a question about cleats from another room, the whole ordinary orchestra tuning, and Dana said, “Item one,” and handed it to him. The lab. The wooden chair. Frame thirty-one, the shelf, the two girls at twenty-three, the five clear squares at the end of the strip. She did it badly, exactly as promised, out of order, twice interrupted by her own breathing, and Tom stayed on the line through all of it and didn’t fix any of it, and when she was done he was quiet a second and then said, “She kept you on the shelf,” not a question, just the finding restated, the way you repeat a number back to be sure you wrote it down right, and Dana, four floors above a city that had been holding her friend’s last week in a drawer all spring, said yes. The item was this. She kept me on the shelf. And Iris’s voice arrived distantly demanding to know if Mom had said anything about the cleats, and Tom said, muffled, one minute, and then to Dana, unmuffled, “Tomorrow you’ll be great. Scratch that. Tomorrow you’ll be true, whatever size it comes out,” and she slept with the envelope on the nightstand, the way you sleep the night before weather, and the lake out there held the city’s lights along its hem, and kept, as ever, its own counsel.

# Chapter 27

[T]{.dropcap}he gallery on Clark ran cold till it filled up, exactly as advertised, and at eleven in the morning it was cold and full of folding chairs and problems.

Her phone had started the day at seven with the household’s send-off, staggered. Tom: true, whatever size. call after. Iris, at seven forty, with the punctuation of state business: tell the pig story. this is not a suggestion. And then, at eight, a third text, Priya: clementine. (preemptive. for the podium. you’ll know when.) Dana had sat on the edge of the hotel bed with all three on the screen at once, her whole small nation reporting in, and had worn, in the end, both layers, exactly as advised by everyone advising.

She had come early because Justine had texted at seven, you don’t have to come early, which in the language they now shared meant come early, and so she was there when the slideshow refused the projector, Memory Weaver declining, with corporate serenity, to speak to any cable in the building. She was there when the chair count came up short and Theo was dispatched to the co-op for two more racks. She was there when Aunt Lou arrived, ninety-one, magnificent, borne in by two grandsons and a walker with tennis balls on its feet, carrying, against every instruction issued by every relative through every channel for two weeks, a casserole.

“It’s catered, Aunt Lou,” Justine said, with the gentleness of a woman on her fourth deployment of the sentence.

“I heard you the first three times.” Aunt Lou surrendered her coat but not the dish. “Catering is for the room. This is for the family. There’s a difference, and when you’re my age you’ll know it, put it in the back where the trays are and don’t let those men touch it till after.” She surveyed the gallery, the white walls, the folding chairs, the big prints Bev’s crew had hung the day before, Nic’s work at Nic’s scale for once, the fire escapes and the hands and the collapsing wedding parties, and her chin trembled once and was governed. “Well,” she said. “She’d say it was too much fuss and she’d count the house twice. Where’s my seat. I want to see the door.”

The morning organized itself around such deployments. Dana ran the check-in table’s setup because a table needed running and her hands were her hands. Bev arrived and recalibrated the slideshow by the primitive expedient of a different laptop, muttering about software named like fabric softener. The caterer’s people flowed in with trays, brisket nowhere among them, some other city’s idea of comfort, tiny elegant things, and Marta and the building people arrived at noon en masse with their own second wave of food for the after, because Aunt Lou’s law operated at every scale, catering for the room, this for the family, and the family, it turned out, had been a three-story building in Rogers Park all along.

The prints made the room. Nobody had planned it and everybody felt it as the chairs filled: Bev’s crew had hung fourteen of them at gallery scale, and the folding-chair congregation assembled inside Nic’s own seeing, the old man’s hands four feet wide, the collapsing wedding party, the geraniums, the fire escape at the back like an altarpiece, so that every mourner sat facing the work and the work faced back, and people kept turning in their seats to look behind them, and finding another one, and going still. Dana watched a bride stand in front of her own wedding print for five whole minutes with her husband’s hand in hers, neither of them speaking, and thought: this is the door Justine wanted. It’s already open. It’s hanging on the walls. All the speakers had to do was not stand in front of it.

The carpool landed at twelve forty, five hours of Ohio walking in the door in a knot, Rachel first, arms already open at range, Amy behind her, Deb pale but victorious from the front seat, and Rachel took Dana in the whole-body hug of the deeply sincere and said into her shoulder, “Look at this. Look at all this. She mattered so much,” and then held her at arm’s length with tears standing ready and said, “How are you, are you ready, I can’t imagine, you knew her best,” and there it was, one more time, the title, hung on her in a cold gallery an hour before the podium, and Dana looked at Rachel’s kind unguarded face and did not perform the title and did not refuse it either, just said, “I’m going to try to get her right,” which was true, and let Rachel squeeze her hands, and showed them to seats.

At one o’clock Justine found her at the check-in table and pulled her by the sleeve into the gallery’s little kitchen alcove, between the coffee urns, for what turned out to be sixty seconds of nothing logistical at all.

“Whatever you say up there,” Justine said, low, fast, a woman spending a minute she didn’t have, “I want you to know it’s already done. Whatever you decide. You went first into the letters and you went first into the roll, and tomorrow I get an envelope with my sister’s last week in it and I already know I can open it, because you told me what country it is. That’s the job you actually did this month. The speech is just the public part.” She straightened Dana’s collar with two fingers, a sister’s gesture, unowed, and stepped back. “Also Aunt Lou has decided you’re ‘the Ohio one’ and has questions about your casserole positions, do not engage, she’s undefeated.” And she was gone, back into the logistics, clipboard rising like a conductor’s baton, leaving Dana at the coffee urns with her collar straight, paid in a currency she hadn’t known the account could receive.

At one fifteen she went to the little back room behind the office to put herself together, and Ella was in there, in a dark green dress, pacing a three-step track, holding index cards.

“I’m reading a list,” Ella said, before Dana could ask. “I couldn’t write a speech, everything I wrote sounded like a college essay, so I’m just reading a list. Mom said a list is allowed. Is a list allowed? It’s called ‘Ordinary Facts About My Aunt.’ It’s things like, she salted watermelon. She could whistle through her hands and refused to teach anyone. She called the camera strap the leash.” The cards trembled very slightly. “Is it stupid? You can tell me. You’re the only one who’d tell me.”

“It’s the best thing anyone will say today,” Dana said, and meant it with her whole chest. “Including me. She asked the whole world for a roll of film of an ordinary day, Ella. You wrote the contact sheet.”

Ella’s face did several things and elected, heroically, to hold. “Okay. Okay. Don’t make me cry before, I did my eyes.” She squared the cards. “You’re after me. Mom, Bev, me, you. You’re the anchor.” She said it with the serene brutality of the young. “What are you going to say?”

And Dana, in the little back room, in the cold gallery, an hour out, with the prepared remarks folded in her jacket’s inside left pocket and the quartered page from the legal pad in the right, where it had migrated that morning from the bag without her fully deciding, one version over each side of her chest like documents at a border crossing, told Ella the truth, because she was done doing anything else in back rooms.

“I don’t know yet,” she said. “I’ve got the speech everyone’s expecting in one pocket, and it’s good, and it’s a Christmas card. And I’ve got four sentences in the other pocket that are true, and I don’t know if I can say them standing up. I’m going to find out which woman walks up there about the same time everybody else does.”

Ella considered her for a moment, Nic’s chin at a familiar angle.

“She used to say a thing about pictures,” Ella said. “When I’d do a hundred takes of the same selfie. She’d say, you can’t compose your way out of what the light is doing. She said that’s the whole secret, everybody thinks it’s about the framing, and it’s ninety percent knowing what the light is doing and getting out of its way.” She shrugged, cards to her chest. “I don’t know. It seemed like it might be transferable.”

Then the door went, and it was Justine in her funeral-adjacent best, hair actually down for once, day forty-six, checklist in hand and something underneath the checklist finally quiet, and behind her the sound of the gallery filling had changed register, chairs and murmur and the specific hum of a room reaching mass, and she looked at the two of them, her daughter with the index cards, her sister’s oldest friend, and said, “It’s time. It’s a full house. She’d count it twice,” and held the door, and the three of them went out into the cold bright room together, which was warming, exactly as advertised, now that it was filled.

# Chapter 28

[J]{.dropcap}ustine went first and was brief and clean, a sister’s accounting: the girl who resurrected a dead camera with a library book, the woman who never sent an invoice on time and never missed a shoot, the aunt who showed up at every recital with a lens like a telescope and made two bored kids feel like heads of state. She thanked the room for coming and did not cry, and stepped down, and Dana watched her take her seat next to Ella and put her hands flat on her knees, a woman setting down the front half of a piano.

Bev spoke about the work, and about Tuesdays. About a printer’s eye, and a laugh you could locate across a darkroom by feel, and how the co-op’s Tuesday nights had a hole in them now the exact shape of a person reading Christmas cards aloud in the voices, and Dana, third chair from the aisle in the second row, received that in front of two hundred people with her face steady and her hands folded, and found it did not knock her down. She had done her falling in a wooden chair by a lab window. It was possible, it turned out, to have already paid a thing.

Ella read her list, and the list did what lists do when the items are true. She salted watermelon. She called the camera strap the leash. She could whistle through her hands and refused to teach anyone, and said the secret would be in her will, and it was not, we checked. She answered every text from a teenager within four minutes, every single one, for nineteen years. The room laughed and wept in the same breath, no border between them, the whole gallery doing at scale what Ella had done in a receiving line in March, and the girl read the last card, looked up, said, “That’s my whole speech. She’d say it ran long,” and sat down to the sound of two hundred people loving her, and then it was quiet, and the quiet turned, and Dana stood up.

The walk to the front was nine steps. She had the pages out of her left pocket by step four, unfolded on the little lectern by step seven, and she looked out at the full house, Justine and Ella and Aunt Lou down front, Bev’s red beads, Rachel already crying, Marta and the building rows, the brides in their delegation, the co-op in its dark plumage, Dominic, she found, standing at the back by the big print of the fire escape, and the afternoon light came down through the gallery’s high windows and lay across all of them in long mild bars, and she began.

“I met Nic on the second day of freshman year, when a lab TA with a clipboard made the worst pairing decision in the history of Ohio science education.”

The laugh came, warm, primed, right on schedule, and she rode it, and told the pig. The lab TA and the clipboard and the alphabetical fate of it, Adair and Ames, one line apart on the roster, so that a girl from Knox County got a girl from Chicago and the twentieth century’s last great lab partnership convened over a tray of instruments neither of them could name. The goggles pushed up into sandy hair. The scalpel raised like a baton. The papal decree, this animal deserves dignity, Dana, and dignity begins with a name, and his name is Gerald, and you can’t stop me, and the TA three tables away asking if there was a problem, and Nic answering, with total composure, that there was not, that there had been a christening. And the semester it launched, the practical Dana nearly failed because the word Gerald said in a certain register could end her from across a room, the TA’s growing certainty that Ames had a medical condition, the final exam during which Nic, passing her in the aisle, had whispered the name once, like a curse in an opera, and kept walking. She told it the way it deserved, tell the thing she did, and the room rocked, Aunt Lou’s laugh riding over the top like a gull, Ella down front mouthing along at the beats she’d inherited, a story getting its third generation, and Dana felt the machine of her own competence come up to temperature, felt the next paragraph queue itself, the wedding, the parking lot wind, the pivot to what she gave all of us, the whole polished staircase of it descending smoothly toward wasn’t she wonderful, and her eyes went down to the page for the transition, and the page was excellent, and that was the thing.

The page was excellent. She heard it from one sentence out, the way you hear rain arrive, heard exactly how the next twenty lines would land, every laugh placed, every catch in the voice arriving on the beat she’d allotted it, two hundred people filing out into the April afternoon saying she was wonderful, they were so close, and the biggest Christmas card of her life already licking its own envelope, and her right hand, resting at the lectern’s edge, moved on its own the small distance to her jacket pocket and found through the fabric the corner of a page folded in quarters, and held it.

Dana folded the prepared pages closed.

She did it without drama, one motion, the paper’s small sound enormous in the microphone, and looked up at the room in the long light, and the room looked back, two hundred faces going carefully still the way faces do when the script visibly leaves a speaker’s hands, and she stood in the silence she had been outrunning since a Tuesday night in February, and let it be as long as it actually was.

“I had more,” she said. “It’s good. It’s exactly as good as I could make it, and Nic would have seen through it before I cleared the second paragraph, so I’m going to stop performing for the one person who could always tell.”

Somewhere in the folding chairs, one person made a sound that was half a laugh, and it helped.

“Here’s what’s true. Nic and I lost touch. Not a falling-out. Nothing you could point to. I stopped answering, a little at a time, over years, the way you put a thing down so slowly you can tell yourself you’re still holding it, and by the end we hadn’t spoken in six years, and I found out she died from a text with the wrong day in it.” She kept her eyes up. Justine’s face, down front, was unreadable, and she did not read it, and went on. “Most of you knew her more recently than I did. That’s the plain fact, and you deserved a speaker without a hole in her, and instead you got me, because I’m the one who has the college stories, and because her sister asked, and because,” her hand, in her pocket, held the rail, “because I wanted the title back more than I wanted to earn it. So the college story is my whole offering. It’s the truth about her at twenty and it’s all I’ve got that’s mine.

“But I’ll tell you what I learned about her this month, from the people in this room, and from some letters, and from a roll of film, and it’s this. She never put anybody down. That is the difference between us, and I’m not saying it to flog myself at a podium, I’m saying it because it’s the finding, and she’d want the finding stated plainly. Everyone she ever picked up, she kept holding. Some of you know exactly what I mean, and the rest of you were being held and never knew, and if there’s one thing I’d ask you to take out of this room, it’s to go home and look at your own two hands and see who’s in them, and answer somebody. That’s the wisdom of the person who did it wrong. She asked you all for a roll of film of an ordinary day. I’d add one line to it: send the text back. It costs nine words. I priced it.”

She looked at the big print of the fire escape at the back of the room, the light coming through the rail in bars, Dominic beside it with his arms folded and his glasses off.

“I’ll miss her for the rest of my life,” Dana said. “I already did it for six years without knowing that’s what it was. It turns out you can. It turns out that’s the one thing about missing someone that nobody tells you, you can do it wrong, for years, with the door open the whole time.” She took a breath, and it went in steady, and came out steady, one true thing said out loud by the one person who owed it, exactly the size it actually was. “Her name was Nicola June Adair. She saw people the way they hoped to be seen. And she named the pig Gerald, and nobody could stop her, and nobody ever wanted to.”

She picked up her folded pages and walked the nine steps back, and sat down in the third chair of the second row, and the room did not applaud, memorial rooms don’t, they did the other thing, the long exhale, the shifting, the sound of two hundred people recalibrating something each in their own chest, and beside her Rachel was crying without any performance at all now, silently, holding Amy’s hand, and down front Aunt Lou was nodding slowly at the middle distance like a judge who had heard the argument she’d been waiting for.

At the front of the room the slideshow began, Bev’s laptop faithful where the casino greeter had failed. Nic at six, gap-toothed, holding a garden hose like a microphone. Nic at eleven with Justine, matching terrible haircuts, an era’s crime. Nic at nineteen behind the lifeguard-summer Canon, one eye shut, aiming at whoever held the cheaper one, and Dana’s breath caught because she knew who had held the cheaper one, the photograph’s photographer sitting right here in the second row, twenty-six years downstream. Nic in the green dress at a wedding by the water. Nic gray, laughing, mid-word forever.
