The room watched a life assemble at six seconds a frame, the old man’s four-foot hands presiding from the wall above it, and Dana sat with her folded excellent pages in one hand and the quartered page unused and honored in her pocket, and did not look at Justine, and felt, from the row in front, without a word, without a glance, Justine’s hand come back over the top of her own chair and stay there, open, a few inches from Dana’s knee, not touching, available, like something plugged in.

# Chapter 29

[T]{.dropcap}he reception assembled itself in the same room, chairs folded and racked by unseen consensus, tables unfolding in their place, the catering trays advancing, and the gathering changed state from congregation to crowd, two hundred people released into motion with plates in their hands under the gaze of the four-foot photographs.

Dana had expected, walking back up the aisle when it ended, to be avoided. She had braced for it all through the slideshow, the polite curved wakes people carve around a confessed thing. What happened instead was stranger and took her most of an hour to understand: people came to her, and they came carrying their own.

At one point a small woman in a raincoat came in late and did not take a plate, and Justine crossed the room to her with a flat parcel in brown paper and put it into her hands, and stood while the woman held it and cried and said something, and Justine said something back, warmly, and touched her arm, and came away, and stopped beside Dana at the drinks table and picked up a cup she did not drink from.

“Krista,” she said, to the middle distance.

“You gave it to her.”

“I gave her the fire escape. A small one. She’ll hang it somewhere and tell people she knew Nic.” Justine set the cup down and turned it a quarter turn on the cloth. “Four years she left that lunch hanging, and I stood there and was lovely to her, and I’ll be lovely to her at Christmas too if she writes. That’s not me being big about it. It’s just that Nic would have given her one, and if I start deciding who’s allowed, I’ll be doing that for the rest of my life, and I’d rather do the other thing.” She looked at the raincoat going back out into the afternoon. “It costs me something. I want one person in this building to know that it costs me something.”

“I know it does.”

“Good,” Justine said, and went to find her aunt.

A man from the co-op, holding his plate like a hymnal, told her without preamble about a brother in Phoenix, four years of unreturned calls, no fight, nothing you could point to, and asked her nothing, wanted nothing, just set it down in front of her the way you set a coin in a case that finally has the right slot, and moved on. One of the brides said her college roommate, and didn’t finish the sentence, and didn’t need to. Rachel arrived in a state, mascara gone abstract, and did not take Dana’s hands.

“Why didn’t you tell me,” she said.

“Rachel.”

“No, I want the answer, because I’ve been standing over there for ten minutes doing the math and it keeps coming out the same.” Her voice had gone up half a step, and two people at the drinks table were being extremely careful not to turn around. “Three weeks ago I called you about Marcus. About whether she’d gone under and none of us noticed. And I said, before anybody spirals, I’ll ask Dana, Dana would know. And you sat in your kitchen and told me she was auditing her commitments, and I hung up and called Deb and said it’s fine, Dana would have known, and Deb called Amy, and Amy called Marcus. I fed a whole phone tree. And you hadn’t spoken to her in six years.”

“That’s all true.”

“I know it’s true, I just heard you say it into a microphone.” She pressed the back of her wrist under one eye, hard, and looked at it, and it came away black. “Do you know what I’ve been this month? I’ve been the one telling everybody it was fine. That was my whole contribution. A woman I loved died and I went around being reassuring, and I got the reassurance from you.”

“I’m sorry.”

“I know you are. That isn’t.” She stopped. Somebody’s grandson went between them with two plates held high. “You could have said I don’t know. Three words. And instead you let me carry it out of the room for you, because it was easier to be right than to be honest with somebody who has never once in twenty-seven years been anything but nice to you.”

There was a great deal Dana could have said, and every sentence of it was a coat, and she stood in the middle of a reception with her hands at her sides and did not hand her one.

“You’re right,” she said.

“Don’t do that either.” Rachel’s chin had gone. “Don’t just agree with me, that’s the same thing in a different hat.” She got her purse onto her shoulder in one motion, the way a woman does when the leaving has already been decided. “Amy’s driving. I’m going to go sit in the car.”

“Rachel.”

“I’m not being dramatic, I actually want to sit in the car,” Rachel said, which was possibly the most accurate sentence Dana had ever heard her produce, and she went, threading the crowd, saying warm things to two people on the way out because she could not help it, and then she was through the door.

And then, near the drinks table, an older woman Dana didn’t know, seventies, good wool coat still on as if she hadn’t fully decided to stay, touched her sleeve. “Thank you,” she said. Nothing else. Her eyes did the rest, and whatever the rest was, whoever it had been, however many years, Dana didn’t ask, because the woman had come this far to say two words and not one more, and receiving them was the whole transaction. Dana said, “Thank you for coming,” and the woman nodded, one economy answering another, and buttoned her coat and left, and Dana stood by the drinks table understanding that she would think about that woman for the rest of her life and never know a single fact about her except the one that mattered, which she knew completely.

Ella found her at the dessert table, plate loaded with the strategic maximalism of nineteen, and stood beside her a moment surveying the room before she said it, sideways, in the family style.

“So the famous Dana was partly a myth.”

“Partly a myth,” Dana agreed. “I’m sorry, Ella. You inherited an exhibit with a wrong label on it.”

“Yeah, I’ve been thinking about that through four desserts.” Ella turned to face her, and there was Nic’s chin at its argumentative angle, and behind it something older than nineteen, arriving early the way it does in the ones who lose someone young. “Here’s where I landed. The stories were true. The pig was true, the alternator car was true, you have the college eyes, I’ve now watched you have them in person. What was fake was the last six years, and I never had any stories from the last six years anyway. So from where I’m sitting, nothing I actually inherited was counterfeit. The label was wrong. The collection’s real.” She stole a cookie off her own overloaded plate as if from someone else’s. “Also she kept you in her favorites and photographed your picture with her last roll, so whatever you think you were to her by the end, you’re wrong about that too, just in the other direction. I’m nineteen, I’m allowed to just say things.” She bumped Dana’s shoulder with hers, briefly, the entire embrace the family style permitted in public, and drifted back toward her cousins, calling over her shoulder, “You’re still the one with the college stories. That part was never fake. I’m coming to Ohio for them, fair warning, I want the extended versions.”

Bev ran her down at three thirty with two plates of cake, handed one over without asking, and stationed them both in front of the fire escape, which was drawing a steady congregation.

“Sold four prints today,” Bev said, to the geraniums. “Not these. Editions, from the co-op files, she’d have been scandalized and then she’d have raised the prices. The money goes to the darkroom fund, we voted Tuesday.” She ate cake, with the efficiency of a woman who had things developing. “Here’s what I came over to say, so receive it and don’t make a production. Tuesdays run seven to ten. The enlarger she used is the third from the door, and nobody’s touched it, and nobody’s going to be weird about it, because we are extremely old and have no energy for weird. You’ve got her camera. Ella says it’s staying with you.” She looked at Dana over the red-bead chain, the whole transaction visible in her face before she made it. “You’re going to shoot a roll in Ohio and then you’re going to need a place to learn what you did wrong. Chicago has one. It smells like vinegar and it’s full of people who knew her, and the coffee’s terrible, and the parking’s worse, and that’s the entire pitch.”

“That’s a six-hour drive for a three-hour Tuesday.”

“It was a six-hour drive for a cup of coffee she never got,” Bev said, not unkindly, and let that sit exactly as long as it needed to, and then patted Dana’s arm with a cake fork still in her hand. “Come in June. We’ll do the geranium wall. Marta’s bringing the plants back for the season, we’re putting them on the escape for the co-op show, Theo’s building the mounts. She’d want a big turnout and she’d count the house twice.” And she moved off toward Raymond, who was standing guard over the diner-hands print in his one suit’s older brother, leaving Dana holding cake in front of forty square feet of soup-gold light, in possession of a standing Tuesday, a June appointment, and the dawning understanding that the city she had driven to twice in grief was planning, with Bev-like efficiency, to become a place she simply went.

Dominic found her at four with a flat envelope under his arm, dressed in what was clearly the suit of a man who owned one. “The runner had them ready, so I brought them myself,” he said. “Saved the co-op a trip.” He handed it over, prints, two eight-by-tens, frames twenty-two and thirty-one, and looked around the room at the walls, at his own decades of processing hung four feet wide, and said, “She’d count the house twice,” not the first time today someone had said it, family idiom, apparently, learned by everyone the woman had ever let near her, and he shook Dana’s hand with a craftsman’s grip and went to pay his respects to the fire escape.

She found Justine in the kitchen alcove at five, alone for the first moment in nine hours, standing at the sink with her heels off and her hands in dishwater, washing the family’s glasses while the caterers packed the room behind them, and Dana picked up a towel without being asked and took station beside her, and for a few minutes they worked in the plain animal comfort of a shared task, wash, rinse, pass, dry, Aunt Lou’s casserole dish waiting at the end of the line like a dignitary.

“So,” Justine said, to the water.

“So.”

“You want to know what I thought. Up there. When you folded the pages.” She passed a glass. “I thought: there it is. That’s the thing I picked on day two, in her phone, at four in the morning. I picked the favorites list and I built you out of it, and I watched you stand up in front of two hundred people and take the building down, and I thought, good. Take it down. I was so tired of living in it, Dana. Both of us propping up the same story from opposite ends, you out of guilt and me out of,” she turned a glass under the tap, “out of needing my sister to have had one thing that never thinned. And here’s what I’ve got now, instead, and I’ve been turning it over all afternoon, so receive it in the spirit it’s given.

“She never blamed you out loud either. Not once, in six years, to me, to Bev, to anyone. You told me about the page she wrote and unwrote, and I’ve thought about that more than anything else you said, because I’d have mailed it. I’d have mailed it the same night. She wrote it and put it in a drawer and went on being nice to you for three more years, and that is not a thing that was done to her, Dana, that’s a thing she did. Twenty-six letters and not one of them mailed. You two were the same animal, I said that once and I mean it more now. She held her side of the silence just as carefully as you held yours. So when you stand there and take the whole weight of it, I need you to know I’m not going to hand it all to you, because half of it was hers, and being dead doesn’t get her out of it, she’d be the first to say so.” The last glass went into the rack. Justine dried her hands and looked at her, day forty-six, hair down, wrung and standing. “That’s not absolution, in case you’re doing the math. I don’t have absolution, it’s not mine to issue. What I’ve got is the accounting. The accounting says two, and it will always say two, and you’re going to have to live with only ever carrying half. That’s the sentence. Some people would call it mercy.”

The caterers wheeled something past the doorway. Somewhere in the emptying gallery, Ella laughed at something one of the grandsons said, the sound carrying the way her aunt’s used to carry across a darkroom, locatable by feel.

“Show me,” Justine said.

Dana opened the envelope on the drying rack’s counter, between the glasses and the casserole dish, and slid out frame thirty-one, and stood back half a step, the job’s last motion, gone first and come back and now holding the door, and watched Justine look at her sister’s final photograph, the shelf, the frame within the frame, the two girls squinting into that long-gone sun, composed square-on in good light by a woman days from the end of her seeing and seven weeks from a door she’d decided to knock on.

Justine looked at it for a long time. Her face did its work privately, and Dana let it, no coat, no detour.

“She kept you at eye level,” Justine said finally. “The whole time. I must have stood in front of that shelf a hundred times this month.” She touched the print’s white border, just the border, careful of the image the way her sister would have been. “Okay,” she said, and it was the okay of a woman closing the last checklist of a long administration, and finding, on the other side of it, not the void she’d been bracing for but a room with photographs in it, livable, exactly as promised. “Okay. Take the casserole dish to Aunt Lou or neither of us will survive the week. And Dana.” She put frame thirty-one back into its envelope and handed the envelope back, keeper to keeper. “The camera’s yours. That was always the shape of it, Ella called it in week one. She’d want it working, not archived. Put film in it. That’s the condition. It’s the only condition.”

Dana carried the casserole dish out through the gallery, past the fire escape coming down off the wall in Theo’s careful arms, past Bev directing the de-installation like a harbormaster, past the glass doors and the lot beyond them where a blue sedan sat with three women in it and its engine not running, and delivered the dish into Aunt Lou’s custody, and was interrogated about noodle ratios, and held her own, barely, and it was six o’clock, and the room was almost a gallery again, white and tall and cold now that it was emptying, and out in the lot the blue sedan started up and pulled onto Clark and went south, toward Ohio, six hours, with Rachel in it.

# Chapter 30

[S]{.dropcap}he called her mother from the hotel at nine, because she had promised the actual day and not three days later, and because there was a difference between the two and her mother had named it out loud, which meant it was load-bearing.

Lorraine answered on the second ring. “Ames residence.”

“It’s me. I got up there and I did it.”

“Well.” A chair took her mother’s weight somewhere in the ordered kitchen, which meant Lorraine had sat down for this, which Lorraine did not do in the middle of an evening. “Tell me you ate something.”

“I ate a taco standing up in a window on Thursday and I’ve had cake since.”

“That is not a meal, that’s a schedule.” The freezer hummed its inventory. “So. Was it a good room.”

And there it was, the door, opened the width her mother opened doors, disguised as a question about the venue. Dana stood at the fourth-floor window with the city holding its lights along the water’s hem and did not reach past it for the small manageable thing.

“I told them the truth,” she said. “About the six years. I had a speech and I put it down and I told two hundred people I stopped answering her. Out loud. Standing up.”

Somewhere fifty miles north of Westerville a tap ran and was shut.

“Well,” Lorraine said, and then nothing, for long enough that Dana made herself stay inside it, which cost her more at that window than the podium had. “That would have taken something.”

“It did.”

“You always could get up in front of people.” A pause with the shape of a woman deciding how far she was going to go and stopping where she always stopped. “That isn’t what I meant. I know it isn’t what you meant either.”

“I know, Mom.”

“All right then.” The plate for the road, arriving down fifty miles of copper. “You drive home in the daylight tomorrow, not the dark. And you call me Sunday like a person.”

“Sunday,” Dana said, and meant it, and they hung up, and it had not been the kitchen with the blue-check chairs, and there had been a receiver in her hand the whole time and a cradle to set it back in, and she had said it anyway. Eleven minutes. She did not write it down.

Then she called her sister, who picked up before the first ring finished, which meant Kim had been holding the phone.

“Well?”

“I did it. Badly. On purpose.”

“Badly on purpose is my entire personality, welcome,” Kim said, and then started to cry, honking, unglamorous, narrating the crying while she did it because she had never once been able to feel a thing without also filing a review of how ridiculous it was to be feeling it. “I’m in the driveway. I’ve been in the driveway since seven. I didn’t want to be in the house when you called, the girls ask questions.”

“You’ve been sitting in a car for two hours.”

“I have a podcast and most of a granola bar, don’t do the voice.” A wet sniff. “Tell me the whole thing. Not the summary. I want the parts where you were bad at it.”

So Dana told her the parts where she was bad at it, and it took a while, and Kim asked the real questions and then stayed on the line through the ugly quiet after each one, which was her one skill and had turned out to be the only one that mattered, and it was eleven forty when they hung up. Dana lay down in a hotel bed in a city she was going to keep coming back to, and slept badly, and did not mind.

Sunday dinner was pot roast, because the full report deserved a meal that could sit through it, and Dana gave it, itemized, at the dining room table, to her whole nation of two.

She told it in order. The apartment loadout and Raymond’s one-sentence memorial. Dominic and the claim ticket and the five clear frames. The contact sheet, square by square, and Iris put her fork down at the mitten on the fence post and didn’t pick it up again until frame thirty-one, which Dana produced, the actual print, passed hand to hand around the pot roast, her family holding the last thing Nic ever composed, and Tom looked at it a long time and said, quietly, “There’s the shelf,” to himself, a man filing a finding under its permanent number. She told the gallery cold and Aunt Lou’s casserole doctrine, which Iris immediately adopted as constitutional law. She told Ella’s list, and the pig story’s third-generation debut, and the fold, the actual moment, the paper’s sound in the microphone, and what she’d said instead, the small version, nine words priced, and Iris, who had ordered the pig story under threat, listened to the account of it with her chin on her fists and ruled, at the end, “Good. That’s what I said. Tell the thing she did,” entirely satisfied that the entire memorial had run on her counsel, which, Dana reflected, it partly had.

Iris, mid-report, had asked the one question with a border on it. “What did the letters say, though. The twenty-six.” Not prying, just twelve, cataloguing, and Tom had gone carefully neutral over the roast in the way of a man who had wondered the same thing and priced the asking.

“Those were hers,” Dana said, and found the sentence already whole, as if it had been standing by. “She wrote them to me but she never sent them, and unsent means she was still deciding, and you don’t publish somebody mid-decision. So I’ll tell you what they added up to, which is mine to tell. She thought she’d lost me, the same years I thought I’d lost her. We were both wrong in the same direction at the same time. That’s the whole cargo. The wording stays hers.”

Iris absorbed this with the gravity of a customs official reviewing sealed diplomatic freight, and nodded, and stamped it through. “That’s fair. Marcy reads her sister’s journal and I always said it was a war crime.” And Tom looked at his wife down the length of the table, the woman who three weeks ago couldn’t hand anyone a grocery list, drawing a border around a dead friend’s privacy in front of her family, holding it, and something in his face settled another quarter turn, and he passed the potatoes, which was, in the dialect of Tom, applause.

“And Justine gave me the camera,” she finished. “On one condition. It has to work. It can’t be a shelf thing.”

“So you have to take pictures,” Iris said. “With film. That you can’t even see.” She considered this technology with deep suspicion and dawning interest. “What are you going to take pictures of?”

“An ordinary day,” Dana said. “It’s what she asked everybody for. I figure I owe one roll.”

“Our day is so boring.”

“That’s the assignment.”

“Can I be in it?”

“You’re most of it,” Dana said, and Iris pretended to find this burdensome and glowed like a filament, and Tom passed the print back to Dana with both hands, and said, “Frame it. The stairs. There’s a spot at the turn,” and it was the spot she’d already chosen, the wall of Nic’s seeing gaining its last entry, and the pot roast went its distance, and the report was filed, all of it, the books of the house open and balanced for the first time in a season.

It was Priya who went quiet the following week.

Dana missed it. Not almost, in the end, though she would tell it afterward as almost, because almost was the version a person could live with. Tuesday’s run request came back can’t tonight, sorry, and she read it at the front desk between a hygiene check-in and a crown, and her thumb did what her thumb did, and the little blue thumb went up under the message, and the message went away up the screen, and she moved down the list to Denny.

That was Tuesday. Wednesday she was excellent.

She was excellent for eleven hours. She confirmed the brisket count with Denny, twice, because Denny answered on the second try and not the first and she had learned to build that in. She got the custodial schedule moved by asking Mr. Reyes what he would do, which was the only lever that had ever worked on the man. She rebuilt the volunteer roster around a family that had quietly stopped answering, and did not once wonder why they had. At four fifteen she talked a woman with a cracked molar and no insurance into a payment plan the office did not technically offer, and at six she made dinner, and at seven she sat at the dining room table with tab nine and the golfer mug full of pens and Gerald keeping the far end, and she was, all day, in the flat public sense, at the very top of her profession.

And the whole time, sitting in the phone in her back pocket, four inches from her, was can’t tonight, sorry.

No runner emoji face down on its side. No counteroffer. No thurs?. Two springs of ceremonial apparatus, the knee complaints and the rival walking club and the hieroglyph for the death of discipline, and not one piece of it, and Dana had thumbs-upped it like a snow-day announcement and gone to save four hundred people’s brisket.

It surfaced Wednesday night at the dishwasher, sideways and late, a plate in her hand and the shape arriving before the words for it. No emoji. No Thursday proposed. She stood with the plate dripping onto her foot and did the arithmetic she could now do in about four seconds, because she had graphed this exact curve at this exact table with her own two eyes in March, and here was its first quarter inch, on her own phone, twenty-six hours old, filed under handled by her own thumb.

A month ago, a year ago, a lifetime ago, she’d have filed it under busy and been technically correct. Every insane week true. The wall gaining its first course of honest brick.

She drove over Thursday at six fifteen, unannounced, in the rain, with a bouquet from the gas station on Cooper that could most charitably be described as assorted, carnations dyed colors carnations had never consented to, half of it baby’s breath, the whole thing wearing its plastic sleeve like a raincoat, and knocked, and Priya opened the door in the clothes of a woman who had not planned on witnesses, and looked at Dana, and looked at the flowers.

“Those are horrifying.”

“They were between the motor oil and the beef jerky. I panicked.”

“They’re the ugliest flowers I’ve ever seen. Get in here before the neighbors think I’ve died.”

The house was dim and the kitchen table held the topography Dana knew from the inside, the laptop open to airline tabs, the phone face up beside it, a mug gone cold at the elbow. Priya’s mother had fallen, it came out, at the table, once the horrifying flowers had been given water they did not deserve. In Mumbai, on Sunday, badly enough for a fractured wrist and a night of scans, and Priya’s brother had handled everything and kept saying handled, don’t come, it’s handled, and Priya had spent four days orbiting the word, running her practice, packing school lunches, being excellent, and quietly pricing flights at two in the morning without booking any, caught in the exact mid-air Dana could have drawn from memory, too far to help, too tethered to go, too disciplined to hand the weight of it to anyone on this continent.

“Sunday,” Dana said. “It’s Thursday.”

“It is Thursday.”

“You didn’t tell me.”

Priya turned the cold mug a quarter turn on the table, and did not do wounded, because Priya did not do wounded, Priya did accurate, and accurate was worse.

“No,” she said. “I thought about it Sunday. And then I thought, she’s just back from the memorial, and then I thought, and then she’s got the auction, and then I thought,” and here she looked up, mild as anything, nine years of friendship in it, “and then I thought about the last four months, and I couldn’t think of the last time I told you a thing before it was over.”

The rain worked at the window over the sink.

“That’s not an accusation. I want to be clear, because you’re already sitting there taking it as one.” Priya turned the mug back. “It’s just what I noticed, and I noticed it Sunday, at eleven at night, on the phone with my brother, and I remember thinking it in exactly those words. I’ve got one person in this country I’d say this to and she’s busy in a way that isn’t going to end.”

“It ended.”

“Sure. But I’d already stopped checking.” She said it without any weight at all, the way you’d report a road closing. “That’s the bit. Not that you were busy. That I’d stopped checking whether you still were.”

Dana sat with that.

There was a plan already assembling itself somewhere behind her sternum, fully staffed, flights and coverage and a list of what could be handed to whom, Kayla could take the Thursday book, the committee could survive Julie for a fortnight, the practice would bend if she leaned on Fanning about the double-bookings first. It arrived, complete, useful, and about nine seconds too early, and she felt her mouth get ready to deliver it, and there was no ceremony in what happened next and no credit in it either. She just did not open it.

“Okay,” she said. “I heard that. All of it. I’m not going to argue with the middle part.”

“You want to argue with the middle part.”

“I want to argue with the middle part very badly.”

“I know. It’s your tell.” Priya put her face in her hands, briefly, and laughed one wet syllable into her palms, and Dana came around the table and sat down beside her, shoulder to shoulder, and did not produce the plan, and did not mention that there was a plan, and did not sit there being a woman visibly not producing a plan, which she had discovered in a moving-blanketed chair in Rogers Park was a whole separate performance with its own costume.

She just sat there. It turned out to be available. It had been available the entire time.

So they sat, and Priya talked, and it came out at its actual size, not the wrist at all. It was the nine thousand miles, and the arithmetic of a mother turning eighty an ocean away, and the specific loneliness of loving someone across a distance you chose two decades ago for good reasons that stayed good and cost anyway, every year, in a currency nobody at this end ever saw her pay. Dana knew the shape from the inside out, distance elected and then grieved, a life built right and mourned anyway at its seams, and she said so, the mile-two version of her own spring, and Priya listened with her chin on her fist, and outside the rain worked the windows, and the horrifying flowers stood their watch, and it was, Dana thought, the exact conversation the loop had been training them for through two springs of knee complaints, the real distance run at last, no split times, seated.

“Book the flight for June,” she said finally, when the turn came. “Not because it’s an emergency. Because it isn’t. Go when it’s just a wrist and a visit, eat the food, fight with your brother in person, take four hundred pictures of your mother’s hands. Don’t save the trip for when it can only be terrible. I paid full price for that one, so somebody should get it wholesale.”

Priya lifted her head and looked at the assorted horrifying flowers standing in their vase, dyed and dauntless, the worst bouquet in Ohio, delivered unannounced in the rain by a woman who had read two data points twenty-six hours late and driven over anyway.

“Late,” she observed.

“Late,” Dana agreed. “I’m going to be late for the rest of my life. I’ve made my peace with late. Late is not the same as not.”

“No,” Priya said, considering it. “No, it isn’t,” and her face did something complicated and settled on the truth. “Okay. June.”

And then, because the treaty had terms and the terms held: “You’re still running Sunday. Grief doesn’t get you out of the 10K. Nothing gets anybody out of the 10K.”

“I wouldn’t dare,” Dana said, and stayed another hour, and the laptop’s airline tabs became one booked itinerary before she left, witnessed and celebrated with the last of somebody’s Easter candy, and she drove home through the rain, and the gas station on Cooper went by lit up in the wet dark, still open, still selling terrible flowers to anyone who needed to arrive somewhere holding something.

# Chapter 31

[S]{.dropcap}he took the Friday off, which she had not done in four years except for the mammogram and for Nic, and told the practice it was a family thing, and told Tom it was a family thing, and drove north at nine in the morning with nothing in the car and nothing on the seat beside her, no soup going back, no photographs, no dread, only the sentence.

She had built it over three days and said it out loud in the car on Thursday at a red light on Cleveland Avenue, and it had four parts, three parts too many. She had cut it to two by Delaware. By the time the fields opened out past Sunbury it was one. Mom, I let a friend go and I learned it here, and I need to know if you know that.

Lorraine had the storm door open before she was up the walk. Of course she did. The Buick sat nose-out in the garage with April on its windshield, and there was a bag of grass seed by the step that somebody had delivered and Lorraine had not yet decided the fate of, and the house closed around Dana warm and ordered and smelling of the same cleaner, and the freezer hummed its inventory, and the list under the Ohio magnet said, in ballpoint, gutters, call about seed, D. coming Fri.

She was on the list. Dana stood in the doorway looking at her own initial in her mother’s hand and had to put her keys in the bowl slowly to get past it.

“You said Friday and you meant Friday,” Lorraine said. “That’s the first time in six years.”

“I took the day.”

“You took a day.” Lorraine said it the way you’d report a comet. “Then you’ll eat something, because a person who takes a day and doesn’t eat has wasted it. Sit down. Not that chair, the leg’s gone again, Ronnie’s coming Monday.”

So Dana sat in the other chair at the blue-check table, and there was coffee, and there was a coffee cake of the kind Lorraine produced from nowhere at forty minutes’ notice, and for a while it was the gutters. It was genuinely the gutters. There was a downspout that emptied onto the neighbor’s side of the property line and the neighbor had said nothing for eleven years and had now, at eighty-one, said something, and Lorraine’s position on this was that a man who waits eleven years to raise a downspout has not been thinking about the downspout, he has been thinking about the fence, and the fence was 1998 and Lorraine remembered it perfectly, and Dana laughed so hard she had to put the cup down, because her mother was, when nothing was at stake, one of the funniest people she had ever met.

The light came in low across the wall by the phone. The photographs went up it, Dana at six on a swing, forty years climbing toward the ceiling, and above and to the left the brighter rectangle held its patch of unfaded wallpaper the way it had held it since Dana was nine.

She did not look at it. She had decided in the car that she would not use it, would not point at it, that using it would be a lever and she had not driven fifty minutes to work a lever on a seventy-two-year-old woman.

“Mom,” she said. “Can I tell you something, and can you not get up.”

Lorraine’s hands went flat on the counter. She was at the counter, she was always at the counter, she had risen at some point in the downspout to do a thing to the coffee cake that the coffee cake did not need, and now both her palms were down on the laminate on either side of it, and Dana watched her steady it, and then steady herself against it, the whole architecture of the woman doing what it did.

“All right,” Lorraine said, to the coffee cake.

“The friend who died. Nic.” Dana kept her own hands around the cup. “We hadn’t spoken in six years. Not a fight. I stopped answering her, a little at a time, for years, until it was gone, and she was writing me letters the whole time that she never sent, and I found out about all of it five weeks after she was dead.” She made herself keep going through the place where her voice wanted to do something. “And I’ve been going around this spring finding out that it’s not a thing that happened once. It’s how I do it. I did it to Priya. I did it to Kim. I do it to Tom.” One beat. This was the sentence, the surviving sentence, one part, and it went out of her into her mother’s kitchen. “And I learned it here. In this room. And I’m not saying that to lay it at your door, I’m saying it because I’ve never once told you a true thing in this kitchen and I would like to know what happens if I do.”

The freezer hummed. Somewhere out on the road a truck changed gear on the hill.

Lorraine did not turn around. That was the part Dana would go over afterward, going south, at seventy, in the fast lane. Her mother stood at the counter with her hands flat on either side of a coffee cake and did not turn around, and the silence went on long enough that Dana understood it was not a pause, it was the answer arriving, it was a woman standing at a rail in the dark deciding whether the rail would hold two.

“Well,” Lorraine said.

“Mom.”

“That’s a lot to carry up here on a Friday.” She picked up the knife and cut two more pieces of the coffee cake nobody had asked for, and set them on the plate, and squared the plate to the edge of the counter. “You’ve always thought more than is good for a person. Even at nine. Your father used to say it, that one’s got the wheels going all the time.” Her voice was level and kind and it was moving, Dana could hear it moving, the way you hear a car pull out of a drive behind you. “You had a friend and she died and it’s knocked you sideways, and now you’re going back through everything with a flashlight looking for where you went wrong, and you’ll find something in every room, because that’s what a flashlight does at four in the morning. It doesn’t mean the house is bad.”

“That isn’t what I said.”

“I know what you said.” Lorraine turned then, finally, and her face had been put back on, entirely, the way a bed is made. “I heard you. I’m not deaf and I’m not stupid.” And then, gently, with love, closing it: “You’re tired. You’ve done a funeral and a eulogy, you’ve got that whole auction coming, and you drove up here on a workday, and I think you want me to tell you that you’re a bad daughter so you can be relieved about it, and I’m not going to, because you’re not, and I don’t do that.”

“I’m not asking you to say I’m a bad daughter.”

“Then I don’t know what you’re asking me for, sweetheart,” Lorraine said, and it was true, she did not know, and she said it standing in front of her own wall with the rectangle above her left shoulder and no idea it was there.

The bag was already being made. Dana watched it happen from the table, the containers coming out of the drawer they came out of, the coffee cake going in, half a chicken she had not been told about going in, the folded paper towel tucked into the top of the bag for reasons known only to God and Lorraine, and she sat in the blue-check chair with her one surviving sentence lying on the table between them where she had put it, unlifted, and understood that her mother was going to walk her to the door now.

“Ronnie’s coming Monday about the chair,” Lorraine said. “And I’ll tell you what I told him about the downspout.”

“Tell me on Sunday.”

“I’ll tell you Sunday.” She put the bag in Dana’s hands, and did the thing, took her daughter’s face in both dry hands for one second and looked at it, hard, the inspection with the love in it or the love with the inspection in it, and let go. “You eat that. Not in the car.”

“Not in the car.”

“And you’ll call me Sunday.”

“Sunday,” Dana said, and drove home fifty minutes with a bag on the passenger seat and the sentence still in her, whole, undelivered, exactly as heavy as it had been at nine in the morning, and she was not angry. That was the strange part. She got most of the way to Westerville before she found the actual feeling and it was not anger at all. It was that her mother had been standing at the counter with her hands flat on it, and Dana had watched her do it, and had known precisely what it was, because she had done it herself on her own couch in February with a phone in her hand and a husband twelve feet away, and had called it, at the time, being fine.

# Chapter 32

[S]{.dropcap}he took Iris with her, which she had never done on purpose before.

The tell, if anyone had been keeping the kind of ledger Dana was only now learning she’d been kept in. For years she had gone up to Mount Vernon alone, or with Tom as a buffer, or she had sent Iris up for a weekend and picked her back up, the two visits kept from overlapping the way you keep two chemicals from sharing a shelf. She had told herself it was logistics. It was not logistics. It was that she had never wanted her mother and her daughter in the same room with her, the three of them, the whole line laid out end to end where a person might accidentally see it. On the second Saturday in April, the morning after driving fifty minutes each way to say one sentence and failing to say it, she buckled Iris into the front seat with a bag of the pretzels her mother didn’t approve of and drove north again, on purpose, into the thing she’d spent a life keeping on separate shelves. She could not have told anyone what she thought a twelve-year-old was going to accomplish. She took her anyway.

“Why are we even going,” Iris said, not unhappy, a twelve-year-old’s honest audit of a Saturday. “You were literally just there.”

“Because I want to see her. And because you’re old enough now that she wants to see the actual you, not the school-picture you.”

“I’m the same in the school picture.”

“You’re never the same in the school picture. Nobody is. That’s the whole tragedy of school pictures.”

Lorraine had the storm door open before they were up the walk, and her whole face reorganized itself at the sight of Iris, softened in a way Dana had spent forty-five years watching happen to other people, the grandmother-face, the one that skipped clean over Dana and landed on Dana’s child with no toll to pay in between. Grandmothers and granddaughters got the easy road. It was the middle span that carried the weight, always, the daughter in the driver’s seat, and Dana understood, following the two of them into the warm ordered kitchen, that she had scheduled her whole life to never be in the car when the easy road and the weight-bearing one ran alongside each other in plain view.

There was pie, because there was always going to be pie, a woman did not get a night’s notice of her granddaughter and produce nothing. There was the interrogation Lorraine ran on Iris, which was love in the shape of an inventory, the teeth, the grades, the friend situation, whether she was reading anything that wasn’t on a screen, and Iris, God love her, answered all of it head-on, everything sent, nothing held back, a person who had not yet learned that you could ration yourself, that you could make yourself the easiest possible person to be a granddaughter to. She told Lorraine she’d cried about the orthodontist and thought braces were ugly and was getting them anyway. She told her about the fight with Marcy and the un-fight after. She narrated her whole small twelve-year-old heart across the blue-check table with her mouth full of pie, and Lorraine listened with her hands folded and her eyes bright and did not once tell her to steady the counter, and Dana sat between them and watched the language she’d been raised in fail to take, one generation down, and thought, that is the most hopeful thing I have seen in a year, my daughter is fluent in the thing we forgot.

Then Iris got up to use the bathroom down the hall, and stopped, dead in the middle of a thing, in front of the wall of photographs by the wall phone.

“Grandma, what was here?”

Dana felt it before she’d fully turned. Iris had her finger up, not touching, pointing, at the brighter rectangle high on the left, the clean patch of thirty-five-year-old wallpaper where the low April light was raking across it exactly as the March light had, the held-breath shape, the absence Dana had grown up under and never once, in forty-five years, asked about, because you did not ask, because the not-asking was the first language, the one under all the others.

“There’s a lighter part,” Iris said, into the silence that had walked into the kitchen and sat down. “Like a picture came off. It doesn’t match.”

And there was Dana’s whole childhood, standing in a doorway in a twelve-year-old’s honest voice, asking the one question the house had been built, brick by brick, to prevent. Dana opened her mouth to do the thing, the thing she’d done her whole life, the smooth redirect, oh that’s nothing, honey, come on, wash your hands, the pie’s going, she felt the front-desk voice come up ready and greased, and she looked at her mother.

Lorraine had gone flat-handed on the table. Both palms down on the blue check, steadying it, and then herself against it, the exact motion she’d made in March when Dana handed her the news, a woman bracing a table that did not need bracing, and Dana watched her mother look at the rectangle for what might have been the first time in years, get caught looking, an old woman ambushed in her own kitchen by the one wall she’d spent three and a half decades not seeing, and Dana did the new thing, the thing she had failed to do on the phone in March with fifty safe miles between them, the thing there was no receiver to hang up here in the room.

She said nothing. She let the silence run. She did not fix it, did not fill it, did not reach past the hard true thing for the small manageable one. She sat with her hands in her lap and let the quiet be as long as it needed to be, and it was long, it was very long, Iris standing in the doorway now sensing she’d stepped on something and not knowing what, and the freezer humming its inventory, and the April light on the rectangle, and Dana just let it be there, the absence, on the wall, in the room, unhandled, for the first time in the history of this house.

“That was your grandfather,” Lorraine said finally. To Iris. Her voice came out level and then, on the second word, not quite. “Your mother’s father. It was a picture of him from before, when the children were small.” She smoothed the tablecloth once with the flat of her hand, though it was already smooth, though it was always already smooth. “I took it down a long time ago, and I never,” she said, and stopped, and Dana watched her mother stand at the edge of the longest sentence of her life and go no further into it than the truth would carry her without breaking, “I never found the thing to put up instead.”

It hung in the kitchen, that sentence, the whole marriage and the whole thirty-five years and the entire architecture of the woman in nine words, I never found the thing to put up instead, and Dana understood she was being handed something across this table she had never been handed before, one inch, exactly one inch, her mother opening the door not a foot, not even a hand’s width, just enough for the light to change in the gap, and she understood that the right response, the only response that would not slam it, was to take the inch and not, God help her, not reach for the mile.

“I remember he had a green chair,” Dana said. Quietly. Not a question. Just a true small thing set down on the table next to her mother’s true small thing, one offering answering another. “By the window. It stayed a while after.”

“It did.” Lorraine looked at her, and something moved in her mother’s face, thirty-six years of table between them and something crossing it at last, not forgiveness, nothing so large, only recognition, only the acknowledgment that the two of them had been in the same house all along, under the same rectangle, not-looking at the same wall. “You’d have been nine. You remember the chair.”

“I remember the chair.”

“You were so good that year,” Lorraine said, and now her hands were not steady, now they gave it up, the steadiness, and lay still on the cloth. “I used to think, thank God for Dana, she doesn’t need a single thing from me, because I did not have a single thing that year, I was scraped right down to the pan, and you just, you tied your own shoes and you got your sister to school and you never once asked me for anything, and I let you. I let a nine-year-old carry it because I couldn’t.” She pressed her lips together. “I’ve been sorry about that for a long time and I never knew how to say it and I’m saying it in front of the baby, apparently, over pie.”

“I’m not a baby,” Iris said, small, from the doorway, and it broke it, the good way, the necessary way, and Lorraine gave a wet laugh that was mostly breath and said “no, you’re not, you’re a witness, God help you, go wash your hands,” and Iris went, sensing correctly that the storm had passed over into something else, and Dana sat across from her mother in the after-quiet with the one inch lying on the table between them, given and taken, and did not try to make it two.

Because that was the whole of it, she saw. Her mother would never say more than she’d just said. The rectangle would stay empty, probably, past both their lifetimes. It was one inch, hard-won, in a doorway, over pie, and it would have to be enough, and the largeness of it, the enormous unlikely largeness of one inch after thirty-five years, was exactly the size of a thing you almost never got, and she’d nearly missed it standing on the same spot her whole life not looking up.

She reached across the blue check and put her hand over her mother’s, which she also could not remember doing, not like this, not initiating it, and her mother let her, and turned her hand over under Dana’s so their palms met, two flat hands that had spent a lifetime steadying counters finding out they could also just hold on, and neither of them said anything, and this time the not-saying was not the old kind, the sealed kind, the kind that kept things down in the part of the house you didn’t go into. This time it was only two people who had run out of the small manageable things to say and were, for one long moment in a kitchen in Mount Vernon, brave enough to sit inside the large one with the light coming in low across the wall.

“Well,” Lorraine said at last, which was still, would always be, the whole of what she could carry out into the open at one time, and she squeezed Dana’s hand once and let it go and stood up, because the moment had gone on as long as a moment could in this house and the gap between tasks had closed, and she was already reaching for the pie to cut Iris a second piece she hadn’t asked for, already provisioning, already at the rail of her own kitchen, and Dana let her go, because you did not take a person’s armor off them at seventy-two. You only, if you were paying attention, got to be in the room and looking up on the one afternoon they set it down.

On the drive home Iris was quiet for a while, watching the fields go by, the corn not up yet, the light long.

“Was that sad back there,” she said. “With Grandma. It felt sad.”

“A little. The good kind.” Dana glanced at her, the whole open uncorroded face of her, everything still sent, nothing yet held back. “Ask me about it in the car whenever you want, okay? Any of it. You don’t have to wait till it’s a lighter square on a wall.”

“That’s a weird thing to say.”

“It is. Eat your pretzels. Don’t tell Grandma.”

And Iris ate the pretzels her grandmother didn’t approve of, and told her mother a long unrationed story about Marcy that Dana listened to every word of, all the way down 71 in the long spring light.

# Chapter 33

[G]{.dropcap}ary returned on a Tuesday, unannounced, and by that Friday the kitchen had counters.

There was no explanation and Dana didn’t request one. He arrived with a helper and a slab of quartz and the demeanor of a man resuming a sentence he’d paused mid-word, and the family gathered at the plywood’s edge that evening for the last time like mourners at a specific, complicated grave, and then the plywood was gone, and there was stone, cool and pale and faintly veined, and Iris laid her cheek flat against it in benediction, and Tom set a coffee cup down on it just to hear the sound, and Dana stood in her finished kitchen, four months late and entirely on time, and thought that she would probably always miss the plywood a little, which she elected to tell no one, on the grounds that some findings were just for the file.

The auction landed the next evening, and it ran, as all her productions ran, on rails she’d laid so well they looked like luck.

Denny of Galena arrived at three with two smokers on trailers and the calm of a man who had fed a church of six hundred, and by five the entire gym smelled like a promise being kept, and the caterer who had withdrawn in March was never once thought of again by anyone, which is the only revenge logistics allows. Mr. Reyes patrolled his refinished floor in a state of guarded ecstasy, the shoe policy printed, laminated, and posted at both doors under his own name, exactly as the long game had always intended, and pronounced the felt pads “professional grade” to Dana at six with the gravity of a knighthood. Carla arrived with Delaney and found Dana at the wine pull, and the two of them spent a quality four minutes on the scheduling dentist at the Kestrel group, in code, at speed, to their enormous mutual satisfaction, and made a plan for coffee that had a date on it, an actual date, written into both phones on the spot.

But she ran it differently, and she knew it, and Julie knew it, and by eight o’clock the whole committee knew it, because Dana Whitaker spent the gym’s golden hour not at the command table with the binder but out in the room, circulating, holding a plastic cup of donated wine, while Julie worked the mic and two committee moms she had personally trained ran check-out and the seating chart held the Brennans and the Wallaces at their negotiated distance without her standing guard. She had handed things over. Badly, in places, the delegation showing its welds, a raffle hiccup, a gift-basket mix-up that sent the Cozy Night In to a man who had bid on golf, but handed over all the same, and the gym thing sailed, and she stood in the middle of it belonging to it instead of holding it up, which turned out to be a different building entirely, made of the same gym.

Gerald went up at eight forty, lot thirty-one.

Julie presented him with full ceremony, the estate of somebody’s great-uncle, value sentimental, and the room laughed, and the bidding opened at ten dollars as a joke and then stopped being a joke, because Westerville contained multitudes, and a man with a den went to forty, and a woman with what could only be a grudge went to fifty-five, and then a paddle rose at the back, held at the confident angle of a bidder with secret financing, and it was Iris, on a chair, with Tom beside her radiating the studied innocence of a man laundering his daughter’s campaign, and the room turned, delighted, and the man with the den, reading the situation with honor, let sixty stand, and Gerald came home.

“He was already ours,” Iris explained in the car, the pheasant belted into the middle seat between her and the gift basket that had been re-homed at closing. “He’s been ours since February. We were just making it legal.” She had negotiated the financing at fifty-fifty with her father against future chores, a deal Dana suspected would be serviced approximately never, and she rode home in the dark with one hand resting on the pheasant’s wooden base, proprietary, content, a twelve-year-old who had spent her own money, half of it, in theory, on a forty-year-old dead bird because it had presided over her family’s strangest season and you did not let the witnesses scatter.

The Saturday after was the one the outline of an ordinary life is drawn for.

Spring had fully arrived, the maple out the window gone from gray business to green, the neighborhood loud with mowers, and Iris was in crisis by nine a.m., a genuine full-spectrum crisis over the goalkeeper situation, which had detonated overnight in the group chat into faction, betrayal, and a proposed walkout of the entire midfield. The texts came downstairs faster than Iris did. By the fourth one Dana was at the new counters with her coffee, and she felt the old reflex present itself, smooth and ready: triage it, right-size it, it’s seventh-grade soccer, deploy perspective, return to the day. The fifth text arrived. It said, in its entirety, MOM. this is not a drill. i need you.

Dana went up.

She sat on the end of Iris’s bed for one hour and eleven minutes while the whole baroque architecture of the crisis was erected, walked through, and defended against imaginary counterarguments, the goalkeeper’s perfidy, Marcy’s tragic centrism, the midfield’s fragile coalition, and she did not right-size any of it. She asked follow-up questions. She requested the org chart, twice. She took the faction’s side where the faction was right and said so, and said where it wasn’t, and was argued with, and held, and somewhere past the first hour Iris wound down like a weather system moving off, and lay back on her pillow, spent and restored, and looked at her mother sideways.

“You didn’t do the thing,” she said.

“What thing?”

“The thing where you make it small so it fits in the schedule.” Iris said it without heat, an observed fact from a lifetime of observation, and it went into Dana like a dart into cork, painless and permanent. “You used to always do the thing.”

“I’m retired,” Dana said. “New policy. Things get to be their actual size.”

Iris considered this policy from her pillow. “The goalkeeper thing is actually pretty small,” she admitted. “It just didn’t feel small at seven a.m.”

“Nothing feels small at seven a.m. That’s not a character flaw, that’s just seven a.m.”

“Are you going to put that on a pillow?”

“I’m going to have it tattooed,” Dana said, and got the laugh, the real one, and stood and stretched and surveyed her daughter’s room, the cleats by the door, the geometry homework in its avalanche state, the whole loud current account of a person twelve years into being furiously alive, and on the wall over the desk, thumbtacked at an angle among the team photos and the magazine pages, she saw it, and stopped.

It was a printout, phone-photo quality, of frame thirty-one. The shelf. The two girls at twenty-three. Iris had photographed the print off the dining room table, where it had been waiting for the framer, and made herself a copy and pinned it between a soccer schedule and a picture of a dog she was lobbying for.

“That’s you,” Iris said from the pillow, following her mother’s eyes, unembarrassed, twelve. “You and her. I like it. You look like someone I’d be friends with.” She rolled over and reclaimed her phone, the crisis dissolving into its aftermath of memes, and delivered the closing ruling of the morning to the ceiling, offhand, devastating, already thinking about lunch: “Marcy and I are going to be like that at your age. Except we’re not going to do the part where you stopped. We’re just going to do the good parts.”

“Do that,” Dana said, from the doorway, in a voice that held because it had somewhere to stand. “Do exactly that.”

And went down to the kitchen, where the light came across the new counters in long mild bars, and Tom was making the second pot, and the ordinary Saturday resumed its ordinary orbit, mowers and coffee and a pheasant presiding over the dining room in perpetuity, legally theirs now, glass eye on the whole bright morning.

# Chapter 34

[M]{.dropcap}rs. Ostrander was on the Tuesday schedule for a ten-forty cleaning, and Dana saw the name come up the night before when she squared the next day’s book, and sat with it a second at the front desk after everyone had gone, the fluorescents doing their end-of-day tick overhead.

Ellen Ostrander. Eight-week recheck. The little flag in the system that Dana herself had set in February, in the software’s blunt vocabulary, PT RECENTLY WIDOWED. HANDLE GENTLY. because the software had no field for the thing and the drop-down menu came no closer, and Dana had typed it back in February and coat-and-detoured the woman out to her car and stood at the window with her hand on the coat rack not examining why, and here the name was again, eight weeks later, come round, the whole town cycling through the chair whether the year had wrecked them or not.

She thought about the banana bread. It was still in her bag, technically, or the note was, the little note that had said for the parking advice in a widow’s careful hand, which Dana had read twice and kept six weeks like a permission slip she couldn’t cash because she didn’t know what it admitted her to. She thought she might know now.

Tuesday, ten-forty, the waiting room did its Tuesday thing, turned over, and Mrs. Ostrander came out of the back at eleven-fifteen with the too-bright face of a person who has just spent forty minutes being told to relax by a stranger with a scaler, and the hygienist, Kayla, gave Dana the look over the woman’s head, the little helpless hover, the same hover from February, this one’s sad and I don’t have the thing for it, you have the thing for it. And Dana had the thing for it. She had the coat and the detour loaded and ready, Schrock Road was torn up again by the Meijer, genuinely, she could do four minutes on the Meijer construction alone and send Ellen Ostrander back to her car upright and unbothered and never once make either of them stand in the open, and it would be kind, and it would be a lie of exactly the shape she’d spent a whole spring learning to see.

She came around the desk. She always came around the desk. But this time, instead of reaching for the coat, she pulled the second chair out from the little alcove by the water cooler, the one nobody sat in, and she said, “Mrs. Ostrander, do you have a minute? Sit with me a second,” and she watched the woman’s too-bright face do something complicated, because this was not the script, the script was the coat and the parking and the door, and Dana had just gone off it in a waiting room with three people flipping magazines eight feet away.

“Is something wrong with the,” Mrs. Ostrander said, the reflex, the bill, the insurance, and Dana said no, nothing, the cleaning’s fine, your teeth are going to outlive us all, and Mrs. Ostrander laughed, startled, and sat.

“How are you, actually,” Dana said. Quietly, under the room. “This is your first spring. I’ve been thinking about it since I saw your name on the book. My friend, a friend of mine, I lost someone this spring too, and I keep finding out that nobody asks the actual question, they ask it in the doorway with your coat already half on, so I’m asking it sitting down. How are you actually doing. You don’t have to have a good answer.”

Mrs. Ostrander looked at her hands, at the wedding ring she still wore, and was quiet for a moment, and then she said, in the flat plain register of a fact carried a long way:

“The worst part is the garage.” She turned the ring. “Everyone asks about the evenings, the empty side of the bed, all that, and that’s bad, but I was ready for that, you brace for that. Nobody warns you about the garage. His side is still his side. His car’s gone, the kids took it, but there’s the oil spot, and his snow shovel, and the little chest freezer he kept his terrible fishing bait in that I have not been able to make myself open since November, and every time I pull in I park on my side, dead center of my side, like he’s coming home and needs the room, and I have caught myself doing it in July, I mean I’ll catch myself doing it in July, I know I will, leaving half a garage for a man who is not going to need to park in it.” She looked up. “That’s the actual answer. It’s the garage. Nobody’s asked me the question sitting down, so nobody’s gotten the garage.”

“The garage,” Dana said, and did not fix it, did not tell her it would get easier, did not hand her a single manageable thing, just held it, the oil spot and the bait freezer and the half a garage kept in reserve for a dead man, held it the way Marta had held her thing, plainly, without ranking it, and the two of them sat in the water-cooler alcove of a dental practice on a Tuesday with it between them, and it was awkward, and it was real, and it took about ninety seconds that the schedule did not have.

Because Dr. Fanning, of course, had double-booked the eleven-thirty, two hygiene checks stacked into one chair like he did roughly monthly, an act of scheduling optimism that defied eleven years of evidence, and Kayla was doing the frantic eyebrows across the room, and the waiting room had a crown consult in it now checking her watch, and the whole small machine of the practice was backing up behind Dana, behind ninety seconds of a woman not being handed a coat.

And Dana let it back up. She sat the extra thirty seconds. She said, “thank you for telling me the real one,” and she meant it, and she got Mrs. Ostrander her actual coat then, at the end, not as a detour but just as a coat, a cold April having blown back in, and walked her to the door, and Mrs. Ostrander stopped with her hand on it and said, “you kept the note, didn’t you. The banana bread note. I don’t know why I know that. You’ve got the face of a person who kept it,” and Dana said, “it’s in my bag right now,” and Mrs. Ostrander nodded like that settled something old between them, and went out to her half a garage upright, but upright the other way this time, upright because she’d been allowed to sit down, not because she’d been kept from it.

Then Dana went and untangled Dr. Fanning from his double-booking, and apologized to the crown consult, and ran the front desk at a dead sprint for forty minutes to buy back the ninety seconds, and was, in the flat public sense, worse at her job that Tuesday than she had been in eleven years, later on everything, the schedule ragged behind her the rest of the afternoon, and she thought, sprinting, rebooking, soothing the backed-up room, that ninety seconds was the actual price and that nobody had put it in the drop-down menu, and that she was going to be late, from now on, in this specific ninety-second way, for the rest of her working life.

She took the banana bread note out of her bag at the end of the day and threw it away, standing at the front desk in the after-hours tick of the lights, which she could do now, having finally found out what it admitted her to.

# Chapter 35

[T]{.dropcap}he college thread came back to life eleven days after the memorial and Dana was not in it.

That was not accurate and she knew it was not accurate, and she kept knowing it, at the front desk, in the car, at the new counters, without it making any difference. She was in the thread. Amy had started it back up with a photograph of the four of them at somebody’s twenty-fifth, and Deb had posted her garden, and Rachel had answered Deb’s garden with four exclamation points and a bee emoji, and when Dana wrote something about the hostas Rachel had liked it. Thumbs up. Tap, done.

Dana looked at the little blue thumb for longer than a person should look at a thumb.

She had sent the letter the Sunday after. Not an email. Paper, because she had learned something about paper this spring, and it had taken four drafts and it had been, she was almost certain, honest: that Rachel had been right in the gallery, that Dana had used her, that the phone call in March had been the easiest lie of the whole month because it hadn’t even felt like one, and that Dana was not asking for anything back. She had reread that last line twice before sealing it, checking it for a hook, because a sentence that says I’m not asking for anything is very often a hook wearing a coat, and she had decided it wasn’t, and had mailed it, and had raised the little flag.

Rachel had texted four days later. thank you for the letter. that was kind of you to write. hope Iris’s teeth survive the pipeline!!

Which was warm. Which was, on any reading available to a stranger, perfectly warm.

She drove out to Powell on the last Sunday in April with a pot of something from the garden center, because she was not, as it turned out, cured of anything, and Rachel opened the door in gardening clothes and was delighted.

Delighted was the word. She was delighted about the plant, delighted about the drive, she wanted to know about the auction and she laughed in all the right places about Denny and the two smokers and the man who had bid on golf and received the Cozy Night In, she made coffee and put out a plate of the cookies from the good place on Sawmill, and for fifty minutes the two of them sat in Rachel’s sunroom and had a completely lovely time.

Dana knew exactly what was happening by minute nine.

She knew because she had run this. She had run it for six years on a woman in Chicago, at a distance, in gray bubbles, and she had run it for a lifetime on her mother across a table thirty-six years long, and she had run it in nine efficient minutes on her own sister from a Kroger lot, and here it was, run on her, at close range, in a sunroom, with cookies. The warmth was real. Nobody understood that about the mechanism until they were on the receiving end of it. The warmth is not a lie. Rachel was glad to see her. Rachel would be glad to see her at Christmas. Rachel would send the card and answer the thread and like the hostas until one of them died.

It simply had a door in it now, and the door was shut, and everything on Dana’s side of it was going to be lovely forever.

At minute fifty she put her cup down.

“I’m not going to do the thing where I keep coming at it,” she said. “I said what I had in the letter and I meant it and I’m not going to sit in your house and work you until you give me something.”

Rachel’s face went briefly, carefully neutral, and Dana recognized that too, she had seen it in her own dining room in March on a woman holding half a clementine, and thought, well. There it is. It really does look like that from the outside.

“You didn’t do anything wrong today,” Rachel said.

“I know.”

“I mean it, this was nice.” And she did mean it. Every word out of Rachel Beal’s mouth was true. “I’m just, honestly, Dana? I’m still a little bit at the memorial. I keep getting back there. Amy says give it till the summer.” She picked a leaf off the new plant that did not need picking off. “And I don’t want to be a project. You’ve had a whole spring of fixing things and I know it’s been hard, I do, and I would just rather not be item seven.”

That landed somewhere structural. Dana sat with it, and did not defend, and did not explain that there was no list, because there was, there had been a list on a legal pad in February with tabs, and Rachel had never seen it and had described it anyway.

“That’s fair,” she said.

“I hate that it’s fair,” said Rachel, and laughed, wetly, and then they talked about Deb’s hip for eleven minutes, because Rachel’s whole genius was absorption and she absorbed this too, and at the door she hugged Dana with her whole body the way she always had, and said “thank you for the plant, it’s gorgeous, I’m putting it by the shed,” and waved from the step until the car was off the drive.

Dana got onto 315 and drove south in the four o’clock light and did not, for the first eight miles, feel anything she could name.

Then she found it, at the Bethel Road exit, and it was not grief and not guilt, it was something narrower and colder. There was no move. That was the whole content of it. She could not fix this by being honest, because she had been honest, in writing, on paper, with the little flag up. She could not fix it by showing up, because she had shown up, with a plant. She could not fix it by waiting, because waiting was the mechanism. She had spent nine weeks learning that the doors worked and that all you had to do was walk through them, and here was the part nobody had thought to mention: sometimes the person on the other side has already got her hand on it.

She was going to have to leave a friendship where it was, in a sunroom in Powell, warm and open and closed, and answer the thread, and like the hostas, and let it be as long as it was actually going to be, which might be a year and might be the rest of their lives, and which was, going seventy in the middle lane, not a thing being done to her.

It was a thing Rachel was entitled to do. Nic had been entitled to it too, every one of two thousand days, and had never once used it, and had gone on writing.

# Chapter 36

[T]{.dropcap}he coffee was on a Wednesday, ten thirty, at the place off Cleveland that wasn’t the chain, and Dana got there four minutes early and did not, for once, use the four minutes to answer anything.

She left the phone in her bag. She sat at a table by the window with two waters she’d gotten for both of them without asking, caught herself doing it, the provisioning reflex, the plate for the road, and let the second water stand anyway, because some habits were just love with its sleeves rolled up and you didn’t have to kill all of them, you only had to know which was which.

Carla came in at ten thirty exactly, and clocked the two waters, and pointed at them.

“You did the thing,” Carla said, sitting. “You got the waters. I do the waters. My husband says it’s a disease.”

“It’s a diagnosed condition in my family,” Dana said. “We provision. My mother sent me home from a funeral with soup by weight.”

“Mine irons the sheets. For the guest bed. That no guest has slept in since the Clinton administration.” Carla shrugged out of her jacket, hygienist’s hands, short clean nails, the small competent movements of a woman who spent her days inside other people’s mouths being reassuring. “Okay. Before anything else. I have to tell you what the Kestrel menace did.”

“Again?”

“You know him.”

“Everybody knows him,” Dana said. “Go. What did he do.”

What the Kestrel menace had done required the better part of a cup to tell, and by the end of it the two of them were doing the thing they’d done on the phone in February, the code, the shorthand two people build fast when they recognize the same enemy, and Dana was laughing the real way, the one that came up out of the chest without asking her, and a man at the next table had folded his newspaper down to see who was having a better morning than he was.

It was the easiest hour she’d spent in months, which was the reason she had almost not come. She could do this indefinitely. She could be delightful company at a coffee once a season for eleven years and Carla would go home and tell the sheet-ironing husband she’d made a nice new friend, and it would be true, and it would be a porch light.

So she did the thing that wasn’t easy, in the middle of the easy hour, on purpose.

“Can I tell you something real,” Dana said, “that isn’t about the dentist.”

Carla put her cup down. Something in her face adjusted, not alarm, just attention, the good kind, a woman making room. “Yeah. Of course.”

“I lost a friend this spring. In February, actually, and I found out a week later. My oldest friend, from college, we’d,” and here the front-desk voice came up under her offering the smooth version, we lost touch, you know how it is, the version that closed the door gently and kept the coffee light, and Dana felt it come and let it go by. “We stopped talking, years ago. Not a fight. I just stopped answering, a little at a time, until it was gone, and then she died before I fixed it. And I’m telling you this at a first coffee, which is insane, and I know it’s insane, and I’m doing it on purpose, because I like you, and the version of me that doesn’t say the real thing until year nine is the version that’s already lost one of these. So. That’s who’s having coffee with you. Somebody who’s going to overshare on purpose and text you back too fast and put the next one in the calendar before we leave.”

The café did its café things. The espresso machine let off its long dragon’s hiss. Out the window a woman walked a dog that clearly ran the household.

“Okay,” Carla said, slowly, and then, because she was, Dana was learning, a person who met a real thing with a real thing, “then I’ll tell you mine, since we’re apparently doing this. I moved here for the husband’s job eleven years ago and I have exactly one friend in this town and she moved to Dublin in the fall, actual Dublin, Ohio would’ve been fine, and I have been so lonely this year I started talking to the patients like they were company, which, if you’ve ever tried to have a real conversation with a person who has a suction tube in their mouth, is a losing game. And I drove here this morning thinking, please let this woman be a real one, because I do not have the energy to make another nice acquaintance I see twice a year.” She turned her cup a quarter turn. “So you can overshare at me all you want. It’s why I came.”

They were both a little pink about it. It was the thing Dana had told Iris in a doorway a lifetime ago, all the important things are awkward, and it was awkward, gloriously awkward.

They stayed ninety minutes. The dentist got relitigated. Delaney and Iris got discussed, the party in March that had ended at ten and launched all of this, the honk that was still, apparently, a live grievance in both households. And somewhere in the second hour they stopped needing the dentist to hold the thing up, and just talked.

When Carla’s phone buzzed the third time, a patient, the practice, the day reclaiming her, Dana did the thing she had promised the empty apartment in Rogers Park she would do.

“Before you go,” she said, and got her own phone out, and opened the calendar, and looked at Carla until Carla, understanding, got hers out too. “Next one. Now. While we’re both looking at it. Not soon, not we-should-do-this-again, not a text in three weeks that neither of us sends. A date. In both phones.”

“You really do have a condition,” Carla said, delighted, scrolling.

“I really do. It’s new. I paid a lot for it.” Dana found a Wednesday, three weeks out, ten thirty, the same slot, because a slot that repeats is a slot that survives. “The thirteenth. Same place. Same time. I’ll get the waters.”

“I’ll get the waters, it’s my turn.”

“We’ll both get waters,” Dana said. “The table will drown. It’ll be a whole thing,” and Carla laughed, and typed it in, and Dana watched the date land in a second phone.

They walked out together into the April light, and Carla went one way to her car and the practice and the afternoon, and Dana stood on the sidewalk a second in the sun, and did not check her phone, and thought that it had cost her one sentence she hadn’t wanted to say, and that she had three weeks to find out what the next one cost.

She got in the car. She texted Carla before she pulled out, because she could, because the store was open, because it cost nothing and always had: this was the best hour of my week and the dentist is a menace and I already can’t wait for the 13th. The reply came before she’d backed out of the space, which she chose to believe was a coincidence of red lights and chose, also, not to examine too closely, because some things you just let be the gift they were.

the 13th. waters on me. bring the contractor stories, i’m not done being appalled.

Dana put the phone in the cupholder, face up, and drove home the ordinary way.

# Chapter 37

[T]{.dropcap}he framer on State Street had opinions about mats, and Dana let him have them, and the two prints came home on the last Tuesday of April in brown paper, and she hung them the same afternoon, alone in the house, with the level and the good hooks, because some jobs you don’t delegate.

Frame thirty-one went to the stairs, at the turn, where the landing window’s light fell in the afternoon, into the spot Tom had called and the wall had been holding open, it turned out, for sixteen years. The two girls at twenty-three took their place among the wedding pictures, the shelf within the frame within the wall, Nic’s last composition joining the gallery of her early ones, the whole stairwell now one long correspondence in light, opened in a parking lot wind sixteen years ago and answered, at the turn, at last. Dana climbed past it four times that first afternoon on invented errands.

Frame twenty-two went to the kitchen.

She hung it on the wall by the window, over the new counters, where the morning light would find it, the fire escape in its soup-gold bars, the empty geranium pots waiting for the season, an ordinary Chicago evening fixed at one two-hundred-fiftieth of a second by a woman paying attention, and she stood back and looked at it in situ and understood exactly what she had done, and did it anyway, on purpose, with the level: she had put Nic in the room where the coffee happened. Not boxed in cedar with the christening gown and the war letters, not enshrined at the turn of the stairs for passing reverence.
