Here, at eye level over the toaster, where she would be seen without ceremony nine times a morning, the way you see the people you live with.

Iris found frame thirty-one before dinner, at speed and mid-transit, and came to a full stop on the stairs, which had happened perhaps twice in her life. She stood at the turn for a long moment with her backpack still on one shoulder. Then she came down into the kitchen and said, “It’s better on the wall than on my printout,” in the tone of an appraiser confirming an attribution, and drifted to the window wall, and looked at the fire escape a while, and said, “So that’s what soup light is,” and Dana had to set down the knife. Tom’s inspection came later and quieter: he stopped at the turn on his way up with the laundry, and stood, and she watched from the hall as he adjusted the frame a single degree that it did not need, the way he touched every hung thing in this house once to make it his responsibility, and went on up without a word, and that was the permit issued, the load approved, the photograph entered into the structure of the building.

Then she sat down at the new counters and wrote to Justine.

The camera sat on the shelf by the window while she wrote, loaded since Sunday, when she and Iris had performed the ceremony together at these counters with Dominic’s handwritten instructions propped against the fruit bowl. It had taken them three tries, the film’s leader slipping the take-up spool twice while Iris provided color commentary in the register of a golf announcer, and on the third try the sprockets caught and the wind lever went taut with that small mechanical bite that meant the machine had accepted the assignment, and Iris had insisted on shooting frame zero, the throwaway, the lens-cap test, pointing the camera at Gerald across the two rooms and pronouncing him lot thirty-one, first citizen, subject one. The counter stood at 1 now. Thirty-six frames of an ordinary day, undecided, waiting in the dark, and Dana found she liked knowing it was there, a held breath of the good kind, the week’s sentence not yet begun.

She wrote the letter by hand, on the good paper she’d bought at the framer’s because it had been sitting by the register like a conspiracy, and it took her three drafts, and she kept all three, the first two folded into the recycling with something like tenderness, because drafts were just practicing, she had it on the best authority, and the third one she wrote clean, shoulders back.

She told her the counters were in, and that the fire escape hung over them, and where thirty-one had gone and why the turn of the stairs was the right wall for it. She told her about the auction and lot thirty-one and a twelve-year-old buying a pheasant with laundered money, because Justine’s month had a deficit of the ridiculous and the family owed her imports. She told her the camera had film in it, the condition honored, a fresh roll loaded at the counter with Iris supervising and Dominic’s brand recommendation followed to the letter, and that the assignment was underway, one ordinary day, actually developed, terms as published.

She asked about the Christmas cards. Six years of them, rubber-banded in the desk drawer, gone into a box marked KEEP on a Wednesday when neither of them could talk about it, and Dana had thought about that bundle more than once in the weeks since, the little archive of her own handwriting that Nic had kept and read aloud and graded like wine. Keep them, she wrote. I mean it as a request, not a courtesy. They were mail that got where it was going, which makes them yours now, the way the letters are mine. But every December from here on there will be a new one, to Evanston, with the same terrible exclamation points, and you are hereby warned that I’ve been told exactly how my punctuation gets read in your family, and I intend to give the performance material.

And she told her, in the letter’s only paragraph that took real nerve, the thing she’d been carrying since a kitchen alcove and a dish rack: that going first into the letters and the roll had been sold to her as a favor to Justine, and had turned out to be the thing that saved her own life’s furniture, and that she knew what it had cost Justine to hand a stranger her sister’s last unopened things, and that the debt ran the direction Justine thought least, and would be serviced indefinitely, in visits, in letters, in casserole intelligence for Aunt Lou, in whatever currency Evanston accepted.

Into the envelope, with the letter, she put a print of frame thirty.

The reflection. The shop window, the street, and small at the edge, gray-haired, half turned, the camera coming up: the only photograph of Nic on the last roll, incidental on purpose, the signature in the corner of the canvas. Dominic had made the print at her asking and mailed it down with a note in darkroom capitals, GOOD EYE. THAT’S THE ONE THE SISTER NEEDS, and it had waited on the counter a week like a small dignitary. It was the picture of the woman herself, going to the person who had loved her longest, from the person who had loved her, the record would now show, the whole time.

She addressed the envelope, and stamped it, and then she sat at the counter with it squared in front of her for a moment, because the moment asked for marking.

Somewhere in her phone, in a folder of one, nine words stood where she had left them in a pickup line in March, Nic. I read them all, and I owe you, stopped mid-sentence, unsent forever, a stone on a grave. She did not open them or delete them. They were correct where they were; some mail had no address and the honest thing was to stop pretending otherwise. But this, she thought, thumb smoothing the stamp’s corner, this had an address. A living woman in Evanston who checked her mail. Paper that would actually cross the actual distance and land in actual hands, and be opened at a counter, and answered or not answered, and either way exist out loud in the world between two people instead of perfectly, eternally, uselessly within one.

It had cost what it cost, and there was no one left to hand the tuition back to, so she spent it forward.

She walked it to the mailbox at the end of the drive in the late-April light, past the marigolds Tom had finally planted, and put it in, and raised the little flag, red against the green street, and went back inside to the room where the coffee happened, under the fire escape’s gold bars, and started dinner, and did not check the mailbox again.

# Chapter 38

[T]{.dropcap}he 10K went off on the first Saturday of May under exactly the sky the forecast had promised with three ironic fire emojis: rain, fine and steady, the kind that made the whole field of runners look like they’d agreed on a filter.

They started in the third corral among four thousand people doing collective damp origami with their garbage bags, and Priya, who had run this race four times and held opinions, delivered the briefing.

“No heroics. We finish together or we finish estranged.”

“I have no heroics. I have knees.”

“You say that and then somebody passes you in a costume.” Priya pulled her cap down. “It happened to a woman I know. She’s not the same.”

The gun went off somewhere far ahead of them and reached them as rumor, and then as motion, the corral compressing and releasing, and they crossed the mat at a shuffle and went out onto the closed streets in the rain with four thousand pairs of shoes hissing on the wet pavement all around them, a sound Dana had not been prepared for and would remember longer than the race, like weather in a forest.

Mile one was ambitious and mile two was honest. Strangers had come out onto their porches with cowbells and travel mugs, in bathrobes, in the rain, at eight in the morning, for people they had never met, and a small girl under an awning on Schrock held out her palm for high fives and got two hundred of them and did not appear to tire. At mile three a church had put out a folding table of orange slices and a hand-lettered sign reading GOD LOVES YOU AND SO DO WE (THE ORANGES ARE FREE), and Priya said, without breaking stride, “amateurs,” and Dana laughed and lost her breathing for fifty yards and had to be talked back into it.

At mile four a man stood alone at the curb in a poncho holding a piece of cardboard that said WORST PARADE EVER.

Priya went. It came out of her in one wet honk and then would not stop, and she had to walk, actually walk, hands on her head, rain running off her elbows, wheezing, while runners flowed around them both like water around a rock, and Dana stood in the middle of a closed street in Westerville at twenty past eight on a Saturday morning holding her friend’s shoulder while her friend came apart over a piece of cardboard.

“Hydration stop,” Priya said finally, straightening.

“That’s what we’re calling it.”

“That’s what it’s called. That’s the official name of it.” She wiped her face with the back of a soaked glove, which accomplished nothing. “Do not put this in the group chat.”

“It’s going in the group chat.”

They ran on. The rain kept up its steady unglamorous work, and the crowd thinned, and somewhere in the fifth mile the thing happened that Priya had promised would happen and Dana had not believed, which was that it got quiet. Not the street. The inside. The body took over the accounting and the mind stopped supervising, and Dana ran through a residential mile of wet dogwood and hand-lettered signs and thought about almost nothing at all, which she had not done in ten weeks.

And then her legs went. Not dramatically. They just stopped agreeing to the arrangement, somewhere past the fire station, the specific slow catastrophe she had last met on a stranger’s lawn two springs ago, the legs going wooden from the hip down, the sparkles arriving at the edges of things.

“There it is,” Priya said.

“I’m fine.”

“You’re gray. You’ve gone the color of the sky.” And Priya’s hand was already in her jacket, and it came out with a clementine in it, peeled, whole, by means that had never once in two springs been explained.

“How,” Dana said.

“Trade secret.”

“You ran five miles in the rain with a peeled orange in a zip pocket.”

“I ran five miles in the rain with a peeled orange in a zip pocket,” Priya agreed, “because I train with a pharmacist’s daughter, which is a thing I say to myself in the mirror,” and she broke it and handed over half, and neither of them said the other thing, which was a stranger’s lawn two springs back and every Tuesday since, all of it arriving in an open palm at a shuffle in the rain.

Dana ate it moving. It was cold and it was too sweet and the rain got into it and it was, without any exaggeration she could later stand behind, the best thing she had ever eaten. Her legs came back, grudgingly, on credit. Priya ate her half and put the peel in her pocket, because Priya did not litter even in extremis, and they went on, and neither of them said the word, and the word was in the road the whole way to the sixth mile, being carried by two women who had built it out of a stranger’s lawn and a bad night in March and had not, either of them, expected to need it out loud today.

They crossed the line together at a time neither of them would ever repeat aloud and both of them would remember to the second, and stood in the finishers’ chute in the rain with medals like coasters around their necks, soaked, ruined, laughing, alive.

“June, Mumbai,” Priya said, medal to medal. “October, we do this again but slower somehow.”

“Both motions carry.”

“Both motions carry,” Priya said, and put her arm around Dana’s shoulders in the rain, which she had never once done in two springs, and they walked back to the car past the church’s ruined oranges under a sky that had not lifted all morning and did not need to.

The days ran on into May the way days do when nothing is wrong, which Dana had learned to recognize as the rarest weather there was.

She told Kim about the rectangle on a Saturday, badly, halfway through a conversation about a job interview, because that was apparently how she was going to do these from now on, sideways and out of order and never at the top of the call.

Kim went quiet. In the background a dishwasher was being loaded and stopped being loaded.

“She said that,” Kim said. “Out loud. To you.”

“Over pie. In front of Iris.”

“In front of the baby.” A long breath going out. “I asked her once. I was twenty-two and full of wine and opinions and I said what happened to the picture, and she looked at me like I’d asked her the time in a language she didn’t have, and then she got up and got the pie.”

“She got the pie.”

“She always gets the pie.” Kim blew her nose. “I’m not mad. I want that on the record, I’m going to need about a week, but I’m not mad.” And then, because she was Kim, because she had never in her life let a thing sit heavy when she could get something underneath it: “Also she has never apologized to me for one item in forty-two years, and I have a list.”

“Of course you have a list.”

“It’s laminated,” Kim said. “I learned it from her,” and Dana laughed at her own counters, and neither of them said the other thing.

Justine’s answer came in the second week, and it came, gloriously, in kind: real mail, block capitals softened at the corners, four pages that read exactly like her, logistics shot through with the other thing. The frame-thirty print had been hung in Evanston, in the front hall, where she passed it eleven times a day, which she reported was “correct and terrible and correct.” Susan had completed the move to Marta’s and was thriving, an empire of one windowsill, and enclosed, wrapped in a damp paper towel inside a sandwich bag inside bubble wrap, engineering worthy of the family, was a cutting, four leaves and a bare root, with a sticky note in the ancestral block capitals: SUSAN II. HOLDS FORTS. WATER WEEKLY, TALK TO DAILY, SHE’S USED TO IT.

The cutting went into a jar on the kitchen windowsill, under the fire escape print, where it put out its first blind white root within the week, and Iris named it, over all objection, Susan Two: The Sequel, and gave it the morning updates, attendance projections, casserole intelligence, the goalkeeper situation. Beside it on the sill, out of its yogurt cup and into a proper pot, sat the spider plant from Rogers Park, throwing out its own blind runners, spoken to on occasion like an idiot, per instructions.

# Chapter 39

[T]{.dropcap}he drawer that had stuck since February stopped sticking on a Tuesday in the first week of May, and Tom stood in the kitchen with the plane in his hand looking at it with the specific suspicion of a man whose problem has solved itself.

“That’s it,” he said. “That’s the whole thing. Three months.”

“Congratulations.”

“I don’t trust it. It’ll swell again in August.” He slid it open and shut, open and shut, testing for treachery. “Everything in this house is waiting for August.”

It was a good evening. Iris was at Marcy’s, the counters had been in for three weeks and still stopped Dana every morning like a stranger in her own kitchen, and the light came in over the fire escape print and lay along the quartz, and dinner was the kind that cooked itself once you’d started it. Dana stood at the stove and thought about how many evenings this exact evening had already been, sixteen years of them, and how few of them she could actually account for.

She had been answering people for eight weeks now, since March, and she had begun, somewhere around the start of May, to be able to see the shape from outside it. Kim on Saturdays. Her mother on Sunday nights. Priya on Tuesdays and Thursdays and once, memorably, at six fifteen in the rain. Carla on the thirteenth, in ink, in both phones. Justine’s four pages. Ella’s texts, answered inside four minutes, every one.

And Tom, who lived here, and who she had not asked a single real question since the night at the dining room table in March.

She had noticed it on the loop that morning and carried it around all day like a stone in a coat pocket. She had built a whole system for reaching people at a distance and she had not once turned it on the man eleven feet away. It was easier at range, and that was not a comfortable thing to have found: a phone call to Mount Vernon cost her forty minutes and a Saturday and she could hang it up, and this could not be hung up, this one she had to live in afterward.

She turned the burner down.

“Can I ask you something,” she said, “and can you not do the thing where you answer it fast so I don’t have to sit in it.”

Tom set the plane on the counter. He did it carefully, and then he leaned back against the counter’s edge himself and folded his arms and waited, which was more than she’d asked for and exactly what she’d asked for.

“In March you said your count wasn’t any lower than hers. The letters. You said some nights you couldn’t tell me it was lower and then you stopped, and I let you stop, because I’d just talked for forty minutes and I didn’t have anything left.” She kept her hands on the towel. “I’ve thought about it every week since and I’ve never asked. So I’m asking. What’s in it. Give me one.”

The refrigerator cycled on.

“You want an item,” Tom said.

“I want an item.”

He looked at the drawer for a while, the one that had stopped sticking, and Dana made herself stand there and not fill it, which was the hardest forty seconds of the month and she had run a 10K in it.

“February of the year Iris started third grade,” he said. “You had a mammogram come back wrong.”

Dana’s hands went still on the towel.

“You went for the second one on a Thursday. I know it was a Thursday because I was on the Rowan job and I came home and you’d made that chicken with the lemons, and you were fine, and we watched something, and you told me about it eleven days later, in the car, on the way to your mother’s, and you told me like a story that was over. You said, funny thing, that mammogram thing turned out to be nothing.” He said it without any weight at all, a man reading a measurement off a tape. “And it was nothing. You were right. That’s not the part.”

“Tom.”

“You asked for one. Let me put it down.” He shifted, and folded his arms the other way. “The part is that for eleven days you were a woman who might have had something, and you were in this house, and I was in this house, and I was, I don’t know. Doing the Rowan job. Being pleased about the chicken.” He stopped. He picked the plane back up, looked at it, set it down again, which told her more than anything he’d said. “I’ve thought about those eleven days about once a year for four years. Not angry. I want to be clear about that, I’ve never once been angry about it. It’s more like there’s a room in the house I’ve never been in and I know which door it is and I go past it.”

The pan ticked. Out the window somebody’s mower started and thought better of it.

Everything in Dana stood up at once with a plan. The plan was fluent and it arrived complete and it had three parts, and the first part was I’m sorry, and the second part was an explanation of why, and the third part was a proposal for how it would be different, and she could feel the front-desk voice coming up under her, warm, ready to make this a thing that had been addressed.

She put the towel down on the counter.

“Okay,” she said. “That’s yours. I’m not going to do anything with it.”

Tom looked over.

“I’ve got about four things I want to say and every one of them is me taking it off you,” she said. “So I’m not going to. It’s yours, you’ve had it four years, and I’d like to just know it now.”

He didn’t answer for a moment, and when he did his voice had gone somewhere she’d heard maybe three times, at the hospital when Iris was born and twice since.

“That’s the first time in sixteen years you’ve done that.”

“I know.”

“I didn’t know it was available.”

“Neither did I,” Dana said. “I found out in a kitchen in Rogers Park in April from a woman with a watering can.”

And Tom laughed, one short surprised syllable, and rubbed his face with the flat of his hand, and the evening went on being an evening, because that was what they did, because a marriage was not a room you stayed in, it was a room you kept coming back to. He got the plates down. She finished the pan. Somewhere in the middle of it he said, to the plates, not to her, “There’s about four more,” and she said, “I know,” and he said, “Not tonight,” and she said, “No. Not tonight,” and that was the whole negotiation, conducted over dinnerware, terms accepted on both sides.

They ate at the new counters standing up, which they had started doing since the plywood went, some habit surviving its own reason. Tom relitigated August and the drawer. Dana told him what Ella’s last text had said, which was a photograph of a lecture hall with the caption coming friday. i have follow-up questions about the pig, and Tom said the pig had become a family industry and he expected residuals, and it was ordinary, all of it, and Dana ate her dinner in her finished kitchen next to her husband and understood that she had just been handed something and had not dropped it, and that this was going to have to be a thing she got better at slowly, badly, over years.

Later, going up, she stopped on the landing at frame thirty-one, the two girls at twenty-three squinting into their long-gone sun, and stood there a second in the dark.

Four more, he’d said.

She had spent a season learning that the door was open and nobody had locked it. It had not once occurred to her, in all of it, that there was a door on this side of the house too, and a man behind it who had been standing there for sixteen years with his hand up, not knocking, being no trouble.

She went up. She did not fix anything. It kept.

# Chapter 40

[E]{.dropcap}lla came the second weekend of May, alone, which Dana had not expected and Justine, it turned out, had quietly arranged, a nineteen-year-old sent three hundred and fifty miles down the interstate by a mother who had decided the girl was old enough to go collect a thing herself. She pulled into the driveway a little after four on Friday and sat in the car a moment before she got out, hands still on the wheel, and Dana watched from the front window and made herself stay there.

The discipline now, and it still showed its welds. Every trained cable in her wanted to be on the porch already, wanted the guest towels turned down and the weekend blocked into its hours with margin built in for the construction on 33, wanted to hand the visit a shape before it could arrive with one of its own. She stood at the glass with her hand flat on the sill and let the girl come up the walk at her own speed, duffel on one shoulder, and held against her chest with the other arm, carefully, the way you carry a thing you have driven a long way, a shoebox.

“That’s the same box everybody in this family owns,” Dana said, at the door, instead of hello.

“It’s my mom’s, technically. She has forty of them.” Ella held it out, both hands, an offering and a problem at once. “These are the ones nobody could name. Mom did the family ones and Bev did the work ones and there’s still, like, two hundred that are just people. Nic knew everybody. And I kept thinking, there’s one person who had the college end of it, so.” She stopped, and looked at Dana with her aunt’s exact chin lifted at its exact argumentative angle. “I’m not going to make it weird. It’s just, I hate that they’re going to a drawer with no names on them. That felt like the worst thing. Worse than a lot of the actual worse things, which I know is insane.”

“It’s not insane,” Dana said, and took the box, and did not say the rest of it, which was that she knew the exact shape of the thing the girl was describing, the specific unbearable weight of a name that no one left alive could supply, because she had been carrying a box of her own up and down the basement stairs since March. “Come in. There’s food. There’s a pheasant named Gerald, he’s part of the family now, we bought him at auction, it’s a whole thing.”

Iris came down the stairs at her usual velocity, which sounded like furniture falling, and stopped four steps up when she saw the black nail polish and the duffel and the chin, and did the frank appraisal of a twelve-year-old meeting a nineteen-year-old, the most consequential age gap the human species has ever devised, and Ella, to her enormous credit, met it head on.

“You’re Iris,” she said. “You have opinions about your own hair. I heard about the treaty.”

“Who told you about the treaty,” Iris said, already lost, already gone, in the way of a child handed the attention of an older girl she has decided on sight to worship.

“My aunt Nic,” Ella said, easy, no catch on the name, the different-kind-of-griever ease that Marta had, that the young sometimes had, “read your mom’s Christmas card out loud at the co-op one year and did a whole bit. The hair treaty of Westerville. She said you negotiated like you had counsel.”

And there it was, the first small collapse of the afternoon, a thing that had happened in this house, filed into a card, mailed north, read aloud in a darkroom to strangers, and come back around the long way in a stranger’s mouth, alive, and Iris’s whole face did something complicated and pleased, being seen from two states over by a woman she’d never met, and Dana carried the shoebox to the dining room table and set it down under Gerald’s glass supervision and did not say a word, because there was nothing to say that would not have made it smaller.

They did the prints after dinner, the three of them and then the four when Tom came in from the basement, spread across the table in the good lamp, and it was the work that carried it, the sorting and the squinting and the passing of a print into better light. Ella had a system, index cards and a pen, a nineteen-year-old’s touching faith in the archive, and she wrote on a card for each one Dana could name, in a fast slanted hand that Dana had to look away from the first time, because it was Nic’s hand, or the ancestor of it, the family slant coming down the generations like the chin.

And Dana named the old ones. She named the co-op darkroom before it was the co-op, when it was a leaking room over a hardware store and Nic had a key she wasn’t supposed to have. She named a diner in Athens, gone now, a phone store now. She named a man in three of the prints, young, laughing, and said his name, and Ella wrote it on a card, and it went in the pile. She was good at it, she was excellent at it, the vault opening on command, the bills crisp, and somewhere in the third stack she reached a print of Nic on a fire escape with a plant and a woman Dana didn’t know, both of them mid-laugh in soup-gold light, and she turned it over and there was nothing on the back, and she turned it face up again and looked at it a long moment, and put it down.

“I don’t know her,” she said.

Ella looked up, pen ready.

“I don’t know any of these,” Dana said, and gestured at the newest stack, the color ones, the digital-looking ones, the last decade of a life. “I want to be straight with you about it, because you drove a long way and you should get the real thing. I’ve got the first dozen years and then I’ve got a hole, and the hole is most of your aunt’s actual life, the part where she became the person all those people at the memorial knew. I can name a diner that’s a phone store. I can’t name the woman on her own fire escape.” She set the print in the middle of the table, between them, unnamed. “That’s the honest inventory. I’m the college end. I’m not the whole thing, whatever anybody told you in a receiving line.”

Ella capped the pen. She was quiet a second, and the freezer hummed, and Tom, across the table, went carefully still in the way of a man who had learned this spring when to hold his own weight and not reach for a task.

“Yeah,” Ella said finally. “I figured that out at the reception. You told two hundred people.” She turned the unnamed print with one finger, careful of the image the way her aunt would have been. “Nobody has my aunt at twenty except you. My mom has her as a little sister. Bev has her as a Tuesday. You have the pig and the alternator car and whatever a soup light is, she said it and I never knew what she meant.” She uncapped the pen again. “Tell me the pig. I want the whole thing, sources, the deleted scenes, everything. My aunt did a version but she did the aunt version. I want the primary document.”

So Dana told the pig.

She told it unabridged, which took a while, because the version she had given a gallery in April had been shaped for a room and this one was not shaped for anything. Ella took notes on the actual index cards, laughing the family laugh, the one that came up out of the chest, and stopping her twice to cross-examine, wait, so the TA thought you had a medical condition, that’s in the record, that’s a documented thing, and Iris, permitted to stay up on the grounds that a college tour of the family archive did not come around twice, sat with her chin on her fists and watched a nineteen-year-old with black nail polish collect the same stories Iris had been collecting since a dead bird arrived in February, and somewhere past eleven the two of them started finishing each other’s setups, and by midnight they had a private joke Dana couldn’t follow about a raccoon named after a committee, and Tom had gone up long since with a hand on Dana’s shoulder, and it ran.

It ran to one in the morning. Dana gave it the whole hour it wanted, the alternator car and the seatbelt looped under the arm and art doesn’t get pulled over, the dining-hall lady who thought Nic had transferred, the boxing announcements, Greg concluded, the Committee’s complete minutes, and she did not once check the time or right-size the evening into a shape that fit a schedule, because the schedule could go hang, the schedule had already cost her everything it was ever going to cost her, and she sat at her own table at one in the morning handing a girl the primary documents of a friendship, badly, out of order, laughing, and watched her daughter and her oldest friend’s niece build, across seven years and the width of a table, in real time, the exact kind of thing that could still be built right.

Saturday they had private jokes at breakfast. By Saturday afternoon Ella and Iris had disappeared into Iris’s room for two hours and come out having formed some youngest-people-in-the-house alliance that no adult was cleared for, and Dana heard them through the floor, the older girl’s lower laugh and the younger one’s helpless one, and did not go up. By Saturday night Ella had promised Iris a tour of Northwestern that no parent had authorized and both of them treated as settled law, and Tom, informed of the college tour over the pot roast, said, “I’ll want that in writing,” deadpan, contractor to the bone, and Iris said it was already in writing, Ella had written it on an index card, and produced the card, and it was, and Tom’s jurisdiction over the matter ended there.

And Sunday at breakfast Dana handed her the camera.

She had thought about it since Friday, out loud in her own head instead of in the sub-basement where the deciding used to happen without her. The camera lived on the shelf by the kitchen window, under the fire escape print, loaded, in working order, per the condition Justine had set and Ella had called in week one. It was the last of Nic, more than the letters even, and it was also, Dana had decided somewhere around Saturday’s second pot of coffee, not a shelf thing, had never been a shelf thing, had been handed to her precisely so it would not become one.

“Here,” she said, and took it down, and put it in the girl’s hands across the counter where the morning light was coming in over the toaster and finding the print. “There’s film in it. She’d want it working, not archived. Shoot the rest of the weekend. That’s the whole condition, it’s the only one, it came down from your mom and it’s yours now too.”

Ella took it in both hands, and Dana watched her thumb find the wind lever without being shown, watched her turn it and feel the small mechanical bite that meant the machine had accepted the assignment, watched the girl’s hands know a thing her mind had never been taught, the family slant, the family chin, and now this, the eye, or the beginning of the possibility of the eye, coming down the same channel as everything else, and Dana had to turn and be busy with the coffee for a second, because there are moments you do not narrate even to yourself.

Ella shot half the roll by noon. Iris upside down on the couch, hair fanned toward the floor. The pheasant. The spider plant on the sill and the jar beside it where Susan II was putting out its first blind white root, spoken to daily per the ancestral instructions. Tom touching a hung frame one degree it didn’t need. She shot the ordinary Sunday of a house a long way from the one she’d grown up visiting, and she did it without ceremony, the way her aunt had done it, the shutter tripping at the instant the performance failed and the people showed through.

At the door Sunday afternoon, the camera back on its shelf with the rest of the roll left in it for Dana, duffel on her shoulder, index cards boxed and rubber-banded, six decades of family material riding shotgun, Ella hugged Dana in the family style, brief and load-bearing, the entire embrace the tribe permitted in a doorway.

“Okay. Confirmed,” she said. “The stories were true and the extended versions are better. Same time in the fall. I want the study-abroad fight and whatever happened in the darkroom sophomore spring, Bev says you know, Bev says you’ll pretend you don’t.” She got in the car, and rolled the window down, and delivered her exit line to the whole porch in her aunt’s exact cadence, offhand, already thinking about the drive. “Answer your texts. I time people now. It’s a whole thing I do.”

And she pulled out of the driveway and went north, up 33 toward the lake and the city and the life that had the newest fifteen years of Nic in it, carrying the oldest dozen out of Ohio on index cards in a shoebox, and Dana stood on the porch next to her daughter, who was already texting someone, thumbs going, everything sent, nothing held back, and understood the thing she had gotten backward for two months.

The inheritance had not passed to her. It had passed through her. She was not the archive. She was the door the stories had walked through on their way to the ones who would keep them out loud, a nineteen-year-old with a shoebox and a twelve-year-old with a phone, and she stood on her porch in the May light watching a hatchback take the college end of Nicola June Adair north where it belonged, and found that being a door, the thing the stories passed through and did not stay in, was not the smaller thing she’d always been afraid it was.

It was the whole job.

# Chapter 41

[H]{.dropcap}e gave her the second one on a Sunday at the end of May, in the garage, with the door up and the mowers going all down the street.

She had gone out to find the good pruners and had found him instead, sitting on the tailgate of the truck with a coffee going cold beside him and a length of scaffold plank across his knees, running his thumb along the grain of it the way another man would work a rosary. The plank had been in the garage for eleven years. She had moved it four times to get at the Christmas boxes and had never once wondered why a man kept one board.

“That’s been in here forever,” she said.

“It has.”

“Is it special or is it a board.”

Tom looked at it. Then he set it down flat on the tailgate beside him and put both hands on his knees, and Dana felt the afternoon change shape.

“You want to sit down,” he said. “It’s one of the four.”

She sat on an upturned bucket in her own garage in her gardening clothes with a pair of pruners in her hand and did not put them down, because putting them down would have been a ceremony, and she had learned that spring that ceremony was one more way of taking a thing off a person before they’d finished handing it over.

“Bill Prather,” Tom said. “You met him twice. The one with the beard, at the thing at Neil’s.”

“I remember the beard.”

“He fell eleven feet off a plank on the Cardinal job in oh-fifteen. Broke his pelvis in two places and his left wrist and he did not work again, not on a site, he does estimating for a supply house in Marion now and he is fine, he’s got the grandkids, we send a card.” He said all of it in the flat register a man uses to read a measurement off a tape. “And the plank he was standing on was one I put there. Not a bad plank. A short plank, and I set it and I meant to go back and cross-brace it and then the inspector came and then it was four o’clock, and I went home. It’s the only real thing I’ve ever done wrong at work and it cost another man his back.”

The mower two doors down finished a strip and turned.

“OSHA came out and it went the way those go, and the insurance went the way those go, and everybody said what everybody says. It was the site. It was the schedule. It was Bill’s own boots. Bill himself said it to me, in the hospital, with his pelvis in a frame, he said Tommy don’t.” Tom picked the plank up again and turned it over. “So there’s nobody who thinks it was me. There’s a whole county of people who don’t think it was me. That’s the thing I’ve been carrying, if you want to know what the shape of it is. It’s not the guilt. It’s that everybody got out ahead of me and took it off me and I never got to put it down myself. There was no place to set it. So I brought the board home.”

Dana’s whole body had gone into motion behind the sternum. The plan was there, complete and useless, three parts and a proposal, and the first part was *that wasn’t your fault*, and she heard it coming up her throat with its warm hands out, ready to be the whole county.

She got most of the way there, and would give him that account of it later, badly.

“Tom, you can’t,” she said. “Nobody cross-braces every, you had an inspector on you and a schedule and it’s oh-fifteen and I remember that year, you were doing three jobs.”

He nodded. He took it the way you take a coat.

“Yeah,” he said, and put the plank down, and reached for the cold coffee.

And Dana sat on her bucket in the garage with the pruners in her hand and watched a man re-arm himself in front of her at four in the afternoon, watched him do the thing she had watched her mother do at a counter in Mount Vernon in April with both palms flat, and heard, in stereo, exactly what she had just done: arrived at the end of eleven years of a man’s private carrying and taken it off him in nine seconds because she could not stand to see him hold it.

“No,” she said. “Wait.”

“It’s all right.”

“It is not all right, I just did it. Give me a second.” She put the pruners down on the concrete. “I did the thing. You handed me the one you’ve had for eleven years and I told you it wasn’t yours, which is the exact thing you just said everybody in the county did. That was your complaint, and I did it in your face inside a minute.”

Tom looked at the garage floor for a while.

“You did, a bit,” he said.

“Say the rest of it, then. If there’s rest.”

“There isn’t much rest.” He turned the cup a quarter turn on the tailgate. “I’d just like it to be a thing I did. Not a thing the schedule did. I set a short plank and I went home at four, and a man doesn’t walk right, and I would like to be allowed to have that be mine. That’s all of it. It’s not a big ask and I’ve never been able to make it come out small enough to say.”

“Okay,” Dana said.

“Okay?”

“Okay. It’s yours.” Her voice did not do anything, which she noticed and let alone. “You set a short plank and you went home at four.”

He breathed out, once, hard, through his nose, a sound she had heard from him maybe three times in sixteen years, and neither of them said anything for a while, and the mowers went on all down the street, and someone’s dog registered an objection to a delivery.

“That’s a horrible sentence to say to somebody,” he said at last.

“It’s the one you asked for.”

“I know. It’s still horrible.” He almost smiled. “Do it again sometime.”

Iris appeared at the garage door with her phone held out and a grievance already fully drafted, something about a group chat and a girl named Tess and the word *screenshot* used four times in the first sentence, and she stopped, and looked at the two of them, her father on a tailgate and her mother on a bucket in the middle of an empty garage with a board between them, and did the swift merciless assessment of a person who has learned to read a room in the last three months.

“Is this a thing,” Iris said. “I can come back.”

“It’s a thing,” Tom said. “You can stay for it. It’s over.”

So she stayed for it, and delivered the grievance at full length, and got a ruling from each parent separately, and appealed both, and Dana went and found the pruners and did not, that evening, put the plank away anywhere, and neither did Tom, and it stayed on the tailgate through the week and was still there on Saturday, out in the open, in a garage with the door up, where anybody could see it.

# Chapter 42

[T]{.dropcap}he ginkgo out front had come all the way in.

Dana stood under it a second with the car door open and her keys still in her hand, because the last two times she had parked on this street the tree had been mostly wire, holding March like a grudge, and now it was a green room with a sidewalk under it, and the difference was so total that she had to stand there and let it be a fact.

“It’s a tree,” Iris said, unfolding from the back seat.

“It’s a completely different tree.”

“It’s the same tree.”

“That,” Dana said, “is my entire point,” and got the cooler out of the back, because Aunt Lou had learned there was a car coming up from Ohio and had routed something through Evanston with instructions attached, and the instructions were that it went to Marta’s and not to the gallery, since the gallery was for the room and this was for the family, and the family, it had been established in April, was a three-story building on a side street in Rogers Park.

Tom took the cooler off her without being asked and looked up at the front of the building, top down, gutters first.

“Somebody repointed that,” he said.

“Please don’t do this all weekend.”

“I’m going to do it a normal amount.”

They came up Friday, the three of them, in the middle of the afternoon, for no reason except that Bev had said come in June and June had arrived. That was the errand. It had taken Dana most of the spring to understand that this was allowed, that you could get in a car and drive three hundred and fifty miles toward people who were not dying and not being buried, and she had booked the hotel in May and had told nobody how long she sat with her finger over the confirm button.

Priya had called at four in the morning on Tuesday, which was half past one in the afternoon in Mumbai, and Dana had answered on the second ring from the hall so as not to wake Tom, and had stood barefoot on the landing in front of frame thirty-one for thirty-five minutes while her friend talked.

It had not gone well and Dana was glad about that in a way she could not have explained to anybody. Priya’s mother had come home from the hospital in April and had been fine, was fine, was in fact holding court, and the wrist was healing crooked and would stay crooked because she would not do the exercises, and Priya had spent nine days in the flat she grew up in watching a woman of seventy-nine decline every single thing anyone tried to hand her, and had come to the end of a Tuesday and told her mother she was frightened, out loud, in the kitchen, and her mother had said that there was rice.

“That’s it,” Priya said, down nine thousand miles of line. “There was rice. She said it kindly. She got up and got the rice.”

And Dana, who had a plan, several, a good one about the exercises and a better one about the crooked wrist and a whole architecture about how you get a stubborn parent to a physiotherapist, stood in her own dark hallway and heard herself begin to deliver it, and got four words in.

“Sorry,” she said. “No. I’m doing it. Say the rest.”

“There isn’t a rest.”

“Then let’s have no rest.”

So they had no rest, for a while, at four in the morning and half past one in the afternoon, and it was awkward and expensive and neither of them was any good at it, and when Priya finally hung up she said, “Thursday when I’m back, we’re running, I don’t care about the jet lag,” and Dana said Thursday, and stood on the landing a minute longer in the dark, in front of two girls at twenty-three.

Marta’s door was propped with a boot. Inside, the apartment had gone half empty in the way of a room whose population has been mobilized: the card table by the good window bare, the sills bare, rings on the wood where a spring’s worth of pots had stood, and the low brown dog asleep in the exact patch of sun the plants had vacated, taking full possession, asking nothing.

“They went down this morning,” Marta said, from the kitchen, not turning. “Theo and two boys carried them. I supervised from the landing, which is a job.”

“It’s the hardest job.”

“It’s the only one with a view.” Marta came out drying her hands, and looked at Iris for a good four seconds, frankly, over the tops of her glasses. “So you’re the one with the treaty.”

“The what,” said Iris.

“There’s coffee,” Marta said. “It’s the bad kind. I’m still not apologizing.”

Nic’s apartment had a new tenant, a young man who taught eighth-grade science and had lived there since the first of May, and the co-op had knocked on his door in the second week of that month and asked whether a dead woman’s plants could spend a Saturday on his fire escape, and he had said yes, and then he had said yes to a photograph show going up on the landing outside his own front door, and then he had asked if he could help. Bev reported all of this at the bottom of the stairs in the tone of a woman reading out a favorable verdict.

“His name is Wes. He’s twenty-six. He offered us his extension cord.” She said extension cord the way other people say inheritance. “There’s no explaining some people. You give up trying.”

The fire escape had eleven pots on it by four o’clock.

Theo had built the mounts, and the mounts were beautiful and did not fit, which Theo had known since the first one and had continued building anyway, because the escape’s rail was not square anywhere along its length and had not been square since the Coolidge administration, and the correction, arrived at by committee in the alley below with everyone shouting up, was a bundle of cedar shims that Raymond produced from the van without a word, having brought them, having known.

“You knew,” Bev said.

“I’ve seen that rail,” said Raymond.

The geraniums went in last, in Nic’s own pots, the ones from the photograph, the terra cotta with the white crust down one side, and Marta did that part herself from the window because she had opinions about depth, and when they were in, somebody down in the alley whistled, and the whole crooked line of them sat there against the brick doing the one thing they were for.

Dana watched it from the alley with her hand shading her eyes, next to her daughter, who had a print of this exact view hanging over the toaster at home and had been looking back and forth between the wall and the world for ten minutes like a person checking a signature.

“It’s smaller,” Iris said.

“Everything is.”

“No, but it’s better smaller.” She squinted. “In the picture it’s the whole thing. Here it’s just, it’s on a building.” She thought about it. “That’s more impressive, actually. She made a building into the whole thing.”

Behind them Bev, who had heard every word and would repeat it for years, said nothing at all, which was, in Bev, an ovation.

The show opened at six and drew about ninety people, which Bev announced as a hundred and twenty and Justine, arriving at six fifteen with Ella and a folding table, immediately audited down to ninety-four and then, on principle, counted again.

“She’d count the house twice,” Ella said, to nobody, to everybody, an incantation the family now used the way other families used grace.

Dana worked, which was the discovery of the evening and took her until nearly eight to notice. She carried chairs. She found the light switch nobody could find. She ran the drinks table for forty minutes because it needed running and her hands were her hands, and at some point she looked up and understood that not one person in the building had asked her to do any of it, that she had simply joined a job already staffed, and that when she left the drinks table to go look at a photograph, the drinks table continued. Nobody’s evening was resting on her. It was, she thought, standing in a stairwell with a plastic cup, an entirely different building made out of the same gym.

She had brought the camera and she was bad with it.

The other discovery of the evening, and less comfortable. She had shot four frames in an hour and rushed three of them, and the fourth had been a woman laughing on the landing with the geraniums behind her, and Dana had gotten the focus somewhere in the brick.

“You’re winding it like you’re angry at it,” Bev said, appearing.

“I’m hurrying.”

“Why.”

Dana opened her mouth to say something capable and did not have anything, and stood there in a stairwell full of people who had spent their whole lives being good at this in front of each other, and told the truth.

“Because I don’t want anybody to watch me be bad at it.”

Bev looked at her over the red beads for a moment. Then she took the camera out of Dana’s hands, checked the top plate, and gave it straight back.

“Everybody here has been bad at it in front of me,” she said. “Some of them are still bad at it in front of me. Raymond has been bad at it in front of me since 1979 and he’s the best printer in three states. That’s the co-op, honey. You come in on a Tuesday and you’re bad at it in front of people and then, eventually, on a Tuesday, you’re not.” She patted the camera in Dana’s hands, twice, like sending a child in. “Third enlarger from the door. It’s still nobody’s.”

Later, when the crowd had gone downstairs to the alley where somebody had produced a cooler that was not Aunt Lou’s, Dana came back up to the landing and found Marta sitting on the top step with her shoes off, alone with the geraniums and the last of the light.

The light was doing the thing. It came down the alley at that hour in long soup gold bars and got in under the rail and laid the shadow of the ironwork across the brick in stripes, and it was frame twenty-two, exactly, the picture over Dana’s toaster, running live at one to one, and it would run for another eleven minutes and then it would stop, and it had been doing this on this wall every clear evening of June for a hundred years with nobody watching, and one woman had gone out on a fire escape with a camera and noticed.

Dana sat down on the step below her.

“I envied her,” she said.

Marta did not turn her head. Down in the alley somebody laughed at something and somebody else defended themselves.

“I told my husband that in March and I haven’t said it since, and I’m saying it here because you’re the one who watered her plants for nine years and you’ll tell me if I’ve got it wrong.” Dana looked at the geraniums, the white crust on the pots, the crooked line of them. “This. All of it. The escape and the pots and getting to be a person who noticed the light. I have a life I’d choose again, I want that said, I’d choose it in the dark with my eyes shut. And I spent about fifteen years unable to breathe around hers, and I never said one word about it to her, and instead I just quietly stopped answering, and I’ve been carrying it since the day I found out like it’s the whole explanation.”

Marta was quiet long enough that the bars of light moved perceptibly up the brick.

“She sat at my table,” Marta said. “Tuesdays, mostly, after the darkroom, because she’d come home wired and I’m up anyway. Nine years of that.” She reached over and turned one of the pots a quarter turn, an old woman’s fussing, adjusting a thing that did not need it. “And what she talked about, if you want to know, was your house. For years. Your card would come every December and she’d bring it across the hall and read me the whole thing standing up.” She looked down the crooked line of them. “This last one was the kitchen with no counters in it. The plywood, the contractor, the microwave on the dining room table. She thought it was the funniest thing that had ever happened to anybody and she did the voices, and then she put it down and said, that’s a house with three people standing in it, Marta, and every single one of them knows where the other two are.”

Dana did not move.

“She’d say it like a joke. She said most things like a joke, that was her whole arrangement.” Marta set her hands back in her lap. “I’ve got no business telling you what she felt, I’m not going to sit here and issue you a verdict. I’ll tell you what I heard through a wall for nine years, which is that whatever you had, she’d been driving past it slow for a long time with the window down. So if you’re asking me did you steal something. No. You had a thing she wanted and she had a thing you wanted and neither of you could figure out how to say so, and you both had a phone.” She shrugged the small settling shrug, the cardigan. “That’s just people.”

The light went up the brick another inch. Somewhere below, Iris’s voice, mid-argument, and Ella’s under it, losing on purpose.

“I don’t get to fix it,” Dana said.

“No.”

“That’s it? No?”

“What do you want, it wasn’t your fault?” Marta said, and Dana laughed, one hard syllable out of the chest, and Marta’s face creased and stayed creased. “I told you in April I wasn’t going to hand you that one, and I’m not starting in June.”

They sat. The stripes climbed off the top of the pots and onto the wall above and thinned out and were gone, and the alley went to that flat blue that comes after, and eleven pots stood along a rail in a city three hundred and fifty miles from Dana’s own kitchen, watered by a woman who had never been a plant person and was one now.

“July,” Marta said, standing, holding the rail. “The Fourth’s a nothing weekend here, everybody leaves, the parking is unbelievable. Bring the girl. I’ll make the bad coffee.”

“July,” Dana said, and got her phone out, and put it in, then and there, on the step, in ink, while the woman was standing in front of her.

Marta watched her do it and did not say anything about it, and went in through the window because at seventy that was still, apparently, the correct way to leave a fire escape, and left Dana alone on the landing with the camera in her lap and the pots going dark.

She raised it. She got the wind lever wrong and did it again, slower. Then she put her eye to it and framed the crooked line of geraniums against the brick with no light left on them worth having and pressed the shutter anyway, and it would be a bad photograph, and she would look at it in three weeks in a drugstore envelope in an Ohio parking lot and it would be black and gray and nothing, and she took it, because the alternative was standing on a fire escape in June waiting to be good enough to begin.

# Chapter 43

[T]{.dropcap}he group chats got answered now. The small mechanical fact underneath the season, invisible to everyone and structural to her: the committee thread, the team-parents chat, Carla, Justine, Ella, who texted at the frequency of nineteen and received her four-minute answers, every one, an inheritance administered to the letter. And the college thread, where Amy posted reports on the new hip and Deb posted her garden and Rachel posted four exclamation points at everything, including, twice a month, at Dana, warmly, from the far side of a door that was going to stay where Rachel had put it for as long as Rachel wanted it there.

And the ones that were never chats at all. Kim every Saturday, badly, the two of them clumsy at it and getting less clumsy, a sister called for no reason now, which had stopped, somewhere around the fourth time, frightening either of them. Her mother on Sunday nights, an actual voice down the wall phone and not a text, the two of them still mostly discussing gutters and weather and the freezer’s inventory, and the rectangle still on the wall with nothing in it.

Dana answered the day things arrived. It had started as discipline, a policy, a retirement. Somewhere in the spring it had become just what her thumbs did, and the day she noticed she no longer noticed was the day she understood the year had actually turned.

The camera lived on the shelf by the kitchen window, under the fire escape print, in working order, per the condition. She carried it sometimes. She was slow with it and bad at it in the way of any beginner at anything, and the first roll came back from the drugstore lab in May, Ella’s clean half and then hers, which was full of thumbs and blur and two frames of her own knee, and also one, just one, of Iris at the new counters mid-argument with her father about cereal, hand raised in oration, morning light on the quartz, that made Dana’s breath stop in the parking lot, because it had the thing, or the beginning of the thing, the shutter tripped at the instant the performance never started because there had never been a performance, just her family, seen. She reordered that one in an eight-by-ten. The wall by the window had room. The wall by the window was going to be a long correspondence, she could tell already.

And on an ordinary Thursday in the middle of June, at four in the afternoon, it happened, which is to say over nothing.

She was at the counters slicing peppers, radio low, Iris upstairs, Tom due at six, dinner an hour out, and the radio played the tail end of a song from another century, and the DJ back-announced it, and got the lyric wrong, confidently, flagrantly wrong, wronger than the wrongest version anyone had ever committed to, and Dana laughed out loud alone in her kitchen and turned, knife in hand, to say it, the whole reflex arc already fired, the sentence already halfway to the door, did you HEAR that, he’s worse than you, and the kitchen was, of course, only the kitchen. The fire escape held its gold bars. The steam went up off the pot.

And she let it come.

That was all. That was the whole revolution, conducted at a cutting board in four o’clock light: she did not outrun it, did not file it, did not stand at the counter and manage the moment into a smaller shape that fit the schedule. She put the knife down and stood in her kitchen and missed her friend, on purpose, at its actual size, the laugh and the ache arriving together, one system now, indivisible, and she looked at the fire escape in its frame and said, out loud, to the room where the coffee happened, in the tone you use for the people you live with, “He’s worse than you. He’s so much worse,” and the missing crested and washed through and drew back, the way it would, at its own tide schedule, for the rest of her life, leaving the ordinary afternoon standing there shining and intact, the way a street looks after rain.

Then she picked the knife back up, because the peppers weren’t going to slice themselves, and Nic would have had something to say about a woman standing sentimental in a kitchen while the water boiled over, and Dana could almost hear the shape of it, not the words, the shape, the eyebrow of it, and she was smiling at the cutting board when Iris thundered down into the kitchen at her usual velocity, furniture falling, phone extended, demanding a ruling on something that could not wait, that was enormous, that was, by every honest measure, nothing at all.

Dana wiped her hands, and took the phone, and gave the nothing her whole attention, at its actual size, in the room where the coffee happened, under the fire escape, in the light.

She answered.

# The End {.unnumbered .unlisted .the-end}
